The host filters only when the fleet config carries `host` (`start --host <name>`); without it there is no filter.
- **Concurrency (`fleetmux/model_usage.py`)**: the unit counted is the **model** (profile cards by `model_card`, unlinked ones by their own id; `claude-coder` + `claude-opus` both count as `opus`); **a slot counts once**, regardless of generation (a rotation / a card change in the same slot is not counted twice); a slot runs the card frozen in this generation's `runtime_launch` (written before the successor spawns, so later checks in the same tick already see a switch decided earlier), otherwise the fleet default, matched to a model card only by exact (cli, model string); no match means not counted, no guessing; **the old generation holds its ticket until its tab is really closed**: during a live switch the slot occupies both the old model (`retired_pane` + `retired_launch`) and the new one, and the old ticket is released only once the old tab is confirmed gone, never early; **a new generation that fails to start rolls back its reservation**: when the switch fails and the successor tab is closed, `runtime_launch` is written back to the old value and the failed new generation holds no ticket; if the successor tab cannot be closed, it keeps holding the new model; a dead slot holds nothing unless its tab is still alive (`Fleet.model_usage` asks cmux about the tab; if the query fails it counts as alive, so no early release); a retiring slot holds until it has really retired; the supervisor is not counted. Real dispatch paths: (1) submit planning uses `Usage.for_planning(active)` as occupancy (if a model already runs on a reusable live slot, that slot no longer counts as occupied), and `assign_profiles(..., running=)` lets a new profile land first on an idle slot already running the same model; (2) a live switch at delivery first passes `Fleet.card_gate`: host mismatch -> the task blocks (no silent swap); model full -> the message stays in the queue, `profile_state=pending`, audit `profile.capacity_wait` (logged once while the reason is unchanged), retried every tick, switched as soon as a ticket frees; (3) **the default runtime is bound by its card too** (`model_usage.default_problem`): a `start` without profiles (before provisioning, SystemExit), new rows from a roster hot reload (whole roster refused, audit `roster.rejected`) and sub-fleet delegation (refused by `subfleet.check`) all count tickets + host against the default model's card; switching back to the default uses `Fleet.card_gate(slot, None, registry)`; a default that matches no model card (including a Codex profile name) = no limit, no guessing. On resume, or with an old config already over its tickets, only a `capacity.over` warning is logged (what is already running is not killed); (4) roster / night switches use `model_usage.check_slots` (the whole roster counted by model + host; default slots also count against the default model's own card). `max_concurrent` is the ticket count of this fleet, not across fleets.
- **Unverified fields**: a card's `unverified` lists fields that lack external facts (`context_window` of Sol / Astra left empty; Fable's `model`; Haiku's `judgement`; FlashNext's `hosts`, `roles_allowed`); profile cards inherit it.
- **`quota_pool`**: names only the provider's quota family (`claude` / `codex` / `free-studio`). The actual accounts go in the `accounts` block of the deployer's private config file (`$FLEETMUX_CONFIG` or `~/.fleetmux/fleet.yaml`, format in `fleetmux/config/fleet.yaml#accounts`); each account carries `provider` (= the quota family of this field), `meter`, `hosts`, `caps`; they are not written into cards and not committed to the repo.
- **handoff_ratio per profile**: the `handoff_ratio` of the profile card (with inheritance) matching the slot's `runtime_profile` is one layer of the section 22 rotation-threshold resolution (after the roster / config `handoff:` table, before `--handoff-ratio`). It gives only the ratio; the window is not taken from the card (the section 22 built-in table / measurements are unchanged). Currently FlashNext 0.5, Opus 0.6 (same as the default); the rest are unset.
- `judgement` / `night_ok` / `domains` / `quota_pool` / `measured` are only data in this item: read by the night switch (B), the brake (E) and the bookkeeper.

### The single meaning of the metadata

| Field | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `desired_profile` | Target intent of the head-of-queue assignment; not proof that it is running |
| `advisory_profile` | Phase 4's first advisory profile; metadata mode uses it and no longer occupies current |
| `runtime_profile` | Profile spawned by this generation; empty for the initial default process; not yet READY while starting |
| `current_profile` | Applied profile that passed READY in this generation; empty when spawn/startup of this generation fails; no longer means advisory |
| `runtime_ready` | Whether this generation passed READY; whether a profile is in effect must be judged from this together with state |
| `runtime_launch` | Frozen snapshot of the card id, actual CLI/model/profile override and role content |
| `profile_state` | `none/pending/switching/ready/applied-live/applied-metadata/blocked` |
| `profile_capabilities/reason/task` | Capability JSON of the target card, status note, related task id |

Metadata-only mode does not change runtime/current/generation: within the safety boundary the first advisory is accepted as `applied-metadata`, a different advisory is `pending`, and the existing runtime keeps running. busy/handoff only record pending; no intent is none. `applied-metadata` is never proof that the actual model has switched.

### Live switch order and failure handling

1. Entered only when the worker is idle and has no unconfirmed idle state left by an abort; starting/busy/handoff/dead cannot switch. While Codex has unfinished native children, the switch stays queued.
2. Validate both switches, assignment, card, role, CLI binary, Pi permission/version/home and the Codex profile file. A missing dependency blocks first, keeps the old runtime, and never silently swaps CLI/model.
3. Save the old screen and the switch history; first increment the generation, keep the stable slot/alias and fence old callbacks. Call the existing `spawn()` to create the successor tab; the actor command pins the generation, and the new CLI waits at the `launch_ready=0` gate.
4. Use the original `tab_into` to keep the right-hand worker pane; the original `kill_pane` retires the old process tree/tab and confirms the old tab is gone before opening the gate to run the new CLI. An actor that has not been granted the gate within 60 seconds fails on its own and does not run the target CLI.
5. The queue is not popped; it waits for the new generation to return a strict `READY`; every CLI is checked for failure/abort/text. Then runtime ready/current and `profile_state=ready` are marked. Profile startup has a default 180-second limit (`profile_ready_timeout`), enforced even when stall_timeout=0; failure or timeout blocks and refuses a late READY.
6. The next idle delivery validates the head of the queue again; the frozen launch must match the current card/role content, and drift blocks. Only after passing is it delivered as `applied-live`. An identical profile that is already READY is not respawned again; a normal context handoff keeps the selected runtime.

Retirement, layout or spawn failures revoke the gate, disable the new generation and try to clean up the successor tab; the old generation is not restored and the model is not rolled back. An old tab that failed to close is recorded in `retired_pane` and needs manual handling. A tab whose startup failed may be kept for debugging; `dead/blocked` means further delivery is forbidden, not proof that every OS process is gone. Terminating the original task clears the queue; it must be fixed and resubmitted. A model READY proves the configured startup and protocol readiness, not an independent check of backend model weights or credential validity.

### Phase 5 verification

- Unit and fake-backend regressions cover the double opt-in, the READY gate, failure cleanup and the three CLIs; a live switch on a real mixed-CLI TUI is not verified. Details: [dynamic-agent-routing.md](dynamic-agent-routing.md).

## 13. Redis run directory inside the project

Redis runtime files move from `/tmp` into this project. This section replaces the old path constraints of §7/§11; it does not change the private instance, the single daemon, task/service, the v1/v2 protocols or the production authorization boundary.

- The default Redis runtime root depends on the install. A source checkout or a release directory (a `pyproject.toml` beside the package) keeps it in `.runtime/redis/`. An installed wheel keeps it in `<FLEETMUX_STATE>/redis-runtime`, by default `~/.fleetmux/state/redis-runtime`. `--redis-runtime-dir` still overrides both. Each run keeps `redis.sock`, `redis.pid`, `redis.log` and `fleet.rdb` in `<root>/<8-char token>/`; the v2 AOF subdirectory lives there too. The Redis binary still comes from `--redis-server`/PATH; no binary is bundled.
- The Redis-only directory is separate from `--runs-dir`: the original workspace/history/agent home paths and the latest entry stay, so long timestamped run paths do not exceed the Unix socket limit. In a source checkout or release directory the default root is `.runtime/redis` next to the code. In an installed wheel the default root is `redis-runtime` under `$FLEETMUX_STATE`, or under `~/.fleetmux/state` when that is unset. The current working directory never decides it.
- `--redis-runtime-dir` may explicitly pick another short, writable root, for read-only installs or separate dev validation. The config pins `redis_runtime_root/redis_runtime_dir/redis_instance_id/redis_socket`; new processes connect per the config, independent of the calling shell's environment or current directory.
- `redis_runtime.py` unifies endpoint validation for startup, CLI/hooks/bridge and control. In a new config the socket must be exactly root/token/redis.sock; cross-instance paths, relative paths, parent-directory traversal, symlink escapes and incomplete metadata are refused; an invalid new config can never be downgraded to the old `/tmp` path.
- The root and each new instance directory are owned by the current user, owner-only (700); an existing root with shared permissions is refused explicitly, and fleet never chmods an arbitrary directory for the user. Existing instance directories are neither reused nor deleted. These permissions are not a security boundary between processes of the same account and do not lift the OS sandbox.
- The socket is checked by encoded byte length: at most 103 bytes on macOS (NUL reserved), 107 bytes on Linux. Too long fails before creation/startup with a hint to shorten the path; no `/tmp` symlink, no automatic fallback to `/tmp` or TCP. Still `--port 0` / `--unixsocketperm 700`.
- Older `/tmp/fleetmux-<token>.sock` configs without managed metadata stay strictly compatible so in-flight instances can status/stop/callback normally; old fleets are not migrated or restarted. It is not a fallback for a new start.
- stop keeps the single daemon's Redis shutdown; Redis cleans up socket/PID, while logs/RDB/AOF stay for debugging and recovery. No shared system Redis is introduced and no other Redis is killed.
- **Orphan Redis reclamation**: when a v2 daemon exits non-explicitly (crash, killed, workspace closed) it deliberately leaves Redis running for `--resume`; but when a run is simply abandoned (replaced by another fleet, never stopped), nobody ever shuts its Redis down. Fix: `start_redis` writes `owner.json` (`{"run": ...}`) in the instance directory; before `start` really starts a new run, and before `start --resume` reattaches, it scans this user's `redis-server unixsocket:` processes once, and for an instance with `owner.json` it SHUTDOWNs only when all three hold: (1) the owner run has no daemon holding the lock; (2) no live process has that run path on its command line (not shut while an actor / hook still writes to it); (3) `owner.json` is older than 120 seconds (covers the gap between start_redis and the daemon taking the lock). A sub-fleet (path contains `/sub/`) is left alone as long as any ancestor run is alive; it belongs to the parent's `collect()`; the target run of `--resume` is left alone; instances without `owner.json` (started before this fix, or not by fleet) are never touched. SHUTDOWN saves; AOF / RDB stay in the original directory, and a later `--resume` restarts Redis from the same directory as usual, lossless. A scan failure only prints and never blocks start; tests disable the machine-wide scan through conftest, and `tests/test_orphan_redis.py` limits itself to its own root. This is the only exception to "no other Redis is killed", and only for instances fleet itself started whose owner run was abandoned.
- `.runtime/` is not under version control; `.gitignore` excludes it.

## 14. Local integration verification of the Redis mailbox

A new start uses Redis v2 only and needs `--allow-development-v2`. Allowed CLI pairs come from `cli_pairs` (section 11). Old v1 configs are read only for drain, status and stop; there is no hot change. Not yet measured: full crash and slow-UI chains, a 1000-message run, and AOF capacity.

Merging does not hot-switch the current v1 run, does not stop its in-flight tasks, and does not mean other machines or production instances have migrated.

## 15. Inventory and migration of communication across all instances

- Inventory configs, processes, registry and transport, distinguishing running instances, old history and leftover windows that are only registered; the number of historical `.inbox` files is not proof that anything is executing.
- A v1 instance is replaced by a new v2 run after its in-flight tasks and queue drain. Old runs/results/handoff records are kept; old generations are not rewritten, and history files are not deleted to fake file-free communication.
- In a new run, the supervisor and every worker must actually verify Redis receipt; acceptance includes no new message Markdown, no model reading message files, and no mechanical supervisor relaying, not just checking config=2.
- Any pair the `cli_pairs` table allows may create a new v2 run. Unverified pairs start only with `--lifecycle smoke`. Protected live/production instances stay out of scope; the communication change does not authorize swapping CLIs, moving subscription credentials, stopping trading services or writing production Redis.
- The read-only inventory tool `python3 -m fleetmux.transport_inventory` reports the machines/directories scanned, config versions, related processes and message file counts; it does not connect to Redis, read message bodies or modify any instance.

## 16. Worker roster hot reload

Requirement: workers can be loaded and unloaded while running, controlled by YAML. This section replaces the old behavior "the worker roster is frozen at `start`"; slot/generation identifiers, the one-page layout, the single daemon and the task/service protocols are unchanged.

- Entry: `start --roster <yaml>`, mutually exclusive with `--workers/--worker-names`; the schema is `workers: [{name, role?, profile?, window?, handoff_ratio?, pi_model?, pi_thinking?, writable?, split?, fresh?}]` (`window` / `handoff_ratio` and the top-level `handoff:` are in section 22); `profile` cannot share a row with `pi_model` / `pi_thinking`; `role` is relative to the yaml's directory and takes precedence over `--roles DIR/<name>.md`. An invalid file at startup refuses to start. A run without `--roster` behaves as before.
- Allowed top-level keys: `workers`, `limits`, `handoff`, `advisor`, `subfleets`, `tabs`, `split`, `max_busy`, `channels`, `applets`, `import_claude_md`, `import_mcp`. Any other key refuses the whole file.
- Run copy (distributed-development.md open question 5): `start --roster <yaml>` with a file outside the run copies it to `<run>/roster.yaml`, and the daemon follows only that copy from then on (hot reload, the supervisor's roster notice, `--advisor`, `fleetmux superpowers load`). Edit the copy, not the source, while the run goes on. Paths the roster resolves against its own directory are written into the copy as absolute paths against the source's directory, so they resolve the same; that rewrite drops YAML comments, and a roster without such paths is copied byte for byte. Sub-rosters (`subfleets[].roster`) and a night roster stay where they are. `start --resume --roster <yaml>` copies the same way; `--kit-fleet` keeps its own `fleet-config/` copy.
- Detection: at the start of every tick the daemon compares the file's mtime/size, then the content hash; the roster changes only in this one place, never inside the per-slot loop.
- Whole-file validation: YAML errors, unknown fields, invalid/duplicate names, a new name not ending in `-<number>` (§3; names already in this run are not checked), zero workers, a missing role file, or a name already used in this run (Redis agent state or workspace directory exists) → the whole file is refused, the current roster stays, and `roster.rejected` is written to the audit (visible in control) listing every reason.
- Add: write `config.json` first (and `latest.json` when it points at this run), then start - hooks/bridge validate membership against the config file; Redis fields are the same as `start_all`, the READY prompt is the same, the tab goes to the end of the right-hand worker column (to the right of the supervisor when there are no workers), and the history page refreshes.
- Remove: mark as retiring - no longer a dispatch target (excluded from decision/request/route validation and profile assignment), but still counted in `max_busy` and in "a worker is busy"; once idle with an empty queue and, in v2, no in-flight delivery, the screen is saved and the old tab closed the same way as a rotation (v2 `retire`), and the slot is removed from the roster and written back to the config. The working directory stays and the history page can still show it. Writing it back into the file before retirement completes cancels the removal.
- Changing only `role`: takes effect in the next generation, no restart.
- Concurrency limit (the limit itself must be hot-reloadable too): top-level `max_busy: <integer ≥ 0>` (0 = unlimited), hot-reloaded by this section's detection; the next `pump()` delivers under the new value, no restart; deleting the key returns to the value of `start --max-busy`. It applies in memory only and is not written back to `config.json` (the first tick after a resume re-reads the roster and applies it again). Non-integer / negative / boolean values refuse the whole file. The budget card's `max_busy` (§ budget card) can still only tighten it. Before this, `--max-busy` was read only once at daemon start, so hot-added workers were stuck in the queue behind the old limit.
- Advisor switch (advisor design v0.7 §2.1): top-level `advisor: on|off`, default off, hot-reloaded by this section's detection, no restart. The roster is the only entry for changes: the user edits the file, `fleet advisor on|off` (changes only the roster; for a run without `--roster` it generates `<run>/roster.yaml` from the current workers and the daemon falls back to reading that file), or the supervisor's decision key `advisor` (the daemon writes the roster and it takes effect immediately; the dispatches of the same decision have already passed the advisor); `start --advisor` only writes `advisor: on` into the roster. config.json no longer stores this switch. A bad value is refused with the whole file; when turning on with a broken kit, enabling is refused, the roster is not written, audit `advisor.rejected`. Turning off clears pending consultation registrations, and slots held waiting for their first config sheet are released with the default (they cannot be held forever); generations already running and generations started from a config sheet do not roll back. Audit `advisor.on` / `advisor.off` (`who`=user|supervisor); the control `STATE` line shows `adv=on|off`.
- Telling the supervisor: no separate message (in task mode a separate message makes the supervisor reply with a RUNNING that has no dispatch, which under the original rule is wrong three times → blocked); instead the current dispatchable worker list is appended to the end of the next business message sent to the supervisor.
- The supervisor adds / retires workers itself (decision: the supervisor can start, stop and unload workers dynamically, without shutting the whole fleet down and starting another): the five supervisor prompts (`supervisor.md` / `supervisor_v2.md` / `service_supervisor.md` / `service_supervisor_v2.md` / `service_supervisor_tui_v2.md`) end part 2 with an "Adding and retiring workers (the roster)" subsection: when to add (more ready independent work than idle workers, and the task description allows it), when to retire (no work left in the plan / parallel width narrows), the task description's limits win (how many at most, which must not be retired, the worktree / role file a new worker needs first); add = a new line `- {name: <role>-<n>, role: <path>}` under `workers:`, retire = delete the line; names are not reused, take the next number, change only `workers:`; dispatch to it only once it appears in the `[Roster change]` list. The placeholder `{{ROSTER}}` is rendered by `roster_notice(cfg)`: when the run has `cfg['roster']` it writes the roster path (with a night roster it also writes the night path and requires both `workers:` to change together, otherwise the day/night switch is refused); without one (started with `--workers` / `--worker-names`) it writes "this run cannot add / retire". The prohibition line now reads "only editing the roster file is allowed"; still no calling the fleet CLI, no typing into tabs, no touching other agents' directories. When the run has a roster, the Codex supervisor's sandbox (`workspace-write`) additionally lists the roster's directory (and the night roster's directory) in `sandbox_workspace_write.writable_roots`, otherwise it cannot change the file (verified with codex-cli 0.164 `debug prompt-input`); the supervisor's `writable_roots` also always include the progress board directory, so `note` is allowed (see contracts §7). **A refusal is also told to the supervisor**: on a whole-file refusal the error text is stored in `rejected` of `fx:roster`, and the end of the next business message gets `[Roster rejected] <errors, one per line>; the roster in effect is unchanged: <current dispatchable list>`, cleared once sent; the same refused content is not reported twice (the content hash is reused); if the roster is changed to a valid one before the notice is delivered, the notice is dropped and only `[Roster change]` is reported. Known boundary: after the advisor switch writes `<run>/roster.yaml` for a run without `--roster`, the daemon hot-reloads it too, but the prompt still says "cannot add / retire"; the few messages sent directly via `self.v2.queue('supervisor', …)` (service `[blocked]` / `[dialog]` etc.) carry neither notice, like `[Roster change]`, and wait for the next message that goes through `Fleet.queue`.
- A Codex supervisor runs in the Codex workspace-write sandbox. For the supervisor slot only, the program adds writable roots (`sandbox_workspace_write.writable_roots`): the directory of the roster file, the directory of the night roster when there is one, and the session board directory (section 65). This lets the supervisor edit the roster and write notes on the session board. Codex workers get no extra writable roots from this rule.
- Known boundaries: a newly added worker that does not reach READY at startup blocks in-flight tasks under the original startup timeout rule; a retiring worker that is held does not retire and needs manual handling; nothing is delivered while paused, so a retiring worker's queue does not drain.

## 17. Resume from a checkpoint

Designed after an internal production-line resume design. This section **changes the §11 rule "a daemon restart in the same run does not redo automatically"**; the rest (single daemon, slot/generation identifiers, one-page layout, task/service protocols, §16 roster semantics) is unchanged.

- **Entry**: `start --resume <run directory|auto>`. `auto` takes the run in `latest.json`; if it already has `result.json`, cannot be read or is not v2 → a new run starts as usual. An explicit directory in those cases is refused with the reason. If the run's `daemon.lock` is held (the daemon is still alive) → nothing is done. Only v2 runs are accepted.
- A `result.json` with `resumable: true` does not make a run finished for `start --resume`. Such a file is written by a lifecycle hold timeout stop or by a close from the fleetmux sidebar. When the run's task is still `running`, `start --resume` deletes that `result.json`, so the task resumes and a later stop does not read the run as finished. Every other `result.json` is kept.
  - **Open jobs (section 58 "Restart")**: a service root run of this fleet home that holds open jobs (`<run>/jobs.json`) and no live daemon is continued instead: by `start --resume <dir>` and `--resume auto` even when it has a `result.json` from an explicit stop (kept as `result.stopped.json`), and by a plain `start` too. The run is found through `latest.json`, the home index `<fleet home>/job-runs/`, or `<fleet home>/runs/*`, newest first; a plain `start` prints which typed options it does not apply. Every exit that would start a new run instead (no usable `latest.json`, a stub there, a finished run, an unreadable or non-v2 config, a refused config, a missing Redis directory, a Redis that does not load) either continues the run or hands its open jobs to the new run (section 58 "Restart"). A retryable refusal (a bad flag, security, the backend) exits non-zero and leaves the run and its jobs as they are.
  - A plain `start` that continues such a run carries the **same redo authorization as `--resume`**: in-flight deliveries are marked uncertain and released as operator-authorized redos, service requests in `fx:reqq` without a result get their timeout window reset and are routed again, a request with `"replay": false` is published `timeout` ("not replayed on resume"), and each service redo counts toward `--max-attempts`; a task dispatch re-queued by the resume keeps its attempt number (see **Attempt limit**).
- **Redis**: first connect to the original socket and use it if it answers (only the daemon died); otherwise restart with the config's original `redis_runtime_dir` and original socket, reloading the AOF from the original directory (`fleet.start_redis`, shared with a new start). `redis_runtime.prepare` is not called (it refuses to reuse a directory). The roughly 1-second window of AOF `everysec` is unchanged.
- **Completion is judged by disk/Redis**: task artifacts keep `check_artifacts`; a worker reply already exists in the `result` field of the message hash (no TTL, no trimming), so no separate checkpoint is written; service completion is judged by `fx:result:<id>`. That key expires after seven days (`RESULT_TTL`).
- **New principle (replaces that §11 rule)**: only an explicit `--resume` redoes uncertain in-flight messages, and each redo is counted; a same-run restart without `--resume` still goes through `recover()`: mark uncertain, pause everything, redo nothing. `--resume` itself is the operator's authorization to redo: in-flight deliveries are first marked uncertain under the original rule, then released as "operator authorized the redo" (`resolve_uncertain`); a reattached live window first gets Escape to abort the old turn. **This is the only place where Escape is taken as "stop accepted" rather than as evidence of a stop.**
- **Windows**: only official CLI (Codex/Claude) workers are reattached, when the window still exists, the CLI process is still alive and it was READY last time; a task supervisor always starts the next generation (starting over from the brief); pi, opencode and dsh workers always start the next generation (`RESUME_CLIS` holds only codex and claude). The next generation takes the original position (`tab_into` the old tab, then close the old tab, as in a handoff); a worker whose old tab is gone goes to the end of the worker column; if the supervisor window is gone too -> close leftovers, start the next generation of everything, and rearrange the whole page with `arrange`. The new control tab is merged back into the history column. Only live workers take part in `arrange`: a worker that is delegated, retired or dead, or whose pane is no longer on the server, is left out of the placement, so one stale pane id never leaves the page without its history pane; when the supervisor window stays but the history pane is empty or gone, it is laid out again with the same rule. A delegated worker whose tmux session still runs gets a new client tab of that session on the parent page (the sub-fleet supervisor's live session), but only while the sub-fleet's current supervisor is still that adopted session; otherwise one `resume.delegated_view_lost` audit record and no tab. A resumed sub-fleet keeps an adopted supervisor whose tmux session still runs and opens a new view of it instead of starting another supervisor generation; a delegated slot stays out of `arrange` either way.
- **Reconciliation**: first replay prepared intents (an applying intent of unknown state → fall back to `recover()`); release each slot's in-flight state and cancel the queue; lift the pause (the original pause reason is written to the audit `daemon.resumed`). task: this task's queued/in-flight worker dispatches are requeued verbatim **by the daemon itself** (message field `replay` = which attempt, carried unchanged: the resume lost them, not the session), then the new supervisor gets a brief: goal, artifacts verified/missing, dispatches completed (with reply summaries), dispatches requeued (stated explicitly as not to be dispatched again), and only the missing ones should be added. The design said "it only dispatches what is missing"; the daemon, not the supervisor, redispatches the lost one so that the attempt limit lands on a definite message. service: requests in `fx:reqq` without a result get their timeout window reset and are rerouted; those with `"replay": false` publish `timeout` (reason "not replayed on resume").
- **Time budget**: downtime still counts toward the task's `hours`; on resume, a task already past its deadline is judged `budget_exhausted` in the first tick and `result.json` is written.
- **A run that never submitted a task**: a run that failed before its first READY and was closed by the fleetmux sidebar (`resumable: true`) never had a task. **The criterion is strict**: `result.json` resumable + reason contains `startup failed before READY` + `task_id` / `goal` / `artifacts` all empty, and `fx:task` has no id / goal / deadline / lifecycle_spec, a status only of `blocked` / `budget_exhausted`, and empty artifacts - only this one shape counts as "no task". **An empty id or a missing deadline alone does not count**: any other record (id without deadline, deadline without id, unreadable fields) is a corrupt/partial record and always fails closed with the reason; no id/goal/deadline is fabricated, and the expiry budget gate of a real task is not bypassed (a really expired task still needs an explicit `--extend-hours`). **"Empty" must really be empty**: artifacts must parse to a real empty list (missing / `[]`; `{}`, a string, a number or unreadable all count as non-empty -> not a placeholder); the deadline must be a usable number (positive, finite; `soon` / `0` / `-5` / `nan` / `inf` are unusable -> corrupt); a non-empty string is not enough. **An empty `fx:task` does not mean no task was ever submitted**: as long as a readable `result.json` still carries a business identity (`task_id` / `goal` / non-empty `artifacts` / `deadline`), an empty `fx:task` is a contradiction and always fails closed; it cannot be taken as "healthy, no task"; a missing `result.json` must be kept apart from one that exists but cannot be read/parsed, and an unreadable result is never treated as an empty result. A truly healthy run that never submitted a task and never wrote a closed result stays valid. There is one more **valid** "never submitted a task" case: a task-mode run closed by the fleetmux sidebar while idle: `status=stopped`, `closed_by=user`, `closed_via=fleetmux-sidebar`, `resumable=true`, `base_mode=task`, `task_id`/`goal` empty, `artifacts=[]`, `at`/`closed_at` numeric, reason non-empty - only a strict, complete, contradiction-free match of this shape counts as none (no startup-failure reason required); any mismatch (has goal/artifacts, another close path, not stopped, not task base mode, unusable time, a closed record that is neither the placeholder nor this shape) is refused as corrupt. On restore it takes the same common resume path as the strict placeholder and clears the old closed record (the original text is kept as evidence).
  - The common layer of `start --resume` classifies centrally with the same `lifecycle_hold.classify` (`none` / `task` / corrupt) **before deleting `result.json` and before spawning the daemon asynchronously**: with no task, the daemon resumes only on the original run / original Redis / original slot names - no new fleet, no fabricated task, no fake `status=running`; a corrupt record exits non-zero with an explicit error, `result.json` is not deleted and `spawn_window` is not called. `lifecycle_hold.reopen` writes `status=running` only for a real task (has an id and a usable deadline); before, it treated a missing deadline as 0, so the placeholder was misreported as an "expired task" with a suggestion of `--extend-hours`, and after bypassing that, `RuntimeV2.brief` raised a KeyError on `task['id']`, leaving a non-empty running task without identity.
  - On the daemon side, `RuntimeV2.resume_run` classifies again **before retiring / respawning / replaying any side effect** (second layer): **no task reuses no early return**; it takes the same common resume path as a healthy empty task - first the `replay_intents` safety gate (an unknown intent still holds and leaves the slots alone), then retire / reattach / generations / respawn (with no task, the startup line is "no task yet, reply READY") and `Fleet.resume` lifts paused; **only the task brief is skipped**; finally the normal writer clears the old `blocked` `fx:task` and the closed `result.json` (kept verbatim as `<run>/result.closed.json`, evidence of the failure event), and the run goes back to waiting for a task / READY, after which a new task (real id, real deadline) can be submitted normally. A corrupt record still exits with an explicit error and touches no slot; `brief` without an id raises an error instead of a KeyError.
  - The fleetmux sidebar's restore button (`^b r`): identity is **the pane id of this spawn** (not the title); only an **explicit non-zero exit** clears the "restoring..." status and leaves one actionable reason on the status line (full output goes to `server.log`, the original output is traceable); exit 0 (the daemon starts asynchronously) and unknown status are not failures; no automatic retry (the operator pressing restore again is the retry), and the daemon's kv `restorable` is not touched; the operator killing that pane is recorded as "restore cancelled". So the last piece of output can still be read, a reaped pane stays in the store for one more tick (`list` / `info` stop reporting it immediately; `feed_output` can still write to it). **Pane reaping must complete in the same tick**: the child being reaped and the PTY reaching EOF are two separate events; in the tick of the reap it must report the exit, remove the pane atomically from store and layout, write `pane ... exited` and give the restore attempt an explicit completion - **without requiring the PTY to be fully closed** (a background child may hold the slave forever). SIGCHLD only wakes the single PTY event loop; the loop first feeds output the reader thread has already sent into the parser, then reaps in **the same tick**: no sleep to guess at draining, no second dispatcher, no unbounded wait, and no dependence on the next PTY / SIGCHLD / other-pane event. The PTY's own reader thread sets `eof` after the last byte: with `eof` the tail is the complete output; without `eof` (the PTY is still open) the tail reaches only what had been parsed at that moment, `pty_open` notes "later output is not included" in the `pane ... exited` log, and the status line never falsely claims "no output" - the boundary is "output collected", not "all output". `list` and layout are always consistent; there is never "the list is empty but the layout still has a dead pane". (macOS hangs up the terminal when the pane's session leader exits, so "PTY still open" is reproducible only on Linux; macOS takes the equivalent EOF path.)
- **Attempt limit**: `--max-attempts` (default 2, written to config, may be overridden on resume). Over the limit, a task is judged `blocked` (stating which worker and which instruction), and service records `timeout` with the reason. A task attempt is spent only by a loss of the session's own (the session ended, a frozen session restarted, an API error retry, §24 / §43); an operator's `start --resume` (and so `fleetmux upgrade`) re-queues a task's lost dispatches without spending one and without judging them against the limit (when every upgrade counted one attempt, the second upgrade during one long instruction blocked the job sub-fleet with "lost again after 2 attempt(s)").
- **End marker**: when a task enters any terminal state the daemon writes `<run>/result.json` (status, reason, task id, per-artifact re-verification, atomic write); it is deleted when the same run accepts a new task; but while the supervisor slot is still held (an in-flight delivery withheld at the previous task's terminal state; a pi supervisor has no hold exit, see §24 known gap ⑦-4) a new task is refused with the reason and a hint to start a new run (before this fix it was accepted and the task could never be delivered). It is also written after an explicit `stop` successfully retires all runtimes (service records `stopped`), so `stop` ends the run, except a service root that holds open jobs, which the next start continues (section 58 "Restart"). A job sub-fleet suspended by its root's stop writes `suspended` (its task stays running; kept as `result.suspended.json` when it is handed on). On resume, a task already terminal in Redis without `result.json` → it is written then and the run is treated as ended.
- **Roster**: the roster already persisted in config.json is kept; a given `--roster` is written into the config, and the daemon reconciles it with the §16 hot reload in its first tick.
- **Known boundaries (P2)**: a lost "report format correction" message is requeued verbatim to the new generation (which has no context); when only the daemon died and Redis did not, a turn the worker finished during the downtime is refused and done again; Pi is not reattached; the whole-page rebuild when only the history column remains is untested on real cmux; `has_cli_process` judges by process (§23: the executable name without `.exe` is codex / claude, or `node` with a script path containing the CLI name; a bare shell or an unrelated node does not count).



- **Oversized resume brief**: after a dev_task run had accepted several hundred dispatches, the "completed dispatches" list of `RuntimeV2.brief` pushed the brief past `message_bus.MAX_BODY` (200000 bytes); `start --resume` raised `ValueError` inside the resume transaction and the daemon died on every start. Now `fit_brief`: when the brief exceeds `BRIEF_LIMIT` (120000 bytes) the full text is written to `<run>/handoffs/resume-brief-<task>-<ms>.md`; the brief queued to the supervisor keeps the opening (goal, workers, downtime facts), the most recent progress and the requeued / already-run lists, and states the full-text path with an instruction to read it first; audit `resume.brief_spilled`. Test `tests/test_resume_brief_size.py`. Known gap: the section 25 night-switch brief uses `progress()` as well and has no such cap yet.
- **Restart state**: `RuntimeV2.resume_run` first writes a restart-state snapshot. After the resume, the daemon reconciles the fleet against the previous snapshot and opens a reconcile ledger (section 60).

## 18. Interface contract: task numbers within one fleet + the start gate

Spec: an internal interface-contract spec (approved, including four rulings) and section 1-2 of an internal nested-decomposition design. This section covers only **a single fleet** (top-level supervisor = this fleet's supervisor); peer notices are section 21, sub-fleets are section 19, escalate and veto are section 20. The single daemon, slot/generation identifiers, one-page layout, task/service protocols, the section 16 roster and the section 17 resume are all unchanged.

- **Switch**: enabled only with task spec `"contracts": true` (strict boolean); without it, Redis, prompts, report relaying and artifact checks are all as before. `skeleton_worker` is optional, default the first worker.
- **State**: one JSON in Redis `fx:contracts` (phase / decomposition table / registry / pending proposals / task table / reply ownership per worker), carried into resume by AOF; every change is written by the program to `<run>/contracts.json` and copied to `<workspace>/contracts.json`, and the tick re-syncs from Redis; `<run>/contracts.log.jsonl` is an append log. Models only read it.
- **Prompts**: `agents/contracts_supervisor.md` (appendix A) / `contracts_worker.md` (appendix B §4), injected only in contract tasks: at submit they are written into `CONTRACTS.md` in every agent directory and the task message points to it; generations started during the task (handoff / roster hot add) get the same section appended to AGENTS.md. The base `supervisor*.md` / `worker*.md` are unchanged.
- **P0**: the first reply must be `phase: "interfaces"` + `plan` + `interfaces` + an empty dispatch; five checks (no producer / no consumer and not final / cycle / sample self-check / exactly one writer per field) refuse item by item; **a P0 refusal does not count toward the three corrections and has no limit** (P0 is relaxed until everything is resolved), bound only by the task's `hours` budget. The interfaces / skeleton phases are exempt from "RUNNING must dispatch when no worker is busy" (`others_active`; v1 and v2 validation share one entry). In P0 `path` is checked only for being relative; whether it is under the `to` worker's directory is checked at dispatch (P0 has no `to`).
- **P1**: after P0 the daemon itself dispatches `[skeleton]` to the skeleton worker; when that reply arrives it reads `skeleton.md`: one PASS line per interface and no FAIL → everything `declared`, frozen at v1, and `[contract] interfaces frozen at v1` is forwarded to the supervisor with the reply; otherwise back to P0, **with no attempt limit** (as above, bound only by the `hours` budget); literal `PASS/FAIL` copied into `skeleton.md` (header / legend) is not a failure.
- **Task table and dispatch**: a dispatch item must carry `id` (M number) and may carry `provides` / `consumes` (`id@version`) / `edits` / `depends_on` + `serial_reason`; the refusal texts of spec §3.1 / §3.3 / §3.0.1④ are used verbatim. Items whose `depends_on` is unfinished are held by the daemon and delivered automatically once all predecessors are `done`. Reply ownership pops FIFO by "whose reply each message expects, recorded at queue time": a dispatch records its M number, `[contract]` notices and proposal refusals record nothing, so a notice turn never closes another task. A task is `done` = every interface it provides is `ready`; replied but not ready records `failed`, and the supervisor redispatches the same number (attempts+1).
- **Readiness and changes**: a worker reports `Interfaces: IF-x@n provided: ready` → only after the program's literal check (exists / length / contains / json_keys) passes is it `ready`, and `[contract] ready` is delivered to running consumers; otherwise `[contract] not ready` goes to producer and consumers. `interface_change` in the last JSON object at the end of a worker report (`raw_decode` from the back, not the first `{`) is validated per §4.3; if it passes it becomes pending and is forwarded to the supervisor, who approves / rejects with `interfaces[].decision`; compatible / breaking declared by the supervisor itself counts as approved. The program grades by field diff (changing path, removing contains / json_keys = breaking). After a breaking change is approved the old version is `deprecated` and its consumers `stale`, and their next dispatch must consume the new version. Notices go only to workers running the task; the rest rely on reading `contracts.json`.
- **COMPLETED**: additionally requires every non-deprecated interface to be `ready`, otherwise it bounces as before with `Independent verification failed ... IF-x@n not ready (...)`. The artifacts of a contract task may add `not_contains`; `cmd_exit` (argv without a shell, run in the shared output directory, `timeout_s` default 120, max 600, output written to `path` and then checked literally) **accepts only what the caller declared in the task spec** (saved separately as `caller_artifacts` at submit, compared by path + argv + timeout): a supervisor decision that adds or replaces a cmd_exit is corrected as an invalid decision, and an undeclared command mixed into the stored table is only reported as a failure and never run; commands run only at COMPLETED, and result.json / the resume brief only recheck files. Non-contract tasks still ignore these two keys. The decisions of `pending_user` are summarized into `contracts` of result.json.
- **v2**: free-text reports of official CLI workers gain the matching keys only when they contain `Interfaces:` / `Peer notices:` / `Question for supervisor:` lines or a trailing JSON with keys such as `interface_change`; the four sections stay verbatim; the resume brief appends one line of the task table.
- **Known boundaries (P2)**: `cmd_exit` runs only caller-declared commands (contract / frozen tasks only, no shell, time-limited), and the daemon blocks while it runs; a Pi worker can hand in contract fields only as extra keys of fleet_result (whether TypeBox lets extra keys through is untested); `consumers_runtime.session` is filled by §21; contract notices cost running workers an extra turn; header lines such as `Interfaces:` are also extracted separately when they appear in a non-contract v2 report. The P0 / dispatch refusal texts are wrapped in the original "not a valid decision JSON" correction; P0 refusals are not counted, the rest (bad JSON, dispatch validation) still block after the original three consecutive refusals; an official CLI worker that does not reply to a `[contract]` notice in Done/Evidence form goes through the original 2 corrections and is then blocked as well; the profile recommendation of a held item is computed at decision time, not recomputed at release; `writers` can only be enforced for `json_keys`.
- **With the decomposition tree on (section 62)**: the contract gates (P0, P1, readiness, COMPLETED) are unchanged and come first; the tree sits on top. The P0 reply also hands in the first tree (a contract task is not exempt from the first-reply rule), every dispatch id must be a block of the tree, and a contract item without `edits` / `depends_on` / `serial_reason` takes them from its block (`owns` / `depends_on` / `serial_reason`); written and different from the tree, it is refused (`M0 edits differ from the tree (owns): change the tree, not the dispatch`; lists compare as sets). The `dispatch[].leaf` line of `CONTRACTS.md` is dropped; `leaf` is the program's.

## 19. Several fleets side by side + sub-fleet start/stop and window handover

This section **replaces section 8 "each machine keeps only one fleet registry"** and **adds one cell to the section 11 v2 support matrix**; the single daemon (one per fleet), slot/generation identity, the one-page layout and the task/service protocol are unchanged. Split requests and their approval are section 20; this section only has the low-level start/stop and handover mechanism.

- **Side by side**: the cmux registry name = `fleetmux` + task number (main `fleetmux`, child `fleetmux:M0`, grandchild `fleetmux:M0.1`), one window registry per fleet (`fleet.manager_for(cfg)`). `latest.json` records only the main fleet; a sub-fleet's run directory is `<parent run>/sub/<number>` (a retry is `<number>-attempt-<n>`, the old directory is kept on file), and its state is in its `subfleet.json`. `status` shows the whole tree; a main-fleet `stop` first stops the descendants (each child daemon first stops its own sub-fleets) and reclaims the panes; a child daemon's explicit stop closes its own Redis, and the parent closes the Redis of a crashed sub-fleet, so no orphans are left. `start --resume` of the main fleet: a sub-fleet whose child daemon is gone has its Redis restarted on the original socket/directory and is brought up with `daemon --resume`.
- **One root fleet per machine; `start` attaches** (`fleetmux/join.py`): a `start` that finds a live root fleet of this user on this machine starts nothing (a task root takes task starts only: see "Task root" below). It does not provision Redis, a run directory or panes, opens no view, writes a join stub and exits 0. The work then goes in with `fleetmux task --config <stub> -`, and the root (service, or a task root whose own task runs) runs it as a job in its own task-mode sub-fleet on the root's server. That sub-fleet is listed in the root's sidebar below the existing ones, in the order the jobs were submitted. The stub's `--name`, `--roster`, `--roles` (absolute paths, resolved when the stub is written), `--lifecycle`, `--budget` and its fleet settings (`overrides`, below) go into each job spec that does not set them (`join.FORWARD`); a dev_task job's `deliver` goes in the task spec itself, as for a root dev_task run. So a whole development fleet (its roster, roles and delivery gate) runs as one job (§58). Both modes attach: a task start becomes a task sub-fleet (a job, above), and a service start (`--mode service` or a service lifecycle) becomes a service sub-fleet of the running root service fleet (§29 "Attached service sub-fleets").
  - **Start options of an attaching start** (`join.start_settings`): only the options the person typed count (a value other than the parser default). Every option of the start parser is in exactly one group (a test checks it): taken by the stub (`--workers`, `--worker-names`, `--roster`, `--kit-fleet`, `--roles`, `--lifecycle`, `--mode`, `--name`, `--budget`); about this command only (`--attach`, `--gui`, `--no-view`, `--no-join`, `--resume`, `--fleet-home`, `--backend`, ...); this machine's set-up, where the root's own value is used and stderr says so (binaries, Redis / cmux / Codex directories, `--runs-dir`, `--host`, `--security`, `--prompt-table`, `--allow-unsandboxed-pi`, ...); forwarded to the sub-fleet's config (`overrides`: `--supervisor-split`, `--request-timeout`, `--max-busy`, `--max-attempts`, `--worker-idle-reclaim-min`, the stall and handoff settings, `--window`, `--claude-window`, `--clear-timeout`, `--lease-reclaim`, `--worker-model`, `--claude`, `--worker-kit`, `--worker-domains`, `--brake-notify`, `--allow-profile-live-switch`; a service start also `--worker-cli claude|pi` with `--pi-model` / `--pi-thinking`, and `--supervisor-model`); refused (`--profile`, `--worker-profile`, `--supervisor-profile`, `--supervisor-pi-*`, a `--supervisor-cli` other than claude, a worker CLI other than claude / pi, opencode / dsh options, `--compact-limit`, `--advisor`, `--night-roster`, `--channel-user-resume`, `--initiator`, `--project-dir`, the retired switches; for a task start also `--supervisor-model` (use the job spec's `supervisor_profile`), `--supervisor-split` and pi workers). A refusal names every such option, starts nothing and points at `--no-join`. `--worker-kit`, `--worker-domains` and `--supervisor-split` are never inherited from the root: when the start does not give them, the sub-fleet gets no kit, no domains and no supervisor split. The sub-fleet's supervisor is a root worker running official Claude Code; with `--supervisor-model` the root takes only an idle worker already on that model, else adds a worker whose launch record runs that model (`named_subfleet.added_launch`).
  - **Service start**: `start` sends the root the op `subfleet.attach` (`fleet.py request --config <root config> -`) with the child's name (from `--name`: lower case, other characters as `-`; `svc-<hex>` without a name), `--roster` or `--workers` / `--worker-names`, `--roles`, the worker settings `--worker-model`, `--claude`, `--worker-cli pi` with `--pi-model` / `--pi-thinking` (`entry`), the start's other fleet settings (`overrides`) and `--supervisor-model` (`supervisor_model`). Only an `accepted` answer writes the stub (`"kind": "service"`, `child`, `attach_id`); a refusal (for example a name clash) is printed, nothing is started, and the message points at `--no-join`. The child comes up on a worker of the root and runs until it is stopped.
  - **Task root**: a root task fleet takes jobs too, while its own task runs. The job path is the service one (§58: same `job_submit` record, program slot pick or added worker, task-mode sub-fleet, result under the job id); only the entry, the close and the guard differ.
    - **Entry.** `fleetmux task` to a v2 root task fleet whose task is `running` is a job (`Fleet.job_root_busy`). With no task running, the first `task` is the fleet's own task, as before. A join stub sends `task --job` (event `job: true`): it is always a job, and refused ("runs no task of its own now") when the root has no task running, so a stub's work never becomes the root's task. A sub-fleet takes no jobs; a second `task` there is still refused ("already running").
    - **Close.** While any job is open (queued, starting or running), the root's COMPLETED is held, not bounced. A slot that supervises a job's sub-fleet does not count as unsettled. When the other workers have settled and verification (artifacts, contract gate) passes, the reason goes into `TASK.jobs_hold`, the supervisor gets one notice ("COMPLETED recorded"), and nothing counts as a bounce or a bad reply. The task stays `running`, so the job sub-fleets run on. `Fleet.job_task_root_tick` (in `job_tick`, every daemon tick) finishes the task `completed` with the held reason once no job is open and no worker is unsettled; `lifecycle_close` then closes the run as for any completed task. A RUNNING reply that dispatches new work clears the hold. Other unsettled slots, including supervisor-split and escalate sub-fleets, bounce as before.
    - **Guard.** `job_guard` runs on task decisions too: no dispatch to a job's slot, no dispatch `id` equal to a job's `M<n>`, no `withdraw` of a job's slot, no answer to a job's split. An open job counts as pending work, so a RUNNING reply with an empty dispatch is valid.
    - **Task end.** When the root task ends any other way (blocked, timeout, cancelled), running job sub-fleets are stopped and collected, and their jobs are published `failed`; queued and starting jobs are published `failed` ("before the job started"). An explicit stop of a task root is such an end (its task is `cancelled`): the section 58 restart rules (suspend, continue, take-over) apply to service roots only.
    - **Service start.** A task root takes no service sub-fleet. When the only live root is a task fleet, a service `start` runs a fleet of its own and says so on stderr; when that task fleet runs in this very fleet home, which runs one fleet, it refuses and starts nothing (stop it, use another fleet home with `--no-join`, or run the root as a service fleet).
  - **Limits**: the number of stubs is not capped, but the root's sub-fleet limits still apply. Jobs have no default `max_children` / `max_agents_total` / `child_workers_max` (§58); a job waits in the root's queue only while a root config or roster `limits:` value refuses another child, or while no worker can be added for it. The sidebar shows at most `MAX_FLEETS` (16) cards; in creation order the newest are the ones cut.
  - **Discovery**: the default fleet home and every `~/.fleetmux/fleets/<name>/` (from `fleetmux/paths.py`; `FLEETMUX_JOIN_ROOT` replaces `~/.fleetmux` here, which the tests use), plus this start's fleet home. A home counts when its `latest.json` names a run whose daemon holds its lock, that is a root (no `parent_config`) and that runs on the fleetmux backend. The choice is deterministic: a service-mode root first, then a root in this start's fleet home, then the earliest started (run directory name).
  - **Join stub**: `{"join": {"root_config", "root_home", "root_mode", "roster", "roles", "lifecycle", "mode", "name", "budget", "label", "created", "jobs": []}}`, with paths made absolute; a service stub adds `"kind": "service"`, `child` and `attach_id`. It is written to `<fleet home>/latest.json` while that file holds nothing to lose: it is absent, names a finished run (`result.json`) that holds no open jobs (section 58 "Restart"), or is a stub with no job and no child yet. Otherwise (it names a live run, such as the root itself in the same home, a run that has not finished and that `start --resume auto` would pick up, or a stub that has jobs or a child) the stub is written to `<fleet home>/joins/<name or generated label>.json`, or `<name>-2.json` and so on while that name holds a stub with jobs. No record is overwritten. The attach line prints the exact `--config` path.
  - **Commands on a stub**: `task` merges the stub's `lifecycle` / `budget` into the spec for the keys the spec does not set (the spec wins), submits it with `fleet.py task --config <root config> -`, and records the job id in `jobs`. A job counts as accepted only when the reply has `status == "accepted"` and a `job_id`. It is sent with `--job`, so a task root takes it only as a job (see "Task root"). A refusal, or a reply that times out (`pending`), is reported on stderr with exit 2, and the job is never resubmitted. `status` shows the last job's row and its sub-fleet entry from the root's `status`, or the job's result once it has ended. `stop` first cancels the job on the root (`task --cancel <job id>`, section 58 "Cancel"): a queued, starting or suspended job ends there with a result; for a running one the cancel is recorded and then `stop --config <that sub-fleet's config.json>` runs, only after checking that its `parent_config` is the root; the root itself is never stopped from a stub. While the root is down, `stop` writes the cancel into the root run (`job-cancels/`) and the root applies it when it starts again. `result` runs `result <job id> --config <root config>`; when the root is down, or its Redis no longer holds the result, it reads the copy the root kept in `<run>/job-results/`. When the stub's root run is down and another root runs in the same fleet home (`root_home`), every command follows it: the stub's `root_config` is rewritten (the old one kept in `previous_roots`) and stderr says so. Every other command refuses a stub. On a service stub: `request`, `results` and `result <id>` go to the child's own config (`<root run>/sub/<child>/config.json`, refused while its daemon is not up); `result` without an id is the root's answer to the attach (the child's address once it is up); `status` shows the child's entry in the root's `status` tree; `stop` sends the root `subfleet.detach`, so only the child goes down (drain, then down), never the root; `task` is refused with a pointer to `request`. `logs`, `operator`, `pause` and `resume` run on the child's own config.json (refused while its daemon is not up), and `fleetmux applet <command> --config <stub>` uses the child's config.json as the applet host. The attach line also prints that config path (`child_config` in the stub) for commands the stub does not forward. Roster, roles and name stay in the stub until the job spec accepts them.
  - **`start --no-join`** keeps the old behaviour: a fleet of its own, and in a fleet home whose fleet is still running, the refusal "fleetmux tabs already exist". An explicit `--backend` other than fleetmux also starts a fleet of its own.
  - **Live root** (`join.live_root`): its daemon holds the run's `daemon.lock`, its private Redis answers, and it records no explicit stop (`fx:service` reason `cancelled: explicit fleet stop...`, or `fx:task` status `cancelled` with reason `explicit fleet stop...` / `stopped by operator`). A root that was just stopped keeps its lock while it winds down (retire, Redis shutdown, tmux); it is no target. When that root runs in this start's own fleet home, `start` waits up to `join.STOP_WAIT` seconds for it to let go of its lock (its tabs would refuse the start), then starts its own fleet. A root in this start's own fleet home with the same `--name` is the fleet being started again: never a target (a live one then gets the "fleetmux tabs already exist" refusal). A root that stops between discovery and a service attach: `start` starts its own fleet instead of refusing.
  - **Sidebar order**: `subfleet.children_dirs` returns sub-fleets in creation order (`subfleet.json` `created`, else the directory mtime), not by directory name, so `M2` comes before `M10`. `tree()`, `status` and the sidebar cards follow it. The sidebar cards then list, at every level, the running sub-fleets first (creation order, each followed by its own sub-fleets) and the finished ones after them, newest first (`fleetmux_sidebar._running_first`), so the 16-card cap never hides a running sub-fleet; a finished sub-fleet keeps its history page.
  - **Closing a sub-fleet from the sidebar**: the root daemon adds an optional `close` object (`title`, `body`) to a running direct child's card when there is a stop path for it (`fleetmux_sidebar.stop_path`: a job whose row is open and not being cancelled, or a named or attached sub-fleet not already going down) and no stop is pending; only then does the server draw that card's close button and open a confirmation box with those texts. Confirming (the `y` key) writes the root-only kv `child_stop` `{"v":1,"state":"requested","id","page","at","action"}` (`action` is the card's `close.action`, `"stop"` here); any other key, a paste or a press outside the confirm button writes nothing. The root daemon handles each request once (`id` + `at`) and checks the card again by the same rule, never by its id alone: a job through `Fleet.job_cancel` (its result is published failed with outcome `cancelled` when the sub-fleet is collected; the result format is unchanged), a named sub-fleet through a `subfleet.down` that the confirmation approves (no question to the root supervisor; one `subfleet.sidebar_down` audit record; the program gate still refuses an autostart one), an attached one through `subfleet.detach`. It answers `accepted` or `refused` with a reason, shown as a notice; while the request is out the card has no button and the status line says so. A card whose stop was sent is drawn `stopping`. Split / escalate sub-fleets and deeper levels have no button. An older server ignores the per-card `close` and draws no button.
  - **Dismissing a finished sub-fleet card**: a finished direct child (its record's `state` is `returned` or `failed`, which covers the completed, cancelled, blocked and failed outcomes; a job or a named sub-fleet; nothing below it still running) gets a `close` object with `"action":"dismiss"` (`fleetmux_sidebar.dismiss_path`); a running card's `close` says `"action":"stop"`. Any other state (`down`, `detached`, `suspended`, anything added later) is not finished here and has no button, and so do split / escalate children and deeper levels, as for the stop. The server draws the button for a `reclaimed` card only with `"action":"dismiss"`, also when the card has no `page` (an older attempt of a task); its box title starts with the card id (`Remove M21 from the sidebar: <card title>?`, so the id survives the server clipping the title to one row) and its confirm button is `[ Remove from sidebar ]`, both in the interface language (keys `sidebar.child_dismiss.*`, `modal.dismiss.*`, section 63). Confirming writes kv `child_stop` with `"action":"dismiss"`; a request without `action` (an older server) is the stop, and the daemon never turns one request into the other. The root daemon checks the card again by the same rule, adds it to `<run>/sidebar_dismissed.json` (`{"v":1,"cards":{"<card id>":{"task_id","created","at","by"}}}`, the card id being the child's run directory name; written atomically next to `sub/`, never inside it), logs one `sidebar.child_dismissed` line in the root's own log and answers `accepted` with `"action":"dismiss"`. Every push reads that file again (no copy in memory), so a restarted daemon and a resumed run, which keep the same run directory, leave the card out as well; the dismissed card and everything below it are skipped before the 16-card cap counts, so the next older card comes in. An entry hides the card only while the record's `created` matches (`null` matches only a record without `created`), the card is not active and nothing below it is active: the same directory brought up again shows again. Dismissing touches no sub-fleet directory, record, `result.json`, workspace, page or audit record; `tree()` and the history page still list it. When the root fleet is closed, its sub-fleet cards left in kv `sidebar` lose their `close` (no daemon is left to answer). There is no button to bring a dismissed card back: delete its entry from `sidebar_dismissed.json` (or the whole file). An older server draws no button for a finished card.
- **Names and titles**: `start --name` sets a human-readable fleet name (default: the first 24 characters of the task goal, then the run token); only when `--name` is given does the main workspace title become `Fleet <name>`, otherwise it stays `Fleet agents`. A child workspace title is `<main fleet name> › worker-1 › 子 fleet M0` (sub-fleet M0), a grandchild `<main> › worker-1 › M0 › worker-2 › 子 fleet M0.1`; past 60 characters the middle levels collapse to `…`, keeping the first segment and the last two. A child workspace is placed after the parent workspace (and older siblings) with `reorder-workspace --after`; the title of the workspace holding the daemon tab, `Fleet daemon · M0`, is unique per fleet. The parent workspace keeps a placeholder tab at the original worker position, `worker-1 → 已委派：子 fleet M0（看侧栏「…」）` (delegated: sub-fleet M0, see the sidebar "..."); this is now only the fallback for a slot still running in a plain pane: an escalated / split-approved slot first moves into the fleet's own tmux, and the parent worker position holds the live session of the sub-fleet supervisor (§44 "worker-area mapping", §29 dual attach). The worker tabs of a sub-fleet carry the prefix `fx-M0/worker-1` (decoration, like `fx-`); the Redis label is still `worker-1`. **No nesting**: a sub-fleet never adds splits/tabs inside the parent workspace (except the placeholder tab).
- **Limits (hard program checks, with a reason)**: `max_depth` 2 (the main fleet is level 1, M0 level 2, M0.1 level 3), `max_children` 3 (live sub-fleets per fleet at the same time), `max_agents_total` 12 (whole tree; a sub-fleet's supervisor is the delegated worker and is not counted twice), `child_workers_max` 3 (a job sub-fleet, §58, has no default for the last three; `job_hot_add_max` caps the workers added for jobs). Source: default ← main config `limits` ← `limits:` in the main roster YAML (read fresh on every check, i.e. hot reload; the rest of the roster is still validated as a whole). The number must belong to this fleet (main level `M<n>`, under M0 `M0.<n>`).
- **Supervisor model** (D27): a split sub-fleet's supervisor runs the approve's card, else the supervisor chain's pick (section 44); a named sub-fleet's runs its explicit model, else the chain's pick (section 29); a job's follows section 58 "Model chains".
- **Handover** (`subfleet.spawn_child`, run only in the daemon tick, outside the v2 transaction; callers inside the transaction use `subfleet.request`, which only writes a request file, and `subfleet.check` for a synchronous verdict): the worker must be idle, READY, with no delivery in flight, and its tab present. When the slot runs in the fleet's own tmux (§29 dual attach, §44 worker-area mapping) the parent tab stays in place, no placeholder is opened and nothing is released; the child daemon opens a second client on its first tick; the placeholder / move-surface steps below apply only to plain panes. Parent: open a placeholder tab in front of the worker → back up the worker's AGENTS.md/CLAUDE.md → create the child run/config/private Redis → post the child task to the child bus (goal = original instruction + the approved split plan + the delivery paths inside the sub-fleet's own workspace (§23; the parent path is no longer given); deadline = the parent task's remaining time) → mark the slot `delegated` (`pane` points at the placeholder tab, the original pane is recorded as `delegated_pane`, `delegate_cfg` points at the child config) → release the original pane from the parent registry and write it into the child registry → launch the child daemon. Any failed step rolls back and refuses with a reason. Child daemon, first tick: start the child workers → `adopt` (cross-workspace `move-surface`) the original pane to the top left of the child workspace → write the supervisor rules into the original worker directory → tell it its role changed and ask for `READY` (when `adopt.hook` is true this goes into the supervisor mailbox in the child Redis and the pane's own Stop hook picks it up; otherwise it is typed).
- **Hook redirection**: the hooks of the CLI process in the pane are pinned at launch (parent config, `worker-1`, generation) and the process cannot change them. After `claude_stop` / `notify` enter the v2 branch they first call `subfleet.delegated_route`: if the slot is `delegated` in the parent Redis and the generation matches → redirect to the `supervisor` slot (generation 0) of the child config. On its first tick the sub-fleet binds the same Claude session through the usual `bridge_session → register_slot → startup(READY)`. Late callbacks with a mismatched generation are not redirected.
  - **Hook mailbox redirection (sub-fleets with `adopt.hook: true`)**: the adopted Claude pane no longer receives by typing. The child daemon puts the role change (`nudge`), READY corrections, dispatches, handoffs and so on into the generation-0 `supervisor` mailbox of the child Redis; the pane's Stop hook (pinned by the parent process as `claude-stop <parent config> worker-1 <generation>`) waits in the switch run with that pinned identity and recomputes its destination with `delegated_route` on every round: when the parent slot turns `delegated` it wakes from the parent mailbox and waits on the child mailbox instead, so the first message "role changed + reply READY" is a Stop hook feedback; on takeback (child Redis closed, parent slot no longer `delegated`) it returns to the parent slot's own mailbox, and the §29 takeback handoff delivery is picked up directly by this Stop hook without waiting for asyncRewake. Stop's stopping / end-proof markers are written to the Redis of the routed destination; before taking an item the hook checks once more that the route has not changed, and if it has, it puts the popped entry back into its original mailbox and never delivers across Redis instances. An unreadable route (parent Redis restarting) counts as unchanged; while the sub-fleet is starting or leaving (child supervisor not yet / no longer live, child Redis closed) and the parent still delegates, the hook waits in place for the route to change: it takes nothing, settles nothing and does not fall back early to the parent channel; only operator-queued input makes it yield. The asyncRewake fallback (`claude_rewake`) follows the route the same way; StopFailure's `turnend` is recorded at the destination. PostToolUse (§34) is not redirected: an adopted supervisor does not rotate. Boundary: the pane has no UserPromptSubmit hook (it was written at launch for a worker), so text typed by hand into the child supervisor pane is not registered as a formal request as in §5; it is handled as an unsolicited turn, as on the old typing path. On a sub-fleet `--resume`, a hook supervisor with a delivery in flight is taken back as usual (the pane belongs to the parent; the sub-fleet does not start a next generation); after Escape aborts the old turn, the asyncRewake of its last Stop hands over the replay (only takeback without rotation was tested; the rewake replay handover was not measured on real Claude Code). Named sub-fleets and the M<n> sub-fleets started by escalate use the same mechanism, identical on the cmux / fleetmux backends (hooks and Redis only, no terminal).
- **Takeback**: the sub-fleet writes `result.json` -> the parent tick records the outcome and sends stop -> on its explicit stop the child daemon **only releases, never kills** the adopted supervisor (`handback.json`), retires the child workers as usual and closes Redis -> when the parent sees the child daemon has exited: `adopt` the original pane back in front of the placeholder tab and close the placeholder (tmux slot / section 44 worker-area mapping: the child side only detaches its second client; the parent tab never left and is renamed in place, section 29 `parent_detach_view`), restore the worker rule files, close all tabs of the child workspace (the history page files stay in the child run directory), delete the child registry -> slot `starting`, type a notice that it is a worker again and ask for `READY` (same generation, same session; `register_slot` is idempotent). If the original session is gone (cmux restart etc.) -> the parent starts the next generation at the placeholder with section 17's `respawn`. With outcome `completed`, the parent daemon first copies the output the sub-fleet delivered in its own workspace to the parent path (section 23), then: the output file the parent wants exists and is non-empty -> `returned`, otherwise `failed` (a retry starts a separate `-attempt-<n>`; sub-fleet runs of the same task count toward `--max-attempts`, section 23); while the parent task is still running, the parent supervisor gets a `[Sub-fleet M0 completed|failed]` business message. When the parent task reaches a terminal state, running sub-fleets are stopped and taken back with it. The next generation started when an M<n> sub-fleet is taken back (started right at takeback on the hook channel, or when the original session is gone) uses a dedicated takeback launch prompt, `subfleet.task_return_prompt`: the previous session of this slot was the supervisor of sub-fleet M0, M0 has ended (outcome), the role is over, the parent supervisor collects the result; read AGENTS.md only and reply READY. It no longer falls through to `RuntimeV2.respawn`'s default "resumed after an interruption". No handoff note is written (the `SUBFLEET_RETURN_PROMPT` handoff turn of section 29 named sub-fleets is unchanged).
- **Resume and failure**: on a main-fleet `--resume`, a `delegated` slot is neither taken back nor given a next generation (the pane belongs to the sub-fleet, which resumes afterwards); the case of a whole lost page (`page=False` rebuild) with a `delegated` slot is not handled. Any failed step of `spawn_child` (including a `SystemExit` from `start_redis`) rolls back: the child Redis is closed, the pane goes back to the parent, the slot returns to idle, `failed` is recorded, and the parent daemon is unaffected. Before takeback the pane gets one Escape to abort a turn the adopted session may still be running, so that its late Stop is not taken as the READY of "back to worker"; this is the second place besides §17 "Escape is the only thing accepted as a stop", limited to sub-fleet takeback. The `stop` CLI returns accepted at once; the daemon then waits up to 180 seconds per sub-fleet.
- **A held adopted supervisor**: when the adopted supervisor ends a turn with an API error (StopFailure), the child daemon holds it under "lease/delivery deadline expired" and pauses. `fleetmux resume --config <sub-fleet config>` lifts the pause. On the next tick `RuntimeV2.adopted_held_tick` then reattaches the channel or hands the sub-fleet back. StopFailure sends no event for a supervisor, the `turn_ended` that `release_held` waits for never comes, and the pane belongs to the parent fleet, so the sub-fleet does not start a next generation. The process was in fact alive (the pane sat at its prompt), so instead of a section 43 `claude --resume` restart the channel is reattached: on every tick the child daemon runs `RuntimeV2.adopted_held_tick`; when the supervisor is adopted, on the hook channel, `held`, and this run is not paused (i.e. the operator has resumed): (1) the pane exists and Claude is still running (`has_cli_process`; for a tmux slot, the session's process tree) -> send one Escape (in case the old turn is still running), settle the held delivery as `cancelled` (`execution_stopped`, justified by the operator's resume, as in `resume_run`), return the slot to idle and put the same message text back at the head of the supervisor queue; the pane's rewake fallback (started on StopFailure, follows the route and waits on the child supervisor mailbox, hook timeout 24 hours) or the next Stop hook hands it over; (2) the session is gone -> the child task `failed` ("the adopted supervisor was held and its session is gone after the resume"), the parent fleet takes it back per section 19, the slot starts a next generation and the parent supervisor gets `[Sub-fleet M0 failed]`; a service sub-fleet has no task and stays held for the parent's liveness check. Audit: `adopted.reattached` / `adopted.lost` / `adopted.release_failed`. `claude_stop_failure` routes first and connects second, like the Stop hook, so the `hook.stop_failure` audit and the `turnend` marker land in the child Redis. Tests: `tests/test_subfleet.py::test_held_adopted_supervisor_is_taken_back_in_its_own_session_after_resume` (real Redis, real `claude_stop_failure` and real `claude_rewake`: held + paused -> resume -> Escape, rewake hands over the original message, the supervisor's decision is committed), `..._whose_session_is_gone_hands_the_sub_fleet_back` (child failed -> parent takes back, slot generation 1), `test_stop_failure_of_the_adopted_supervisor_is_recorded_in_the_child`. Boundary: if the rewake fallback is also gone (Claude alive in the pane but no hook waiting, e.g. after the 24-hour hook timeout), nobody takes the requeued message and the supervisor sits idle with mail in its mailbox; no timeout handover to the parent fleet was built; the whole sleep -> resume path was not measured on real Claude Code.
