WezTerm has no such layer: **one window; tab 1 = supervisor (left) + control (bottom-right split); one tab per worker**; `tab_into` / `dock_*` / `place_*` degrade to "put into the matching tab / no-op returning True" without error; with `supports_browser = False`, `arrange()` lays out the workers, puts control into tab 1 (bottom-right split, implemented by the WezTerm backend itself) and returns `None`.
- Layout and history are the only two places where the fleet reads capability bits (`Fleet.announce_history_page()` / `subfleet.follow_jump()` read `supports_browser`); every other call path uses the original interface, and apart from `available()` / `supports_browser` / `nested_tabs` the cmux backend is line-for-line identical to before the move.
- History page: with `supports_browser = False` no browser cell is created; `Fleet.announce_history_page()` prints the `<run>/history.html` path in control and, when a graphical environment exists, opens it once with the system opener (macOS `open` / Linux `xdg-open`; otherwise it only prints); `refresh_history()` calls `reload_browser` only when a browser cell exists; `subfleet.follow_jump()` returns at once when there is no page (and no longer calls cmux every tick). The cmux branches all keep their original calls.
- **Known limitation: WezTerm does not support sub-fleets.** A sub-fleet works by handing the parent fleet's live panes to the sub-fleet's registry (`release` / `adopt`, tmux view); WezTerm runs one private mux server per run, so a sub-fleet can neither see nor take the parent fleet's panes. Therefore under `backend: wezterm` every sub-fleet entry refuses with the same reason (`subfleet.backend_problem`): `subfleet.check` (split subtasks / `subfleet spawn` / named sub-fleet up / escalation approval), the child daemon's `start_adopted` (blocked outright), `start` / `--resume` with a roster containing `subfleets:`, and a roster hot reload that introduces `subfleets:` (the whole roster is rejected, the old roster stays). Sub-fleets run on the default fleetmux backend (one server, several pages, see section 33.6 "Sub-fleets") and on cmux. The refusal text suggests `--backend cmux`.

### 33.5 Small platform changes

`fleetmux/platform.py::keep_awake_prefix()`: macOS `caffeinate -i`; on Linux `systemd-inhibit --what=idle:sleep --why=fleetmux` when `systemd-inhibit` exists and a trial run with `true` succeeds (5 s timeout); otherwise empty (when started from an ssh session or on a headless server, polkit refuses the inhibitor with "Failed to inhibit: Access denied", and a daemon wrapped in it dies at start). The two original `caffeinate` calls in `fleet.py` and the one in `subfleet.py` now call it. `deps.yaml` gates by backend (`when: cmux` / `when: wezterm`, the latter including `wezterm-mux-server`, minimum 20240203), `deps.yaml` also gates `fleetmux` and `fleetmux-web` with `when: fleetmux`; `fleetmux-web` is needed only in a desktop session (`desktop: true`), so on a headless machine a missing `fleetmux-web` is a warning, not a failure. `scripts/check_deps.py` picks the backend with the same section 33.3 rule and skips items that do not belong to it.

**Verification**: `tests/test_backend_interface.py`, `tests/test_backend_selection.py`, `tests/test_platform.py`, `tests/test_check_deps.py`, the `tests/test_wezterm_*.py` files (a fake CLI, and a real mux server when `wezterm-mux-server` is installed), and the `pytest-linux` job of `.github/workflows/tests.yml`.

### 33.6 fleetmux backend (the default on every platform)

Decision (plan A): write our own fixed-layout terminal in Rust that serves only the fleet: `fleetmux`. The code lives in the repo under `rust/`, a separate cargo workspace, MIT licensed; `cargo deny check licenses` admits only MIT / Apache-2.0 style licenses. Its sole design basis is an internal design document; the wire protocol is `rust/PROTOCOL.md` (protocol version 1). This section records only what the fleet side wires up; it does not change the §2 / §3 / §4 baselines, and cmux and WezTerm backend behavior is unchanged.

- **Selection** (section 33.3): fleetmux is the default on every platform when `--backend` is not given; `--backend cmux` and `--backend wezterm` still select the others. The choice is written to `config.json`, and the daemon, the actors, `--resume` and sub-fleets follow it.
- **Structure**: one private `fleetmux server` per run holds every pane's PTY, parser (alacritty_terminal), layout and screen. The fleet sends a JSON-lines protocol of 22 commands (including `server.stats` and five `web.*` commands) over `<state_dir>/fleetmux/<token>/ctl.sock` in the runtime directory; people watch with `fleetmux attach --runtime <dir> [--no-mouse]`. attach is a separate client; several can be open at once, and closing them does not affect the agents. A server that answers `hello` with `pages: 1` also takes four `page.*` commands (26 in total). The backend starts the server as `fleetmux server --runtime <dir> --session <name> --daemonize`. When `FLEETMUX_THEME` names a palette file, it is passed as `--theme FILE` (section 39.1).
- **`fleetmux tabs`**: `fleetmux tabs [--config C] [--json]` lists every tab of a running fleet: page, group, title, type, pid and pane id. A root config lists every page of the fleet tree; a sub-fleet config lists its own page; a join stub resolves to its service sub-fleet or to its root. `fleetmux tabs read <pane id or title prefix> [--lines N]` prints the last N text rows of one tab (default 50); several matching tabs are refused. The command is read-only: it sends only `hello`, `page.list`, `pane.list` and `pane.read`, never starts a server and never writes to a pane. Exit codes: 0 ok, 2 usage or no single matching tab, 3 the server cannot be read.
  - Runtime directory 0700, socket 0600; the peer uid must match.
  - The runtime directory also holds `server.json`, `panes.json`, `layout.json`, `start.lock`, `server.log`. After the server shuts down, `layout.json`, `start.lock` and `server.log` remain (R7).
  - `start.lock` belongs to the starter, i.e. the lock the Python backend takes before starting the server. The server process itself does not block on this lock (fix-m0-2). On the normal path the starter holds the lock and then starts the server, and on this path atomic publication of the socket guarantees a single instance. This **does not mean** every race is gone: concurrent starts without the starter lock while a stale socket exists still carry a recorded TOCTOU residual risk; the hard-link publication path on Linux is also not yet verified.
- **Python backend**: `fleetmux/backends/fleetmux.py` implements all 39 `METHODS`, plus `meta_path` / `_meta` / `_save_meta` / `_tag`. The registry is `<state_dir>/<session>.fleetmux.json`, deliberately named differently from the cmux / WezTerm `<session>.json`.
  - Capability bits: `supports_browser = True`, meaning "has a history cell", not a browser; `nested_tabs = True`.
  - Availability: `$FLEETMUX_BIN` or `fleetmux` on PATH. `fleetmux --version` must print `fleetmux <x.y.z> (protocol 1)` with a version ≥ `programs.fleetmux.min` in `deps.yaml` (`when: fleetmux`); otherwise it refuses before starting.
- **Layout**: three fixed groups. Top left is the supervisor group; bottom left is `history` (`python -m fleetmux.history_view <run>`), `control` and the applet tabs (§54); the right side is the worker group. On rotation the successor is inserted back at the original tab position.
  - Tab style: tabs sit on their group's border row, which keeps the content background. Only the selected tab has a block: a dark grey pill with one padding column on each side. In the focused group the selected tab is white bold text on that pill; in an unfocused group the selected tab is grey text on the same pill. Unselected tabs are plain grey text with no background. Neighbouring tabs of one group are separated by a thin dark grey vertical line. The focused group's border is cyan and bold; other borders are grey. Only a click on a tab selects it. Tab colors use only the 16 ANSI colors, so each terminal shows its own palette. A supervisor or worker tab label shows at most 24 title columns. A lower-group tab in the fixed slots shows at most 15 columns; a lower-group titled block, such as an applet tab, shows at most 24 columns. With many tabs the equal share of the row width still truncates a title.
  - Dragging: drag the vertical divider to change the left/right ratio; drag the left column's horizontal divider to change the top/bottom ratio; the horizontal divider has a visible drag handle. Both ratios are clamped to 10%–90% and stored in `layout.json`.
  - Keys (prefix `C-b`), mouse, paging, copy, multiple viewers and the 256-color downgrade: see `PROTOCOL.md` §10–§14.
  - GUI scrollbar: the GUI window draws one scrollbar per visible cell, because the server owns the scrollback. A viewer that sends the handshake key `scroll` receives frame 0x86 with each pane's offset and total. Dragging or clicking the bar sends frame 0x05 with the new offset, and the next 0x86 is authoritative. The bar is invisible at rest. It appears when that pane's offset changes, while it is dragged, or while the pointer is over the right 12 px of the cell. It fades out about one second after the last activity, never during a drag. An old server never sends 0x86, so the window then shows no scrollbar.
  - Prefix + `o` in `fleetmux attach` first shows the history page in the client's own `fleetmux-web` window. The window is used only when the client has a display and a `fleetmux-web` of the same version. When the client runs on another host, the server sends the page and the client loads its own copy (`page/history.html`, mode 0600). Without a window the system opener is used (`open` / `xdg-open`), and last the path is shown. Why a window could not be used is shown only on that client's status line. The window is stopped when the client exits or detaches.
- **`start` wiring**: when a live root fleet already runs on this machine, `start` attaches to it (§19 "One root fleet per machine"): it prints one line naming the root and the `fleetmux task` command, and opens no view. Otherwise, with the fleetmux backend, `start` prints one line `fleetmux attach --runtime <dir>` in the terminal. `start --attach` execs that command after start completes; with other backends, `--attach` refuses with exit 2.
- **Automatic view** (`fleet.auto_view`): without `--attach` / `--gui` / `--no-view`, a fleetmux `start` opens a view for the person who ran it. Conditions, in order: stdin and stdout are TTYs (`interactive_terminal`); no ancestor process is a `fleetmux server` (`inside_fleetmux_pane`: the start runs in a sidebar bash or other pane, which already shows the server); no `fleetmux gui` / `fleetmux attach` is open on the same runtime (`viewer_running`). If they hold: a desktop with `fleetmux-web` and no `SSH_CONNECTION` gets `fleetmux gui` started in the background in a new session (stdin from /dev/null, output to `<run>/gui.log`) and `start` returns; otherwise `start` execs `fleetmux attach`. The automatic choice never refuses: a missing window half falls back to attach instead of the exit 2 of an explicit `--gui`. `--resume` returns before this point and opens nothing. The test suite pins `interactive_terminal` to false (`tests/conftest.py`). Bare `fleetmux` (section 50) is not this automatic view: it opens the root fleet's window from a fleetmux pane and without a terminal too. With no root fleet it starts the `bare` root fleet with stdin from /dev/null, so this automatic view stays off for that `start`; the new root daemon's `gui_keep` opens the window, bare waits for it (`bare.window_wait_s`) and raises it, and opens it itself only when that wait runs out. The host-terminal rule of bare `fleetmux` (`bare.host_terminal_env`, section 50 item 2) is not part of this automatic view: in a host terminal on a desktop `start` still opens the window.
  - On macOS the automatic view raises the new window: `auto_view` waits up to 5 seconds for the `fleetmux-web` child of `fleetmux gui` and brings it to the front through System Events. A failed raise leaves the window where it is.
  - `start` on a desktop with `fleetmux-web` prints `Watch it in a window: fleetmux gui --runtime <dir>` before the `fleetmux attach` line. `start --gui` execs `fleetmux gui --runtime <dir>` after start finishes. It refuses with exit 2 before provisioning when given together with `--attach`, when the backend is not fleetmux, or when there is no desktop. A missing or mismatched `fleetmux-web` is not refused by `start`; `fleetmux gui` itself exits 4 for it. When a window is already open on the runtime, `start --gui` prints that and opens no second window.
- **Window keep-open** (`fleetmux/gui_keep.py`, `fleet.yaml#gui`): a root fleet's window stays on the desktop, and the fleet does this itself (§1): no launchd / cron item opens or reopens it. Every `gui.check_s` seconds (default 30) the root daemon looks for a `fleetmux gui --runtime <its runtime>` process (`ps`, runtime compared by real path; an `attach` does not count). The process is the window: it exits when the window is closed. When there is none, the daemon starts one the way `auto_view` does (new session, stdin from /dev/null, output to `<run>/gui.log`). This also covers a fleet started over ssh or by a scheduler: the first check opens the window, so `start` over ssh needs no extra step.
  - macOS: a process started from an ssh session can open a window on the desktop of the same user when that user is logged in at the console, so the daemon starts `fleetmux gui` directly (no `open`, no `launchctl asuser`, which needs root from ssh). The desktop exists when `/dev/console` is owned by the daemon's user; owned by root (nobody logged in) or by another user: no window, one `gui.no_desktop` log line, checked again every `check_s`.
  - Linux: the daemon's `DISPLAY` / `WAYLAND_DISPLAY`; without them, a `wayland-*` socket in `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` (else `/run/user/<uid>`), passed to the window as `WAYLAND_DISPLAY` / `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`. An X11-only session that the daemon's environment does not name is not found: one `gui.no_desktop` line.
  - One window: nothing opens while a matching process runs, or while the window this daemon launched is still running (it may not be listed yet). A daemon restart finds the old window by `ps`.
  - Failures: an exec error or a non-zero exit of `fleetmux gui` (4 = cannot open the window, see `gui.log`) logs `gui.open_failed` and waits `check_s`, then twice as long each time, at most `gui.backoff_max_s` (default 600); a window that opens resets it. Exit 0 (window closed) reopens on the next check. A missing `fleetmux` / `fleetmux-web` logs `gui.unavailable` once.
  - Not raised: a reopened window does not take the focus.
  - Off: `gui.keep_open: false` in the private `~/.fleetmux/fleet.yaml` (read on every check), or `start --no-view` for that run (config.json `gui_keep_open: false`). Sub-fleets never open a window: they show in the root's sidebar. `start --gui` does not exec a second window when one is already open on the runtime.
- **Install**:
  - From source: `cd rust && cargo install --path crates/app` (toolchain per `rust/rust-toolchain.toml`);
  - Prebuilt: `scripts/install_fleetmux.sh` downloads GitHub release `$FLEETMUX_VERSION` (set by `scripts/install.sh` to the release it installs) or else `v<min>`, and installs nothing if the sha256 does not match. `.github/workflows/release.yml` builds `fleetmux` and `fleetmux-web` for four targets.
- **OSC 52 in the outer terminal**: copy sends OSC 52 to the outer terminal and also uses `pbcopy`, `wl-copy` or `xclip` on the host. Whether the outer terminal accepts OSC 52 depends on that terminal.
- **Crash recovery order**: after the server dies (e.g. `kill -9`), **first** `fleetmux reap --runtime <dir>`, **then** `fleetmux start --resume`. The first spawn of a new server rewrites `panes.json`, after which reap can no longer find the old process groups. Demos or verification may only be done on a dedicated test run; never kill / reap a server the user is using or another fleet.
- **Sub-fleets**: no longer refused. The whole tree shares the root fleet's server, one page per fleet (the fixed layout is unchanged, just repeated); `subfleet.backend_problem` refuses only WezTerm (original wording unchanged). A runtime capability gate `subfleet.pages_problem` is added: when the server's `hello` reply lacks `pages: 1` (an M3 server), `check()` (split / `subfleet spawn` / named up / escalation approval) and the child daemon's `start_adopted` refuse and state the reason; when no server is running it does not judge (the version belongs to the root that starts the server). Binding keys, view hooks, the read-only record page and the sidebar card's `page` are in section 29 "View hooks on the fleetmux backend". A sub-fleet's manager never starts or stops the server (`end_server` sends nothing); only the root starts it, and the root closes it last. `deps.yaml` requires fleetmux 0.2.0 or newer. A server without page support (no `pages: 1`) is refused for sub-fleets at run time by `subfleet.pages_problem`, not by the version floor.
- **Sidebar bash shells: a clean page of their own, mapped into the main fleet's worker area and managed by the main fleet**: any new bash opened from the fleetmux sidebar is shown as a clean bash and is also mapped into the main fleet's worker area, under the main fleet's management. Both hold at once: the display is the bash's own page, the management is the root page's tab. A bash opened by double-clicking blank space / the top-bar `+` / `^b c` is started by the server as a tab at the end of the **root page**'s `workers` group (title `bash-<n>`; the pane record's page is the root page, also when a child page was current). It is shown at once on its own page `_bash-<pane id>`, whose layout holds the same pane zoomed: the shell fills the content area, no fleet pane is drawn, and the root page's tabs, focus and zoom are not touched. Clicking a sidebar bash card shows that page again; clicking the root fleet card brings the root page back as it was, with the bash as a live tab of its worker area (clicking that tab shows the shell there and stays on the root page; a double click zooms it there as for any tab). Only the root layout decides the pane record's group / index / focus (`PaneStore::sync_page_layout`); a PTY shown by two layouts takes the current page's rectangle. A wrong reading to avoid: "mapped into the worker area" never means "moved onto the main fleet's page" — opening a bash must not show the supervisor or the other workers. The root daemon's per-second tick (`fleetmux_sidebar.tick` → `adopt_shells`) and `arrange` take the root page's `type: shell` panes into the registry (`type: shell`, `label` = title), move any that are not in the workers group into it and delete vanished ones from the registry; when the registered set differs from what this daemon last listed, the history page is rewritten (`history_slots` lists them at the end via `shell_slots()`, slot = pane id; `start_all` writes the history page before `arrange` adopts, so the first tick always rewrites it once); `arrange` orders them after the roster workers. A bash opened while no fleet runs still lands on the root page and is adopted when the next root fleet starts. It is **not a worker**: it takes no slot, receives no dispatch, does not count toward `--max-busy`, is not in the roster, and a sub-fleet's manager never adopts it (`adopt_shells` returns at once when bound to a page). The card's `×` close semantics are unchanged (it asks first when busy). Implementation: Rust `server/view.rs` (`new_bash` / `show_bash` / `fleet_view` / `sync_bash`, which closes a bash page whose shell is gone), `server/handlers.rs` (`bash_page`), `render/draw.rs` (no zoom / page on the status line of a bash page); Python `backends/fleetmux.py` (`adopt_shells` / `shell_slots` / `arrange` / `_kill_idle_shell`, which deletes the registration on close), `fleetmux_sidebar.py`, `fleet.history_slots`.
- **Sidebar bash pages close with the fleet**: a bash (`type: shell`; a tab in the root page's worker area, registered by the root fleet but not a worker) is closed together with the root fleet (`close_panes` / `close_all`, the same path as sidebar × ↺ and lifecycle close) as long as the shell sits at its prompt (pane record `fg_pgid` = `pgid`), and is deleted from the registry; one with a foreground program running, or on an old server that does not report `fg_pgid`, is left in place together with the server, and the close result line names the pages left; when no page remains, `close_all` stops the server as usual. When the server stays because of a busy bash page, the daemon no longer pushes the sidebar, so the closer itself moves this run's card out of running / paused / stopping (a resumable run stopped at its deadline writes a `closed` card and kv `restorable` as on the sidebar × path, showing `到点停 · 可恢复` (stopped at deadline, resumable) and ↺; others such as `fleet.sh stop` write `stopped` / `已停止` (stopped), with no × and no ↺; sub-fleet cards write `已收回 · 随根关闭` (reclaimed, closed with the root)); otherwise idle reclaim (§13.4) would never let the server go. Reclaiming a sub-fleet never touches bash pages. `pane.kill` (the bash card's ×, the same escalation path as a forced `server.shutdown`) also sends the same signal to the process group of the PTY's foreground job, so a foreground job that ignores SIGHUP is no longer left orphaned; background jobs (`cmd &`), a shell exiting by itself and `fleetmux reap` after a crash are excluded (PROTOCOL.md §4.13).
- **Root card close**: the root daemon pushes the fleet sidebar into the server kv `sidebar` once per tick. The value lists its own run first and then every sub-fleet in pre-order, at most 16 cards; only the root daemon pushes it. The root card has a close button. A click on it, or `^b X`, opens a confirmation box. Pressing `y` writes kv `stop_request` with state `requested`; any other key, a paste or a click outside the confirm button closes the box and writes nothing. The root daemon reads the request in its next tick and closes the run resumably through lifecycle close. It answers `accepted`, or `refused` when the run has no lifecycle. The closed run keeps its card with state `closed`, a grey dot and a restore button, and its entry is added to kv `restorable`. When kv `sidebar` lists no running, paused or stopping card, the server waits for 10 seconds with no pane and no viewer and then exits through the normal shutdown path; any pane or viewer resets that count. A server without kv `sidebar` never exits this way.
- **Restore history**: a run is listed as a grey `closed` card when its `result.json` says `resumable: true` and it was closed from the sidebar (`closed_via: fleetmux-sidebar`) or stopped by a lifecycle timeout (`closed_by: timeout`). The run must use the fleetmux backend with this window's session and state dir, and its daemon must be gone. The list is newest `closed_at` first, at most 16 cards (`MAX_CARDS`). The same scan (`fleetmux_history.restorable_runs`) feeds three writers: `gui` when no daemon runs, the root daemon's sidebar push, and the closer. They write kv `restorable` and kv `sidebar` only when the value changed. With no history and no daemon, `gui` prints one line and exits 1 without starting a server. While any card is still running, a restore from a grey card only shows a status line asking to close the running fleet first.
- **Limitations**:
  - No browser inside the terminal. On a desktop, `fleetmux-web` opens a native window (`fleetmux gui`). `browser_goto`, `browser_url` and `reload_browser` send `web.goto`, `web.url` and `web.reload` to the server. The server relays them to the `fleetmux-web` window of every GUI viewer and waits a bounded time for the replies. `browser_goto` is true only when at least one window loaded the URL. Without a window, `browser_url` returns the registered URL. The window only loads pages that its allow-list admits. `fleetmux web` finds `fleetmux-web` beside the `fleetmux` binary, then on `PATH`, then through `FLEETMUX_WEB_BIN`, refuses a different version, and execs it. A plain `fleetmux attach` has no browser.
- **Verification scope**:
  - `scripts/fleetmux_gate.sh` is the delivery gate. Its phases are `p0`, `a`, `m0`, `m12`, `m25`, `m26`, `m3` and `m4`. Each phase runs the cargo tests and the matching pytest subset.
  - The `m12` and later gates also run the ignored cargo tests (perf, real CLI replay).
  - `fleetmux server --record DIR` writes the raw output of each pane to `DIR/<pane id>.rec`. These recordings are the source of the replay fixtures in `rust/fixtures/recordings/`. Without the flag nothing is recorded.

### 33.7 Claude input box: sends are refused

The fleet never types into a Claude Code input box (section 48). `send_prompt_to_idle` on any backend routes a pane registered with `cli_type=claude` to `claude_input.send`. That call pastes nothing, sends no key, sets `last_error` and returns False. Non-Claude panes keep the paste path. `claude_input.visible_editor` remains for read-only screen checks.

- There is no paste, lock or verification path for Claude panes. Configs that would need the input box are refused by `runtime_v2.typed_claude_problem`: `clear_after_result` true, `claude_hook_delivery` false, a Claude supervisor without a hook mailbox, and an adopted Claude pane without `adopt.hook`. Claude agents take deliveries through the Stop-hook mailbox and the first prompt as a CLI argument.
- On refusal `last_error` is one fixed text that starts with "Claude input held: fleet never types into a Claude Code input box" and ends with "nothing pasted, no key sent". Callers must treat False as not sent and must not add an Enter.

## 34. Mid-turn rotation (daemon probe + forced auto-handoff prompt)

Requirement: every worker CLI must be able to hand off mid-turn; it is basic functionality. The daemon runs a probe and forces the auto-handoff prompt while a turn is running. Rotation between turns is section 32 `idle_rotate`.

- **Probe**: a separate thread inside the daemon; loop interval = max(1 s, time taken to check all busy slots this round), single-threaded and sequential, never overlapping or piling up. It looks only at busy slots (busy, lease received, the in-flight message not itself a handoff delivery); idle slots still belong to section 32 `idle_rotate`. The same probe also serves the section 44 split check of a long busy turn. Each round it first `stat`s the slot's current-generation session file (mtime, size) and skips it if unchanged; if changed it reads only the tail (<=64KB), never the whole file.
- **Usage measure**: pi slots use pi's own algorithm: `calculateContextTokens` plus trailing messages not yet counted by a model call, estimated as `ceil(chars/4)` (UTF-16 length); the probe copies this measure rather than inventing its own. After a compaction and before the next model response, usage is unknown (not 0; the last value read is kept). Claude slots use the existing `claude_transcript_usage`. Codex slots use `midturn.codex_rollout_usage` (the latest `last_token_usage` in the rollout tail). opencode and dsh slots have no usage reader and get no mid-turn handoff; the daemon audits `midturn.no_usage` once per generation and shows a notice card.
- **Mid-turn line**: settings `midturn_ratio`, `midturn_retry` and `midturn_max_splits`; their defaults are in `fleet.yaml#handoff` (section 22; shipped 0.8, 300 s and 3). A roster `handoff:` entry per CLI or CLI:model may set `midturn_ratio`; otherwise the config `handoff` table, then the config top level, then the block. Mid-turn rotation runs only under transport v2. When usage >= `midturn_ratio` x window and this generation has not been nudged yet, the daemon bypasses the delivery gate and hands the slot a fixed one-line prompt that carries an `fx-<16hex>` id (the `midturn_id`), once per generation. Codex and pi slots get the prompt typed into the pane. A Claude slot gets it from its PostToolUse hook (`claude-posttool`), taken once after the next tool call; nothing is typed into a Claude input box (section 48). Every role prompt explains the mid-turn prompt, so a worker does not refuse it as a plan change.
- **Turn settlement**: when that turn ends with the message carrying the mid-turn mark and no valid formal report → record `handoff_split`: not complete, not BLOCKED, no attempt counted; the continuation message goes to the head of the queue (FIFO head), rotation follows the original §4 flow (the model's draft if the model edited it, otherwise the skeleton), and the successor generation continues the same dispatch.
- **Fallback**: `midturn_retry` seconds after the prompt with the turn not ended → send it once more; that long again still not ended → hand over to the existing `Watchdog.rotate`, which kills the window, does a machine-skeleton handoff + starts a new generation in place + continues.
- **Limit**: one dispatch is split at most `midturn_max_splits` times (`midturn_splits` is counted on the message); past that it is not split again but turned BLOCKED with `请拆小` (please split it smaller) for the supervisor / service to handle.
- **Clock**: a service request's timeout clock pauses during mid-turn rotation; when the split chain ends (settled, turned BLOCKED, canceled) the paused duration is added back on resume; the task's overall `hours` budget is unaffected, and the hard budget does not pause (§17 / §23).
- **Three bypasses**: the mechanism must get past three gates that would otherwise block mid-turn input — the pi extension's `input` hook lets mid-turn prompts with the `fx-` prefix through (it would otherwise swallow them or turn them into operator_input); the Claude supervisor's `UserPromptSubmit` hook lets registered mid-turn prompts through; Codex `codex_queue.bound` is relaxed to "a second user message equal to the registered mid-turn prompt still counts as this generation".

**Known boundary**: the probe can see tool results that are appended but not yet sent to the model (the pi measure estimates them as ceil(chars/4)), but the CLI checks for self-compaction within milliseconds of appending a tool result, faster than the 1-second poll -- a single large batch of tool results can push usage from below the mid-turn line straight past the CLI's self-compaction point in one step, before the probe can send its prompt. With a 200k window the 0.8 line leaves little room before the CLI's own compaction, so finishing the handoff first is not guaranteed.

**Verification**: `tests/test_midturn_v2.py`, `tests/test_midturn_claude_hook.py`, `tests/test_midturn_limits.py`; end-to-end smoke `scripts/smoke_midturn.sh`. The code is in `fleetmux/midturn.py` and `Fleet.midturn_apply`.

## 35. Restart the session when the link breaks

Requirement: "communication lost, execution state unconfirmed; waiting for reconciliation" is a disconnection; on detecting a broken link the fleet should restart that session according to the actual situation.

- **First tell whether it is a broken link**: bridge faults are of two kinds. One only restates a slot hold the daemon has already made (`daemon holds execution` / `settlement unconfirmed/held` / `recovered PEL may have executed` / a manual-input collision / opencode hold / dsh hold, `runtime_v2.BRIDGE_HELD_REASONS`); the pane shows a "held by the fleet" line with the reason the daemon wrote on the message, and the continuation limit shows a "continuation limit reached, waiting for the supervisor to split it" line; when the daemon gets such a fault and the slot is already held, it only audits, without holding the slot a second time or pausing the whole fleet. Everything else is a broken link (the bridge's own Redis error, child process exit, the lease cannot be renewed); the pane shows a "communication lost; the fleet will restart this session automatically" line.
- **How the daemon detects a broken link**: (1) busy slot: the lease `lease_until` has passed while the dispatch `deadline_at` has not (the bridge stopped renewing) -> broken link; both passed is the original timeout. (2) idle slot: the bridge's lease-renewal thread writes a heartbeat key `<prefix>:hb:<slot>` each round (value = generation, TTL 3 periods); a heartbeat of this generation that appeared and then vanished -> broken link. A bridge that never wrote a heartbeat (old code) is not judged. (3) a link-type fault reported by the bridge itself. All three go to `link_lost`: hold only this slot (`pause=False`) and mark `link_lost=1`. This applies only to bridge slots (pi, opencode, dsh); Claude / Codex leases are renewed by the daemon and are excluded; the advisor slot is still restarted in place per section 27.
- **Restart**: each watchdog round first handles slots with `link_lost=1`, reusing the frozen-rotation `Watchdog.rotate`: save the screen, skeleton handoff note + terminal snapshot, start the next generation, kill the old session, `confirm_retired` cancels the original dispatch. The original dispatch is **not rerun automatically** (v2 principle unchanged): in task mode the supervisor receives "FROZEN … bridge link lost, canceled and not rerun; check the workspace before dispatching again", in service mode the request is recorded as stalled; a dispatch carrying the mid-turn rotation mark continues per §34. No restart while paused; it happens after resume.
- **After the machine slept** (laptop lid closed): the bridges were suspended with the daemon and their heartbeat keys expired during the gap, which is not a lost link. When a tick finds the previous one more than two bridge renew periods ago (period = `min(10, lease_ms/3000)` s), `RuntimeV2.daemon_gap` gives the bridges three periods (`hb_grace_until`) to beat again before `heartbeat_check` judges ②; a bridge still silent after that is a lost link as before. During the same wait the lease check does not call an in-flight slot lost for a lease that ran out during the gap. The gap is measured at the start of `Fleet.tick` (`RuntimeV2.gap_tick`), before any of its parts judges a heartbeat, and once per loop pass. Every other wait is in awake time (next item), so a startup that began just before the lid closed and an applet that beat just before it are judged on the time the machine was awake. If a startup still timed out (the session needed longer after the wake), the slot is `dead` and a service run is paused as before, but in a service run the slot keeps a `startup_timeout` mark (generation, and `fx:service.at` when this timeout set the pause). A clean READY of that same generation then revives the slot through the normal READY path instead of `startup.duplicate`, audits `startup.late_ready`, and lifts the pause through `pause_owner_resume` (`execution.auto_resumed`; never past a brake or budget line) only while `fx:service.at` is unchanged, so a pause set earlier or later by someone else stays. A busy daemon can tick every two to three periods (§47): only the first of such consecutive gaps starts the wait, while a gap longer than three periods always does; a daemon that always ticks slower than three periods never judges ②.
- **Every deadline is in awake time** (`fleetmux/awake.py`): awake time = Unix time minus the time the machine slept. A closed lid (also on battery, where `caffeinate -i` does not hold the machine awake) stops the fleet, so that time is nobody's wait. Patching each deadline after a wake left the next one (startup, applet heartbeat, then escalate / `decision_wait_s`, worker idle reclaim, the SDK's own Redis-down exit); now one clock serves all of them. One deliberate exception: the Claude hook keepalive runs on a clock that counts sleep, because Claude Code times its hooks on wall time (section 68).
  - **Measure**: the sleep is the wall delta minus the monotonic delta. Python's `time.monotonic()` stops while the machine sleeps: on macOS it is `mach_absolute_time()` = `CLOCK_UPTIME_RAW` ("does not increment while the system is asleep", clock_gettime(3)); on Linux it is `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` ("does not count time that the system is suspended"; `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` does, clock_gettime(2)). The total therefore holds every sleep, including the short dark wakes of a closed laptop, and any step of the wall clock. Time the machine is awake counts, also a dark wake in which the daemon ran, and also a daemon that was stuck while the machine was awake.
  - **Publish**: the daemon owns the fleet's clock. It folds the measured sleep into `slept_s` at the start of every `Fleet.tick` (`awake.tick`, also once when `run` starts, so a restarted daemon goes on from the stored total and leaves out the sleep while it was down) and writes `fx:clock` (contracts.md). Any other process on the same boot (the applet SDK, a CLI, a launcher, the Claude hook, the parent of a named sub-fleet) reads it and measures the sleep since the published point itself (`awake.of(r)`), so there is no lag between a wake and the daemon's next tick. The SDK reads it on start and on every heartbeat; while the host Redis is down it keeps measuring from its last read (or from its start) with the same rule.
  - **Unit**: every time that fleetmux writes and a deadline or age check reads back is awake seconds: `startup_deadline`, `profile_ready_deadline`, request `created` / `clock_paused_at`, applet `started_at`, applet lease and op times (`requested_at`, `granted_at`, `active_at`, `expires_at`, op `created`), `host.draining` `at` / `deadline_at`, `fx:applet:ctl` `at`, the advisor answer deadline, the empty-reply retry, the advisor restart gap, the network startup-retry backoff, the clear deadline, the StopFailure backoff, the supervisor yield-wait deadline, a named sub-fleet's `drain_since`, and the task `hours` budget: the task `deadline`, `report_wait_at` (§46), the waiting horizon `until`, the remaining time a task-mode split hands its child, `start --resume --extend-hours`, the lifecycle `hard_timeout_min` and the brake card's `max_hours` (the last two compare their Unix start stamps through `awake.between_wall`). Task-mode bus messages no longer carry the task deadline as their bus `deadline_at` (`NO_DEADLINE_MS`, as service requests): the tick ends the task on the awake budget (`budget_exhausted`), and a wall-clock bus deadline would hold the slot after a sleep longer than the time left. `fleetmux status` shows the budget as `budget_remaining_s` and `budget_used_s` (awake seconds); the waiting line shows its horizon as the clock time it falls on if the machine stays awake. Awake seconds equal Unix seconds until the first sleep after the fleet's clock was created, so stored values from before stay valid; they drift behind Unix time by the total slept, so they are not shown as clock times. Times written outside fleetmux stay Unix seconds and are converted when read (`awake.from_wall`, which leaves out the sleeps logged after the time): the applet's `hb_at` and file mtimes (worker idle reclaim). Waits inside one process use `awake.mono()`.
  - **Not converted (open)**: the bus lease and message deadlines (`lease_until`, `deadline_at`, ms), shared with the bridges (Python, TypeScript) and Lua; the bridge heartbeat key TTL (held by the wake grace above); `handoff_deadline` and the mid-turn pause, which feed those message deadlines; the one-hour bus deadline of a message with no request and no task budget; the watchdog stall windows (floored by the daemon gap, §24). `tests/test_awake_scan.py` lists every remaining wall-clock deadline shape with its reason and fails on a new one.
  - **Applet exits after a wake**: the applet's own process can break on the wake (a health check times out, an ssh child was stopped by the sleep, a connection drops). A crash within `applet_host.WAKE_GRACE_BEATS` (2) heartbeat periods of awake time after the last measured sleep (`awake.since_wake`) is restarted and logged `applet.exit_after_wake`, not counted toward `crash_max`; the crash window itself is awake time. Three crashes with no sleep in between still make a crashloop.
  - **For an operator**: a laptop that slept for hours wakes with every wait where it was: no startup timeout, no applet stall or Redis-down exit, no `no_decision`, no expired lease, no reclaimed worker, no timed-out request, no task budget used up overnight. Awake time still runs out as before. Times in `fleetmux applet` lease records are awake seconds; `remaining_s()` and `fx:clock` convert them.
- **A startup that fails on the network is retried, not blocked**: a startup event with `failed` true, not aborted, not a wall, whose error is a transport error (`runtime_v2.startup_offline`: `fetch failed`, `ECONNRESET`, `ENOTFOUND`, `EAI_AGAIN`, `socket hang up` ...) leaves the slot `dead` without pausing the run and schedules its next generation (`Fleet.schedule_startup_retry`, backoff 30 / 60 / 120 / 300 s, the last repeating). `Fleet.startup_retry_tick` starts it through `wall_rotate` when due, the run is not paused and the network answers (`Fleet.network_up`: a TCP connect to any of `NETWORK_PROBES`); while it does not, the retry is pushed back 30 s with no attempt spent and no new generation, so the short dark wakes of a closed laptop, during which the daemon still ticks, use up nothing. READY resets the count. After 24 failures with the network up the old stop applies (`blocked`, service run paused). Any other startup failure blocks as before.
- **No restart while offline**: `Watchdog.link_restart` asks `Fleet.network_up` first (an offline answer is reused for 10 s). While the network does not answer, the slot stays held with `link_offline=1` (one audit `link.restart_waits_network`), no restart is counted and no generation is started; the restart runs on the first round the network answers. Together with the startup retry above, every new generation the daemon starts after a wake waits for the network.
- **Limit**: at most 3 restarts of one slot within 30 minutes (`watchdog.LINK_MAX_RESTARTS / LINK_WINDOW_S`); a further break holds the slot and pauses dispatch, reporting "check Redis / the bridge before resuming"; a failed restart is marked `link_lost=failed` and is not retried every round.

## 36. The daemon and the supervisor watch each other

Requirement: when the daemon exits and the supervisor finds it cannot talk to the daemon, the supervisor brings the daemon back up; conversely, when the supervisor is stuck, the daemon brings the supervisor back. The two heartbeat each other; whoever finds the other gone checks the logs and how long it was stuck, and resumes from the logs. When the supervisor sees no heartbeat for 2 minutes, it runs a command from its loop, and that command is written in AGENTS.md. Per section 1, the whole mechanism lives inside the fleet: no crontab / launchd, no extra resident process.

- **The supervisor watches the daemon (pi supervisor)**: the fleet's own pi extension (`pi_extension_v2.ts`, already installed in the supervisor session) starts a timer after READY and sends `daemon_beat` through its own bridge every `check_interval_s`. The bridge (`agent_bridge.daemon_beat`) reads Redis and `<run>/daemon.lock` directly, not through the daemon, so it still answers when the daemon is dead and the bridge has already faulted for lack of settlement. Fresh heartbeat → do nothing (no turn, no tokens spent); heartbeat older than `stale_after_s` → only then probe daemon.lock (probing takes the lock for an instant, and a daemon that is starting could fail to start if it hits it, so the probe runs only on a suspected outage), and use `runtime_v2.daemon_outage` to take facts from the logs: last heartbeat, the daemon's last audit entry (within 2 s after the heartbeat, skipping `renewed / received / slot_* / turn.* / hook.*`, which bridges / hooks keep writing), the number of events in the stream that the daemon's consumer group has not fetched / acked, held / expired slots. No daemon holding the lock, run not finished (no result.json; in task mode the task still running), session idle, not in a dispatch turn, and `realert_after_s` passed since the last alert → the extension starts **one** supervisor turn with `sendMessage(customType fleet-daemon-watchdog, triggerTurn)` whose body is one line of `[Daemon watchdog] …` facts. This turn is a manual turn (not a dispatch; it cannot deliver a fleet result). Daemon alive but heartbeat stopped (hung) → no turn, nothing killed. The decision is the pure function `daemonWatchdog`, with the contract test `tests/test_pi_daemon_watch.mjs`.
- **The restart command is in AGENTS.md**: the v2 main fleet's supervisor AGENTS.md ends with a generated "Daemon down" section (`fleet.daemon_down_section`) that holds this run's verbatim, paste-ready command: `env FLEETMUX_HOME=... FLEETMUX_STATE=... FLEETMUX_CONFIG=... CMUX_BIN=... <python> <fleet.py> start --allow-development-v2 --resume <run>` (the environment comes from the locations pinned in config.json; `private_config: none` is not written), plus `status` and `logs ... | tail -n 30` with the same prefix. Steps: check the logs and `daemon.heartbeat_age_s` -> stop if the heartbeat has recovered -> first write one line in PROGRESS.md (the resumed daemon will rotate the current supervisor to the next generation within seconds) -> when the run has `channels:` and is not a sub-fleet, tell the user once through `channel send --event daemon_down` (a failed send is not retried and never blocks the restart) -> run the restart command once (with a live daemon it only prints nothing to resume) -> check the heartbeat again. Forbidden: running it a second time for the same outage, starting the daemon any other way, stopping / killing any process, tab or Redis; if the command fails (e.g. sandbox refusal), do not retry, but hand the command and the error to a human in the reply. This section is the only exception to "do not call the fleet CLI".
- **Briefing after resume**: `resume_run` computes the same `daemon_outage` before clearing slots: the `daemon.resumed` audit carries `down_s / last_event / pending_events / held`, and the task-mode `[Resumed task …]` briefing gains a line "Daemon outage (from the logs): last heartbeat …, down N s …; M events were waiting and are processed now; held/expired deliveries found: …". A manual `start --resume` gets the same line.
- **The daemon watches the supervisor**: ① stuck mid-turn (in flight) → §24 watchdog frozen → `rotate` (existing; applies to the supervisor slot too). ② the pi supervisor process is gone → the bridge heartbeat vanishes → §35 `link_lost` → `link_restart` → `rotate` (existing). ③ **New**: after a Claude / Codex supervisor CLI exits, only a shell is left in its tab (`auto_close=False`), with neither a heartbeat nor work in flight → the 30-second liveness check calls `has_cli_process` on the supervisor; two consecutive misses of the CLI process (`cli_missing` counter, reset when seen) → `link_lost('supervisor', 'supervisor CLI exited …')` → the same restart path and limit as ② (3 per 30 minutes). Only an idle / busy supervisor that is READY and not adopted is judged; an idle supervisor whose process is alive is never restarted for "no activity" (only an in-flight turn is judged stuck). ④ Every supervisor rotation (frozen and broken link, not §34 mid-turn rotation) takes a log summary in `rotate` (`Watchdog.revive_summary`): last activity time and seconds stuck, the in-flight message and whether it was applied, the daemon's last 3 committed supervisor decisions; the summary is appended to the end of the new generation's first message (the takeover prompt) and audited as `supervisor.revived` (`stuck_since / stuck_s / in_flight / applied / decisions`), recorded alongside `watch.frozen_restarted`. If the supervisor tab is gone entirely, it is still §24 hold + pause (no evidence of a stop; original design unchanged).
- **A failing liveness check**: an error inside the supervisor CLI liveness check (`cli_check`) never stops the daemon. It is audited as `cli_check.failed` (level error), and the next check runs 30 seconds later.
- **The daemon replaces a held supervisor**: a held supervisor that the existing recovery does not release is replaced with its next generation by the daemon (section 24 "Supervisor regeneration"), within its own budget, and reported outward when the budget is used up.
- **Configuration**: `fleetmux/config/fleet.yaml#daemon_watch`: `check_interval_s` (default 120), `stale_after_s` (120), `realert_after_s` (600), in seconds and > 0; a private fleet.yaml can override the whole block. The daemon reads it once when it starts a supervisor generation and passes it to the extension via the environment variable `FLEETMUX_DAEMON_WATCH` (only to the main fleet's pi supervisor; a sub-fleet's daemon is resumed by its parent fleet, section 29, so it is not given). The same block also holds `held_after_s` / `held_realert_s` of the daemon's held watchdog (section 24 "Held watchdog backstop"); the daemon reads them, and they are never passed to the extension. The block also holds `subfleet_stuck_after_s` (600) and `subfleet_check_s` (60) of section 24 "Parent watch of sub-fleets"; they are not passed to the extension either.
  - The same block holds `supervisor_regen`, `supervisor_regen_max`, `supervisor_regen_window_s`, `supervisor_regen_backoff_s` and `supervisor_regen_probe` (section 24 "Supervisor regeneration"); the daemon reads them, and they are never passed to the extension.
  - The same block holds `subfleet_blocked_marks_child` (section 24 "Supervisor BLOCKED on a sub-fleet notice"); the daemon reads it, and it is never passed to the extension.
  - The same block holds `no_route_alarm` (section 24 "No route for human notices"); the daemon reads it, and it is never passed to the extension.
  - The same block holds `held_requeue`, `held_requeue_causes`, `held_requeue_after_s`, `held_requeue_max`, `held_requeue_window_s` (section 24 "Held requeue") and `pause_review`, `pause_review_after_s`, `pause_review_every_s`, `pause_review_wall_retry_s` (section 24 "Pause review"); the daemon reads them, and they are never passed to the extension.
- **Known gaps**: Claude / Codex supervisor sessions have no periodic mechanism to hook into (hooks such as `claude-rewake` are for workers only), so they do not notice a daemon outage by themselves and follow that AGENTS.md section only when a human brings it up; the Codex supervisor runs in a workspace-write sandbox and cannot write the run directory / fleet home, so the restart command will most likely be refused and can only be handed to a human. When Redis is down too, the bridge cannot read the heartbeat and the pi supervisor does not start a turn either. A hung daemon (holding the lock, no heartbeat) can only be handled manually.

## 37. New work in a new session: `dispatch[].fresh`

Problem: when a new task is added, the supervisor does not use a new worker but stuffs the task into an old worker whose context is entirely unrelated, wasting tokens; it should either dispatch to a new worker or close out the old worker that has finished its work. This section adds only one dispatch switch and two hints; handoff (section 4), generation labels (section 3) and the roster (section 16) are unchanged.

- **Switch**: a dispatch item may carry `"fresh": true` (default false, booleans only). Task / service decisions, pi's fleet_decision tool (the `dispatch` object in `pi_extension_v2.ts`) and the JSON of Claude / Codex supervisors all use the same key; `parse_decision` / `parse_service_decision` reject non-booleans (the v2 validator calls both of them too).
- **How it rotates**: when the decision lands, only `fresh_pending=1` is set on the slot, and the instruction is queued as usual. `deliver()` sees this mark at the original rotation decision point (after reading usage, before comparing the ratio) and goes to `Fleet.fresh_rotation`: the same order as `finish_rotation` (save the old screen -> generation +1, label `ctl-1` -> `ctl-1.1`, the old name still routes -> start the new generation in place (v2 launch-held first) -> take over the old tab position -> kill the old session -> v2 `confirm_retired` -> open the gate), but with **no handoff turn, no handoff note and no relay text in the first message** (it is only told to read AGENTS.md and reply READY). The working directory / worktree, role file and AGENTS.md are untouched; the history page records a generation as usual (`source: fresh`, no handoff note). After READY, the next tick delivers the queued instruction to the new generation. Claude / Codex / pi all take this generic path. `Fleet.fresh_rotation` is also used by section 45 (`fresh: always`), section 51 (`fresh_after_result`) and section 24 (model refusal); the `dispatch.fresh` audit carries `source`. Audit `dispatch.fresh`: slot, old->new generation, the dropped context tokens (`dropped_tokens` / `window`), the old transcript.
- **Guards**: target worker not idle (busy / handoff / held / clearing / delegated / starting with work), queued items, an in-flight v2 delivery, `clear_required`, adopted by a sub-fleet, a pending profile swap -> **the whole decision is rejected**, and the reason goes through the supervisor's correction channel (like other dispatch rejections, it counts toward the three bad replies). A slot with `delivered=0` (a new session) is not rotated but delivered to directly (audit `dispatch.fresh_skipped`). A worker with roster `fresh: always` is never refused and gets no `[Hint]`; its rotation happens when its queued dispatch is delivered (section 45). Pause and `max_busy` wait per the original rules, and if `deliver()` is not called there is no rotation; while a Codex native sub-agent is running it is deferred.
- **Worker status board**: each line of §32's `worker_board` gains `context used N% (k tokens)` at the end (only for slots that have done work, reading `usage()` live) and `last task: <first line of the last delivery, ≤80 chars>` (v1 `last_msg`; v2 takes the message body of the current `msg_id`; handoff deliveries do not count). service keeps it in the routing message as before; task mode now appends it to each worker report (`Worker status now (state, queue, context, last task)`). No data, no field.
- **Gentle hint, no automatic rotation**: when a dispatch lacks fresh, the target slot has done work and its usage exceeds `fresh_hint_ratio × window` → deliver as usual, and append one line to the end of the supervisor's next message: `[Hint] ctl-1 was at 55% context when it got this dispatch; if the work is unrelated to its last task, add "fresh": true next time.` (stored in `fx:fresh:hints` and cleared after `Fleet.queue` appends it, like advisor notes).
- **Supervisor prompts**: the five supervisor prompts gain a "Fresh session or reuse" subsection after "Adding and retiring workers" (continuing / fixing / retesting its own work → reuse; another package / file / topic → `"fresh": true`; more independent ready work than idle workers → add workers; no work left in a direction → retire), and the reply format gains a `dispatch[].fresh` line; `contracts_supervisor.md` gains a prohibition. The advisor only configures models and does not choose who gets the work; it is unchanged.
- **Configuration**: `fleetmux/config/fleet.yaml#fresh_dispatch.fresh_hint_ratio`, default 0.3, a number in (0, 1]; a private fleet.yaml can override the whole block (`fleet_config.fresh_hint_ratio()`, read live on every dispatch). A bad value does not reject the dispatch; it only records `dispatch.hint_failed` and gives no hint.
- **New session fails to start**: spawn failure (or the v2 new session cannot take over the old tab) → delete the half-started new session, restore the slot's Redis state wholesale to the old generation, and the instruction **stays in the queue for the old session** (not lost, not returned); the supervisor's next message carries a line `[Fresh failed] ctl-1: a clean session did not start (…); your instruction went to the existing session ctl-1.`, audit `dispatch.fresh_failed`. A failure after the old session was already killed (the old session could not be closed) → same as an ordinary rotation: `finish('blocked', … two sessions hold slot …)`.

**Known gaps**: each fresh costs one more CLI cold start (about 10–20 s to READY), during which the slot is `starting` and does not count toward `max_busy` (same as after an ordinary rotation). M items of a contracts task held back by `depends_on` are released later by the daemon without fresh. Under v2, fresh rotation and Claude hook delivery behave as in an ordinary rotation: the old generation's Stop hook dies with the old session, and the new generation uses the hook key of its own generation. A rejection counts as one bad reply; a supervisor that sends fresh to a busy worker three times in a row gets the task judged BLOCKED like any other bad replies (service pauses).

**Verification**: `tests/test_fresh_dispatch.py` (23 items: idle worker rotated cleanly then delivered / Claude worker / busy and queued rejected / new session not rotated / spawn failure falls back to the old session and says so / one-line hint over the ratio / no hint under it / fleet.yaml threshold change and bad value / task and service status boards / service rejection / validator rejects non-booleans / five prompts / v2 branch opens the gate and restores on tab-takeover failure), and the TypeBox contract in `tests/test_pi_decision_schema.py` (task and service both accept `fresh` and reject non-booleans).

## 38. Test log of the delivery gate

The dev_task `done_when` gate (`lifecycle_deliver.deliver`) used to put only the **last 1500 characters** of the `test_cmd` output into `rec['tests']['tail']`; the full output lived only in daemon memory and was lost as soon as the gate ended, and timeouts and start failures did not even get those 1500 characters. So in an incident a `tests failed (exit 101)` could not be investigated (the output of the earlier `cargo test` stages was all lost). This section only adds log evidence; verdicts and order are unchanged.

- **Path**: every `test_cmd` run (success, failure, timeout, start failure) writes `<run>/gate/<task_id>-tests.log`; when `deliver()`'s `run` is empty it falls back to the workspace's **parent directory** (`<workspace>.parent/gate/…`, never inside the workspace). An empty `task_id` gives `-tests.log`; the file name is fixed, without a timestamp, and a repeated gate for the same task overwrites it.
- **Header**: the first line is `gate test log`. Then `cmd:` (the argv as a JSON array), `cwd:`, `started:` (local ISO-8601) and one outcome line: `exit: <code>`, `timeout: <seconds>` or `start failed: <exception>`. A line `--- output ---` follows, then the full `stdout + stderr`.
- **Cap**: when the content exceeds 20 MiB, the **first 1 MiB + last 19 MiB** are kept with a marker line containing `truncated` in between (nothing is dropped silently); capture still uses the original `runner` / `capture_output` semantics; Popen is not changed for this.
- **Timeout**: the test timeout is `deliver.test_timeout_s` in the task spec, default 3600 seconds, and must be a positive number. The partial `stdout` / `stderr` of `TimeoutExpired` are still written to the log (they can be `bytes` even under `text=True`; both are decoded), and `rec['tests']['returncode']` is `None`.
- **Record**: `rec['tests']` is still `{cmd, returncode, tail, log}`; `tail` keeps its original meaning of the **last 1500 characters**, and `log` is an absolute path (`''` if writing failed); the record / `result.json` / Redis `gate:<id>` never hold the full text.
- **problem**: failure is `tests failed (exit N); log: <absolute path>`, timeout is `tests timed out after N s; log: <absolute path>`, start failure is `tests could not start: <exception>; log: <absolute path>`; a failed log write neither raises nor changes the verdict, it only notes `test log not written: ...` in problem (on a successful gate too, with `ok` still true). `rec['tests']['log']` is an absolute path when the log was written, and an empty string when writing failed.
- **Audit**: the `tests` of `lifecycle.gate` carries `returncode` / `log` / `tail`, where the audit's `tail` is the **last 20 lines** of those 1500 characters (the 1500 characters in the record are unchanged).
- **Unchanged**: the in-gate order `fast_forward_check → tests → merged → enqueued` (the review step was removed), fast-forward via CAS, enqueue dedupe, and writing `gate:<id>` only once all stay as before.

## 39. The history page colors follow the terminal background

Origin: a light system appearance + cmux `appearanceMode = dark` gave a dark terminal next to a white history page. The daemon runs in the control tab (a foreground process whose stdin is that pane's tty), so the daemon asks the terminal once, rather than each slot's actor.

- **One query**: at the very start of `Fleet.run()`, `Fleet.capture_terminal_theme()` → `terminal_theme.capture(run_dir)`: in raw mode it writes OSC 11 / OSC 10 queries to `/dev/tty` with a bounded `select` (0.3 s in total), accepts ST or BEL and 8/16-bit components, and afterwards does `TCIOFLUSH` and restores termios with `TCSANOW`.
- **File**: on a reply it writes `<run>/terminal_theme.json` (`{"bg","fg","source":"osc","at"}`, atomic `os.replace`) and audits `theme.captured`; no reply / not a tty / timeout / any error only records `theme.unavailable`, writes no file and never blocks or raises. An old file is kept (it is most likely still right).
- **history**: `history.write_page()` reads that file (strictly `^#[0-9a-f]{6}$`; anything else counts as absent); if present, it injects `<style id="terminal-theme">` after the original `<style>` (same `:root`, overriding `@media (prefers-color-scheme)` by order, including `color-scheme`), picks the dark / light base set by bg luminance and mixes `--panel/--line/--muted` from the terminal bg/fg; without it the page is byte-for-byte as before.
- **Limitation**: if the terminal theme is switched while the fleet runs, the page follows only at the next daemon start (`stop` -> `start --resume`, which respawns the control window and queries again); backends that do not answer OSC (such as sub-fleet slots inside tmux) fall back to the current colors automatically. Visual checks on a real machine belong to the delivery gate.

### 39.1 Which terminal answers the color query

- **Root cause of the white background**: the page honors only `prefers-color-scheme`, which follows the macOS system appearance; cmux's built-in browser with `browserThemeMode = system` renders pages by the system appearance, regardless of cmux's own `appearanceMode = dark`. So "light system + dark cmux" always gives a white background. Do not fix it through cmux browser settings or the system appearance; ask the terminal itself.
- **cmux answers the queries**: it answers OSC 10 / 11 / 12 / 4, in the format `ESC ]11;rgb:1e1e/1e1e/1e1e ESC \` (ST-terminated, 16 bits per component). With an empty ghostty config and `cmux themes` showing `inherit`, this is cmux's built-in default palette: bg `#1e1e1e`, fg `#ffffff`, cursor `#98989d`, ANSI 0–15 = `#1a1a1a #cc372e #26a439 #cdac08 #0869cb #9647bf #479ec2 #98989d #464646 #ff453a #32d74b #ffd60a #0a84ff #bf5af2 #76d6ff #ffffff`.
- **Whoever answers the query is the one followed**: the OSC answer comes from the terminal layer closest to the process. On the cmux backend that is cmux. On the fleetmux backend the fleetmux server answers, not the outer terminal. The server's default palette is the cmux palette (`Palette::CMUX`): background `#1e1e1e`, foreground `#ffffff`, cursor `#98989d`, and the fixed 16-colour ANSI table of cmux. The server answers OSC 10, 11, 12 and 4 from this palette. `fleetmux server --theme FILE` replaces it with a JSON object whose optional keys are `bg`, `fg`, `cursor` (each `#rrggbb`) and `ansi` (exactly 16 values); an unknown key or a bad value is a usage error. The Python backend passes a non-empty `FLEETMUX_THEME` as `--theme`. The server sends the palette as `hello.theme`, and the GUI window uses it for its terminal colours and its page background.
- **How to probe**: the query must run in a real terminal panel (Claude Code / child processes have no tty and cannot ask). Method: `cmux new-workspace --focus false --command "bash -c 'python3 probe.py; sleep 2'"`; the probe writes the query in raw mode, reads with a bounded `select` and writes the result to a file, then `cmux close-workspace --workspace <id> --force` closes it.
- **Do not let tests hang**: every pty / tty read goes through `select` with a timeout (`terminal_theme.query` reads with a bounded select, 0.3 s total). A single test file finishes within 10 seconds; one that does not return within 60 seconds is a hang bug to fix first.
- **Result**: with default palettes, cmux and the fleetmux server both answer background `#1e1e1e` and foreground `#ffffff`, so the history page matches the terminal cells. The theme is captured once at daemon start; a theme change mid-run needs a restart.

## 40. Extra writable directories for workers: roster `writable:`

- Origin: the worker role prompt forbids writing outside the worker's own directory (`OWN_DIR_RULE`). Some workers need to write elsewhere (an acceptance directory, a delivery repository, a test machine), and a strict model will not lift the rule from a supervisor message.
- Roster rows gain an optional `writable: [<path>, ...]` (a list of non-empty strings; `host:path` means writing that path on that machine over ssh). It applies only to that worker. The notice is appended after the own-directory rule, after any program grants (section 46): "Exception granted by the roster for you only: you may also write inside X, Y (a host:path root means that path on that host over ssh). Everything else outside your own directory stays forbidden." Without this key the prompt is unchanged word for word. With a `claude_native` sandbox policy, the worker's writable roots are also added to the Claude sandbox `allowWrite` list. The sandbox reads both the day roster and the night roster; the prompt notice reads only the day roster.
- Roster hot reload works as before: a `writable` change takes effect in the slot's next generation (the same path as a role change). Deletions still go to the user under the deletion rules; `writable` does not grant deletion.
- `start --project-dir DIR` creates the run under `DIR/.fleetmux/runs/`. It cannot be combined with `--runs-dir`. It adds `.fleetmux/` once to the git `info/exclude` of DIR. Every agent cwd is then inside DIR, so Claude Code loads the project `CLAUDE.md` as a parent file.
- Roster key `import_claude_md: [path, ...]` copies each file's text to the end of every agent's AGENTS.md and CLAUDE.md at each spawn. The text is copied, not linked, because Claude Code asks before loading files outside the cwd. Roster key `import_mcp: [{from, servers}]` merges the named MCP servers into each Claude agent's `mcp.json`; a kit server with the same name wins. Import paths are relative to the roster file, also in the run's roster copy.

## 41. dsh (DeepSeek Harness) workers: dsh-TUI + in-process bridge plugin

Decisions (from an internal plan): ① the pane runs the third-party dsh-TUI and the fleet loads a bridge plugin into the same process, the same shape as pi, which is not the headless CLI banned by §11 (this replaces v0's "resident ACP session + fleet print-style pane front end"; `dsh_pane.py` is deleted, so there are not two paths); ② dsh workers exist for evaluation against pi workers and are not in the default pairs; ③ the default model is WorkBuddy DeepSeek. The single daemon, private Redis, slot/generation labels, one-page layout, task/service protocol and handoff flow are all unchanged.

- **Shape**: the actor execs `node dsh --profile dsh-tui --patch <model patch> --patch <fleet patch>` (`dsh_adapter.argv`). **argv holds only launcher arguments**: at the first argument the launcher does not recognize (such as `--backend`) it hands that argument and everything after it to the app, and the app's positional argument is a startup prompt — measured: `--backend dsh --patch …` left both patches unloaded and turned the path into a prompt; placed after the patches it added an extra "dsh" prompt turn. So the backend is pinned by `backend: dsh` in the dsh-tui row of the patch (row config beats a remembered /kernel choice), not on argv. The startup prompt goes through the environment variable `FLEETMUX_DSH_STARTUP` (like opencode) and is injected by the plugin with its own message id.
- **Shared profile, per-pane HOME**: dsh-tui is installed once, in a shared DSH_HOME (`start --dsh-home`, default `~/.fleetmux/dsh-home`, recorded as config `dsh_home`); `start` prechecks dsh == 0.2.1-rc.5, node ≥ 22.19 and `@deepseek-harness-tui/dsh-tui` == 0.18.0 in that DSH_HOME's `profiles/dsh-tui`, and refuses otherwise, giving the install command (`DSH_HOME=<dir> dsh plugin --profile dsh-tui add @deepseek-harness-tui/dsh-tui@0.18.0`, about 2.5 minutes); it never installs per run. Each generation's pane HOME = `<run>/dsh-tui-home/<slot>/g<N>`: pre-written `.dsh-tui/onboarding.json {"completed":true,"version":1}` and `.dsh-tui/home.json {"seen":true}`, env `DSH_TUI_NO_LAUNCHPAD=1`, straight into the conversation; from the real HOME only `~/.gitconfig` and `~/.npmrc` are symlinked (only if present; `~/.ssh` deliberately excluded). dsh-TUI's `~/.dsh-tui` state therefore lives entirely in the pane HOME and never touches the user's. The fleet patch moves `session-persistence-jsonl.root`, `storage-json.root` and `dsh-tui-storage-json.root` to `<pane HOME>/dsh/` (measured to work: session logs and storage land in the run, and the shared DSH_HOME no longer grows new sessions; DSH rewrites the empty skeleton `cordis.yml` in the profile at every start and leaves `cordis.patch.yml` alone). Other env: `DSH_HOME` (shared), `DSH_AGENTS_HOME` (an empty directory per generation), `DSH_TELEMETRY_MODE=DISABLED`, `DSH_TELEMETRY_DISABLED=1`, `DSH_PERMISSION_MODE=workspace-write`. dsh-tui's npm new-version check at startup only shows a notice, which is accepted (a fake `NPM_CONFIG_REGISTRY` would also break the worker's npm, so it is not used).
- **Single source of models = pi's `models.json`**: `dsh_adapter.model_patch` takes the `dsh_model` entry (with `fleet.yaml#pi_context` applied) and generates the `llm-pi-ai` route (baseURL / api / this one model), `agent-default-model`, and the dsh-tui row `{provider, model, backend: dsh, upstreamRetry: false}` (the patch replaces the config row wholesale, so it writes the full provider + model pin; a half pin is ignored; the TUI is not allowed to seed a retry policy into the route). models.json is read before HOME is switched. The key reaches the dsh-TUI process only through the actor environment (`apiKeyEnv: FLEETMUX_DSH_API_KEY`), never in the patch, config.json, the spawn command line or the argv record, and the plugin strips it when starting the bridge child process. **The default model** is the explicit config `fleet.yaml#dsh.default_model` (`workbuddy-ds/workbuddy-ai/deepseek-v4.1-flash-sg`, through the local gateway): without `--dsh-model`, `start` takes it, prints one line to stderr and records it as `dsh_model` in the run config; `--dsh-model flashnext/…` still works. The `start` precheck also checks that the provider endpoint `GET <baseUrl>/models` lists this model id (with the key as bearer), and refuses if it is not listed or there is no answer. Model card `workbuddy-ds-dsh` (context 400000, `max_concurrent: 2`) sits beside `flashnext-dsh`.
- **Bridge plugin `fleetmux/dsh_bridge.mjs`** (a cordis plugin row; `createRequire(ctx.baseUrl)` gets `defineTool` from `@deepseek-ai/dsh-tools`; a semantic port of `pi_extension_v2.ts`): starts `agent_bridge.py` over a pipe, with the same frames as pi / opencode; binds the root agent of `ctx.agents.roots()` / `agent/created` (the agent id is the DSH session id, which is the bridge's `thread`); writes its own transcript `<run>/dsh-sessions/<slot>/g<N>/<session>.jsonl` (one line per session event, for `event()` validation and the watchdog's mtime signal; DSH's own zstd session log is not used as a signal); logs to `<pane HOME>/dsh_bridge.log`, never to stdout (the terminal belongs to the TUI).
  - One turn: `agent.followup(frozen user message, id generated by the plugin)`; `agent/inbox/claimed {message.id, turn}` matches the id, and `turn/end` of `session/event` (`reason.kind` ∈ completed / aborted / error / blocked / max-tokens) settles it: completed → `settled`; aborted → `aborted`; others → first report `candidate {tool: model, error}` (the wall check reads it), then `failed`. Text = the text segments of this turn's `assistant/message`, joined with a newline per segment (measured: the TUI's "⏵ title" narration and the answer are two segments, and joining them directly glues READY onto the narration so it fails the READY check). Usage = `ctx.get('tokenMeter').measure(session).totalTokens` (synchronous); the window is models.json's `contextWindow`, reported with every frame as `ctx_used / ctx_window`. Model lock: in a fleet turn, if the provider/model of `assistant/message.source` is not `dsh_model` → that turn fails.
  - Report: the `fleet_result` tool (`defineTool`, parameter `{result: {type: object, required: true, additionalProperties: true}}` -- object parameters must state additionalProperties) accepts candidates only in the turn of this delivery and not for handoff deliveries; task mode checks locally that done / evidence are arrays of non-empty strings and not_done / blocked are strings, service checks the request's required / allowed keys, except that an object holding only a `blocked` string is accepted without that check; on accept it calls `exec.concludeTurn()`. v0's dsh-only branch "final text -> candidate" is removed; the role file uses the bridge's `_v2` (fleet_result) version, with the same correction text as pi / opencode.
  - Manual turns (as in pi / opencode): a turn that claimed another id is a manual turn (`availability idle:false`, back to idle at the end, no settlement); a delivery arriving during a manual turn gets `reject_delivery`; a turn that claimed both our id and another id → `hold` (the bridge accepts `hold` for dsh too; `dsh hold` counts among the faults that only restate a slot hold, not a broken link); a mix-in during the startup turn fails the startup. After the daemon judges a startup answer not READY, the next turn with another id (the correction the daemon types into the pane) is treated as the new startup turn.
  - Session switch: a new root agent appearing after binding (`/new`, session restore) — per pi's rule "a bound session is never replaced without retirement": no rebinding; the plugin reports an error and stops the bridge, and the daemon, seeing the heartbeat / link lost (§35), starts the next generation in the same slot.
  - Permissions: an `approval/request` waterfall (`{prepend: true}`, ahead of the TUI's own responders; request = `{agent, toolName, callId, reason, displayReason, signal}`; the command / path are taken from the arguments of the preceding tool-call with the same callId, and `command / file_path / path / pattern / url` go into `resources`) → an opencode-shaped `permission` frame with the same deletion classification (`permissionClass` in `opencode_plugin/lib.js`; the bridge's second check can only be stricter). It also calls `next()` so that the TUI's own dialog asks the person at the pane. Deletion / move classes wait only for that person (a supervisor `permission_reply` is refused with `permission_refused held_for_user`); for other classes, whichever arrives first wins, the person or the supervisor ruling (`permission_reply` once → allowed-once, reject → rejected). No self-approval on timeout while waiting; the outcome is reported as `permission_resolved` (`decided_by` fleet / tui_user / none). The daemon side (`prompt_channel=dsh`, cards, the watchdog pressing no keys) follows v0.
- **Registration** (unchanged from v0): `worker_cli: dsh`, worker only, transport v2 only; `is_bridge` includes dsh; default `max_busy` 2; one `pi+dsh` task row in the combination table, status verified; dsh is listed in `report_only`, so a dsh model is shown but never a default candidate; `transport_inventory` recognizes dsh processes by `--patch <run>/dsh-tui-home/...`.
- **Not done**: a dsh supervisor; mid-turn rotation (the mid-turn prompt the daemon types into the pane is a manual input in dsh-TUI and would count as a mix-in; when the probe sees dsh it only records a warn + card, and rotation happens only at turn boundaries); resuming a session (rotation = new session + handoff note); joining the default pairs; `deps.yaml` gets no dsh entry (the version gate is in the `start` precheck).
- **Verification**: `tests/test_dsh_worker.py` (two patches / no key / pane HOME and symlinks / env / argv / version and dsh-tui gates / endpoint gate / default model / model card / actor / fleet_result role / bridge pass-through and hold), `tests/test_dsh_bridge_plugin.py` (a JS fake cordis ctx + fake bridge drive the real plugin: startup and delivery id matching, fleet_result, the four turn outcomes, model lock, handoff turn, manual turn and delivery rejection, mix-in hold, deletion-class permissions handed to the person / others ruled by the supervisor, a new root agent retires the bridge, startup correction turn), `tests/test_dsh_daemon.py` (private Redis).
- **Known risks**: dsh is a release candidate, and the dsh-tui 0.18.0 plugin surface (`agent/inbox/claimed`, the `approval/request` waterfall, `tokenMeter`) was all found by measurement; before upgrading, rerun the internal plan's probes on a development machine, then change `dsh_adapter.VERSION / TUI_VERSION`; if the supervisor rules first on a non-deletion permission, the TUI's own dialog does not close by itself (it only honors the request's abort signal) and stays in the pane until someone presses a key — which then no longer takes effect; `workspace-write` only governs files and does not block network access; `rm -rf` inside the worker directory runs directly under `workspace-write` with no permission request (weaker than opencode); the preset brings its own instruction loader, and v0's line pinning the project root to the worker directory has no effect in dsh-tui (dsh-tui disables the host row), so a worker directory inside a git repo reads the repo root's AGENTS.md / CLAUDE.md; a sub-agent's permission request whose tool-call arguments cannot be found is treated as a deletion class and handed to the person; it competes with pi workers for the same endpoint.

## 42. Development-flow role pack devflow: tests first, interfaces frozen first

Requirement: "write the tests first and the interfaces and constraints follow; make this development flow a standard at the supervisor". This section adds an **optional role pack**. devflow is prompt text only: the program does not check its gates; the supervisor runs them with grep. It is off unless attached with `--roles` and a roster `role:` column. It coexists with contract mode and with the decomposition tree. It does not change the base `supervisor.md` / `worker.md`, the single daemon, the task/service protocol or the slot/generation identity; a run without `--roles` behaves as before.

- Location: `fleetmux/agents/devflow/` (`supervisor.md` is appended to the supervisor via `--roles <that directory>`; `worker.md` is appended to each development worker via the `role:` column of `--roster`; `roster.example.yaml` is an example roster). The example roster names its first slot `contract`; `start` refuses that roster, because a new slot name must end with `-<number>`. Rename the slot `contract-1` in a copy of the roster.
