- Flow: round 0 dispatches only the `contract` worker, which delivers the skeleton (real signatures, bodies raise NotImplementedError) + the key table (exactly one writer per key/field) + the contract tests and one end-to-end test without test doubles + the open conventions (for structural ones it picks a conservative answer and writes it into the contract) + `phase0_check.txt`; only after the supervisor greps `IMPORT_OR_SKIP: 0` and `ROWS_WITHOUT_WRITER: 0` and the gate passes are the implementations dispatched in parallel. An implementer fills only the bodies of its own functions; it does not change signatures, the key table or the contract tests and never invents an interface. A gap is written as `接口缺口` (interface gap), `contract` bumps the version and the work is dispatched again. One file goes to one worker per round. The REPORT carries a `CONTRACT_TESTS:` line, and skipped counts as failed. Before completion, all candidates are stacked and the end-to-end test is run once.
- Contract mode (task spec `contracts: true`; docs/contracts.md and `fleetmux/contracts.py`) enforces the same idea in code: an interfaces phase, a skeleton phase, then parallel work, with one writer per JSON key checked by the program. It is off by default for each task. devflow runs with or without it. With both on, the supervisor gets both texts, and nothing joins the devflow contract worker with the contract mode skeleton worker.
- The decomposition tree (section 62) is on with the shipped configuration (`fleet.yaml` `decompose.enabled: true`, read once per task at submit). So a devflow supervisor first replies with a tree only, and dispatches the contract block after the program accepts the tree. Mapping devflow onto tree cuts (the `contract` block decides the shared interfaces with `cut: decision`, implementation blocks `consume` them, the end-to-end check is a serial integration block with `depends_on` + `serial_reason: integration`) is guidance; the devflow files do not mention the tree and no code links them. When contract mode is also on, the task table's parts come from the tree.
- Why: when an external project ran M1/M2/M3 with several workers in parallel, it got fields that "only said who reads them, nobody wrote them", two pieces taking opposite answers to the same convention, tests silently skipped when a dependency was missing, and workers each assuming an interface and redoing work repeatedly.
- Boundary: the gate greps marker lines, which proves "the marker was written", not that the counts are right. Contract tests catch names and shapes, not semantics such as timing or units; semantics are unified up front in `decisions.md`. No acceptance on a real fleet run yet.

## 43. No global request deadline; an expiry holds only its slot; a frozen session resumes first

Requirement: a supervisor check that is not finished but healthy should just keep waiting; remove the 900 s timeout that froze the whole fleet; resuming the old session is acceptable.

Cause: a fixed request deadline duplicated health checks that other parts already own. Health already has its own owners: the three watchdog signals (section 24 transcript / screen / process-tree CPU), the bridge heartbeat and link loss (section 35), StopFailure (section 24), and the tab / process liveness checks. A wall-clock request deadline is a duplicate health check; it only makes sense when the caller itself needs a fresh answer (for example one review caller passes `timeout_s: 120` per request, the memory-extraction caller passes `timeout_s: 900` per request). And on expiry `expire` called the generic `hold()`, whose default is `pause=True`: the whole service fleet stopped dispatching, queued requests timed out one after another, the supervisor got no messages, nothing resumed automatically, and only a manual `resume` / `start --resume` helped. This section changes three things on top of the section 5 request protocol, section 7 failure semantics, section 17 replay and section 24 watchdog; the single daemon, the task / service protocol, the result states and the slot / generation identity are unchanged.

- **No global deadline**: `DEFAULTS['request_timeout'] = None`, and `start --request-timeout` sets nothing by default. A request without `timeout_s` never expires by wall clock. An explicit `--request-timeout N` and the named sub-fleet entry `timeouts.request` (§29) are still the run's default deadline; a request's own `timeout_s` (10-86400) is unchanged. Every reader goes through `fleet.request_limit(spec, cfg)` (None = no deadline): v2 `expire`, v1 `expire_requests`, `midturn_order`; a sub-fleet entry without `timeouts.request` takes `DEFAULTS`, i.e. None.
  - **Request messages carry no bus deadline**: the `deadline_at` of a service request's messages (the one dispatched to a worker and the one handed to the supervisor for routing) is always `runtime_v2.NO_DEADLINE_MS` (2100-01-01 in milliseconds, 13 digits, still an integer under Redis Lua `%.14g`). The request's own deadline is enforced only by `expire()` scanning `fx:reqq`. Before, message deadline = request deadline, so at expiry three places could hold the slot and pause the fleet before `expire` did: the tick's "lease/delivery deadline" branch, the late check in `settle`, and lease renewal in `renew_tui`; now they fire only when the lease itself expires (original semantics unchanged). Messages still queued for a request that is already terminal are dropped by the "already terminal" check in `deliver()` (existing), no longer by a deadline at offer time. That check drops only request-bound messages: every kind except `followup` / `advisor` whose request id has no open request, §29 op or applet op. To the supervisor, only the `[Request <id>]` routing prompt is request-bound. Every other program message to a service supervisor (`[sub-fleet report]`, `[sub-fleet interfaces]`, `[Sub-fleet M<n> completed|failed]`, `[contract]`, `[escalate]`, `[dialog]`, a decision correction, an advisor note, and so on) is queued with kind `followup` by `RuntimeV2.queue` and is delivered even when the request id it inherited (`notice`, or the request the daemon was settling) has ended. Before this fix those notices were cancelled as "already terminal" about one second after they were queued, so a service-mode top fleet never showed its supervisor a sub-fleet report. The control details show this constant as the `deadline` of such messages.
  - **Which supervisor messages survive a request**: in service mode only the routing prompt that starts with `[Request <id>]` is bound to its request. Every other program message to the supervisor (sub-fleet reports, interface notices, corrections, escalation notes) is queued with kind `followup` and is delivered even when the request id it inherited has ended. When a request has ended, `deliver` cancels a queued message for it as `already terminal` only when the message is not `followup` or `advisor` and no sub-fleet op or applet op with that id is open.
  - **While paused**: a request with a deadline keeps counting and goes `timeout` on expiry; one without waits in the queue for the resume. Request age counts awake seconds (`midturn.effective_age`): time the machine sleeps is not counted, and a mid-turn handoff pauses the request clock. A fleet pause does not stop the clock. From now on section 7 "requests keep queueing and still time out" and the README's "the caller never waits forever" hold only for requests with a deadline; a caller that needs an upper bound passes its own `timeout_s`.
- **Expiry = a stale answer, not a failure; it never pauses the fleet**:
  - Only the worker slot that is executing it is held: `hold(pause=False)` (the same call as the §24 lease expiry and §35 link loss), `timeout` is published as before, and everything else dispatches normally; the supervisor keeps routing new requests to idle workers. A request whose `to` names that slot waits in its mailbox until the slot is released (or expires on its own deadline). A held slot still counts toward `max_busy` (as with StopFailure).
  - The supervisor is never held for this: a request that expires while the supervisor is routing it → only `timeout` is published; the supervisor's turn ends and commits normally, and its dispatch for that request is skipped because the request is no longer in routing (`dispatch.unknown_request`, existing).
  - A late answer: `fx:result:<id>` is still the first result; its state does not change and the late answer is not used. The answer text goes into the audit `result.late` (`request_id`, slot, `label`, `detail.public_status` and `detail.reply`: a JSON object if it parses as one, otherwise `{raw: last 4000 chars}`). Official Claude hook slot: the turn of the same session ends after the hold → `turn_ended` carries the turn text → `release_held` puts the slot back to idle (§24 hold exit, existing) and records `result.late`. pi / bridge slot: a late `settled` still only records `settle.late` plus `result.late`; **the slot has no hold exit** (§24 known gap ⑦-4). Now only that one slot is lost instead of the whole fleet stopping; getting it back needs a manual `start --resume`. The v1 `result.late` log carries `raw`.
  - Only the request-timeout pause source and the interrupted-request hold (section 24 "Held requeue") change. Supervisor BLOCKED, brake / usage wall, a dialog waiting for a human, handoff timeout, the link-loss restart limit, `applying` intent recovery, and a failed lease renewal of an official-CLI slot in service mode still pause as before.
- **A frozen session resumes first (official Claude worker)**: §24 frozen rotation, after its existing guards (only hold while the run is paused, only hold for a sub-fleet adoption, only hold when not in the registry, do not kill when a dialog waits for a human, the advisor takes its own branch, the §34 mid-turn rotation path), launches the next generation of a worker slot with `claude --resume <thread>` when three things hold: the transcript file still exists, this generation is not itself a resumed one (`resume_generation != generation`), and the in-flight item can be redelivered (task: the running task's dispatch / continuation, `replay < max_attempts`; service: a request dispatched to this slot, not `replay: false`, `replay < max_attempts`). The decision and the startup prompt are shared with the §24 "tab gone" case (`RuntimeV2.resumable` / `resume_prompt` / `redeliverable`; `next_generation` now uses the first two as well).
  - **Order** follows frozen rotation: the new generation starts launch-held, `tab_into` puts it in the old tab's position → kill the old process tree and confirm the pane is gone → `confirm_retired` → redeliver → open the startup gate. Until the startup gate opens the actor only waits and does not exec `claude`, so `claude --resume` always starts after the old process tree is confirmed gone and two processes never write the same session; the position stays in the original tab (unlike the "tab gone" case, which can only go to the end of the worker row).
  - **No skeleton handoff note** (the session carries its own context); the screen and terminal snapshots are still saved, and the history page records a generation with `source: frozen-resume`. The startup prompt is the same as for "tab gone": the session was interrupted (it froze: …), the daemon has resumed it, after READY the unfinished work is sent again, check what is already on disk before continuing.
  - **Redelivery** is the same as for "tab gone": task `requeue_lost`, service `replay_request` (`replay` +1; service is routed again, a request with `to` goes back to this slot). No FROZEN report to the supervisor and no `stalled` published. The audit `watch.frozen_restarted` gains `resumed=<thread>` (empty for a brand-new generation).
  - **Falling back to today's behavior**: a resumed generation that freezes again → a brand-new generation + skeleton handoff note, and the original instruction is not rerun (task: a FROZEN report to the supervisor; service: `stalled` published); a missing transcript, exhausted attempts or `replay: false` → the same path as today. A resumed generation that is not READY within `startup_deadline` → the same `resume_fallback` as "tab gone": a brand-new generation + skeleton handoff note (`-lost.md`); **the item already redelivered has not run in the resumed generation, so the brand-new generation runs it once**, without adding to the count.
  - **pi / Codex / opencode / dsh workers**: an interrupted pure request is requeued by section 24 "Held requeue" (next generation, request routed again within `max_attempts`). A frozen session of these CLIs still takes the frozen rotation.
  - A failed rotation (the new generation cannot start / cannot get into the old tab / the old session cannot be killed) → the existing `abort_rotation` (restore the old generation, hold); the resume marker is restored with it, and nothing is redelivered.
  - Unchanged: the supervisor slot, Codex / pi / opencode / dsh slots and the advisor slot still use today's frozen rotation (possible follow-up: session resume for Codex / pi).

**Verification**: `tests/test_request_no_deadline.py` (8 items: no default / explicit default / `request_limit`; a request without a deadline does not expire in a month, the message deadline is a constant and stays an integer through a real Redis round trip; expiry holds only that slot, still no pause after renewal and tick, other requests are routed and answered normally; an expiry during routing does not hold the supervisor, and the late routing decision is skipped; a late answer goes only to `result.late`; v1 without a deadline does not expire), `tests/test_frozen_resume.py` (9 items: a frozen Claude worker resumes in its original tab, the startup gate is closed while the old one is killed, argv carries `--resume`, the instruction is redelivered with `replay` 2, no FROZEN report; no transcript / exhausted attempts / freezing again after a resume take today's path; a resumed generation that is not READY falls back to a brand-new generation and the item runs only once; when the old session cannot be killed everything is restored and no resume marker is left; the supervisor is unchanged; a replayed service request is answered, and `replay: false` still goes `stalled`). Old tests changed with the constraints: the three frozen cases in `test_watchdog.py` now assert the resume; in `test_midturn_v2.py` the request message deadline is now the constant; in `test_service_stop_failure.py` "a timeout without StopFailure still pauses" now holds only that slot; tests relying on the 900 default now set a deadline explicitly.

**Known gaps**:
- A run started by an older release may still hold `request_timeout: 900` in its config.json, and `start --resume` keeps it. A fresh start writes no default. No pause on timeout and the frozen resume take effect immediately for a resumed run; messages queued before the resume still carry the old deadline.
- The hold exit of pi / bridge slots is still missing (⑦-4), see above.
- The `claude --resume` resume covers Claude workers only.
- The users (docs/contracts.md section 4) pass `timeout_s` explicitly. docs/contracts.md section 4 states the unset default and that an expiry holds only the slot.

## 44. Long task split: probe nudge -> worker submits a split plan -> supervisor approves with models -> after rotation it supervises a sub-fleet

The long-running nudge applies only to a task that runs with the decomposition tree switch off (section 62). The switch is False in the code defaults (`decompose.DEFAULTS`), and a bad `decompose` block reads as off. The shipped `fleetmux/config/fleet.yaml` sets `decompose.enabled: true`. A private `decompose` block replaces the shipped block as a whole, so a key it leaves out falls back to the code default. So with the shipped configuration the tree is on, and the long-running nudge is not sent. A task reads the switch once at submit. With the switch on, the program never nudges a worker to split; a block on the longest chain that runs past est x `overrun_ratio` minutes gives the supervisor one `[overrun]` note instead (section 62).

Requirement: when a task runs a long time and is not just a plain download or computation but a workflow that keeps moving on, the current worker should be considered for becoming the supervisor of a sub-fleet that splits the task. The worker proposes how to split it to the supervisor, and the supervisor assigns models again and has the worker hand off. A task-mode worker that has been busy for `check_minutes` (default 40) is checked. The probe counts its tool calls in the last `window_minutes` (default 10). Both values are set in the roster `split:` block. All parts already exist: the section 34 probe, section 20 escalate / approve, section 19 adoption, the section 4 rotation handoff note; the single daemon, slot/generation identity and task/service protocol are unchanged.

- **Exception to §27**: on this path the supervisor picks the models itself. An approve may carry `supervisor_profile` (the card the worker's next generation uses) and `worker_profiles` (one card per child worker); with the advisor on it may suggest, the supervisor decides. Elsewhere "the supervisor does not pick models" still holds; `supervisor.md` / `supervisor_v2.md` state this as an exception, the original rule is not deleted.
- **Nudge (probe extension)**: only task-mode workers are watched. After `check_minutes` of continuous busy time (counted from this delivery's `busy_since`), the main loop adds `split_since` to the target of the §34 probe thread. The probe reads the tail of this generation's session file (≤1MB, read only when due, separate from the 64KB usage read; read even if the file has not changed) and counts the tool calls of the last `window_minutes`: calls ≥ `min_calls` and distinct files / commands ≥ `min_distinct` counts as "flowing"; otherwise it counts as "one thing that is slow by itself" and is looked at again after `check_minutes`. Every look writes `split_last` (e.g. `calls=37 distinct=12 files=9 in 10m: flowing; nudged`) and the audit `split.checked`. A generation that is in the middle of a §34 mid-turn rotation is not looked at.
- **Only nudge what can be split**: after "flowing" it first passes `subfleet.check(ready=False)` (task-number ownership, depth, sub-fleet count, whole-tree headcount, model quota, slot type); if that fails there is no nudge and the reason goes into `split_last` (`not splittable: ...`). Only a Claude Code worker of a v2 task fleet is nudged. The program checks this itself after `subfleet.check`; a non-Claude worker gets `split_last` "not splittable: only a Claude Code worker takes the nudge (PostToolUse hook)". pi / opencode / dsh panes cannot be handed over (section 19), and v1 does not support adoption. At most one nudge per slot per task (task number), and none while the same task already has a pending request.
- **Leaf blocks**: a leaf block is a unit not worth splitting further: one worker does it whole, which costs less and runs faster than a sub-fleet. A task-mode dispatch item may carry `"leaf": true` (strict boolean, refused otherwise; the pi `fleet_decision` schema accepts it). The program stores it on the item (`fx:items[M<n>].leaf` for a plain task, the contract task row `leaf` for a contract task; a later dispatch of the same id without it clears it). The nudge check refuses a leaf before `subfleet.check`, recorded in `split_last` as `not splittable: M<n> is a leaf ...`, so a leaf is never asked to split however long it runs; an item without `leaf` is nudged as before. Boundary: only the nudge is refused by the program; a worker's own `[escalate]` split of a leaf still reaches the supervisor, whose prompt says to reject it unless the report shows the block was not a leaf after all. The supervisor writes the acceptance check of a leaf into its instruction and accepts the leaf itself from the report. Prompts: `supervisor.md` / `supervisor_v2.md` section "Leaf blocks", `contracts_supervisor.md` reply format. Tests: `tests/test_leaf_dispatch.py`.
- **With the decomposition tree on (section 62)**: no worker is nudged (`split_due` returns nothing, the probe never counts `split_since`), and `dispatch[].leaf` is not taken from the supervisor: `leaf` is the program's judgement on the tree (off the longest chain, or a split does not pay: a leaf). A long block goes to the supervisor instead: a block on the longest chain past `est x overrun_ratio` minutes gives one `[overrun]` note (re-estimate, merge, extract a decision, or split it in the tree). A worker may still ask to split on its own, with `delta_t` / `delta_c`. The roster `split:` keys are read as before and apply again when a task runs with the switch off; automatic numbering does not apply (every dispatch names a tree block).
- **How it arrives**: a one-line prompt `[FLEET LONG-RUNNING fx-<16hex>] ...` (task number, minutes run, call count; it asks the worker to finish the current step, start no new one, and send a report in the AGENTS.md format: Done = what is done, Not done = what is left, an escalate JSON at the end, each piece with a `kind` describing the nature of the work; it says explicitly that this is not a context handoff and no handoff note is written). It is registered only in the agent hash (`split_id / split_prompt / split_g / split_message / split_send_pending`), **never as a message**: the clock is not paused, nothing is retyped after 300 seconds, no pane is killed, and the turn settles normally (it is not `handoff_split`). Claude takes the same PostToolUse hook as section 34 (`claude_posttool` takes it when there is no pending section 34 prompt; taking it once clears it), and it is handed only to the delivery it was registered for (`msg_id == split_message`), so one not taken by the end of the turn never leaks into the next delivery. At the same time the supervisor gets a `[long-running] <label>` message. It names the worker, the minutes run, the tool calls in the window and the files changed. It says to wait for the [escalate] request, that an approve may pick the models, and that without `supervisor_profile` the program picks the supervisor model from the supervisor chain. It uses its own prefix instead of reusing `[FLEET MID-TURN HANDOFF`: the two ask opposite things of the worker (one forbids a report, the other requires one), and a shared prefix would confuse the model. Cost: the pi extension's input gate and Codex `codex_queue.bound` do not recognize this prefix; today they never get a nudge anyway (previous item), and once pi / Codex panes can be handed over both places must let it through. The task number in the nudge is the `[M<n>]` of the first line of the delivery that is running (only when that is missing, the number of the slot's last dispatch), so a dispatch queued behind it is never taken as the task to split.
- **What each CLI provides**: Claude transcript: `tool_use` of main-thread `assistant` lines (skipping `isSidechain` / `agentId`); distinct = `file_path` / `path` / `command` / `pattern` / `url`; file changes = Edit / Write / MultiEdit / NotebookEdit. Codex rollout: `function_call` / `custom_tool_call` / `local_shell_call` of `response_item`; distinct = the argument text (in newer versions a script text, deduplicated only by raw text, coarse); file changes count only apply_patch's `*** Update/Add/Delete File:`. pi session: assistant `toolCall` of `message` lines; distinct = `path` / `command`; file changes = edit / write. All three cut the window by the line's ISO `timestamp`. opencode / dsh have no session file the probe can read → the result is "unknown", recorded as `split.unknown`, **no nudge**. Calls older than the tail are not counted (this can only undercount, and undercounting means no nudge).
- **Approve with models**: an approve item in `escalations[]` may add `supervisor_profile` (a card id) and `worker_profiles` (a list of card ids whose length is the number of child workers; if `workers` is also given they must be equal). Shape errors (not a card id, not a list, wrong count, models on a non-approve, models on a dialog / blocked answer) take the original correction path and count as consecutive rejections. A content failure takes the same path as a limit failure: no consecutive-rejection count, the request stays pending, and the supervisor gets `[escalate] approve of M0 refused by the program: <reason>`. `supervisor_profile`: the card must exist, must be a claude card (only official Claude panes can be adopted), and must pass `night.resolve_launches` (CLI pairing and the v2 config check), `card_gate` (host + this fleet's quota) and `preflight_profile` (CLI binary; v2 does not allow changing the CLI). `worker_profiles`: inside `subfleet.check(profiles=…)` they pass `resolve_launches(..., workers)` from the sub-fleet's point of view (the adopted Claude supervisor + these cards), i.e. card exists, pairing, host, quota (`check_slots`); checked once at approve and once when the sub-fleet really starts. Cards come from the fleet config's card library (the same source as roster profiles). The `[escalate]` notice lists the available cards at the end.
- **Rotate, then adopt (order)**: an approve with `supervisor_profile` does not write the start-sub-fleet request directly (`spawn_child` needs the worker idle + READY + nothing in flight, which holds neither before nor after rotation). The request is recorded as `rotating` (the sub-fleet start parameters, the new launch and the current generation are stored in the request record), and the slot gets `split_restore` (the original `runtime_launch`; not recorded when `restore_model` is off). Everything after that happens in `escalation_tick` (daemon tick, outside the v2 transaction): (1) slot idle + READY + nothing in flight -> write `split_launch` and `start_rotation` (the handoff note prompt is `handoff.SPLIT_PROMPT`: the new session will supervise sub-fleet M0; write what is done, the approved split plan and what is left for each piece); (2) the handoff turn settles as in section 4, and `finish_rotation` writes `split_launch` into `runtime_launch` before starting the next generation (the same place as the advisor rescue's `rescue_launch`); the new generation starts on the card the supervisor picked, reads the handoff note and returns READY; (3) the generation has changed and the new generation is idle + READY -> write the start-sub-fleet request (with `worker_profiles`), the request becomes `approved`, and from there it is the original section 20 flow (`process_requests` -> `spawn_child` adopts the new pane -> the child daemon's first tick starts the child workers on their cards). An approve without `supervisor_profile` (every sub-fleet walks the supervisor chain): the program picks the supervisor model with `Fleet.supervisor_chain_pick` (the same pick as a job supervisor, section 58 "Model chains"): the first usable Claude entry of `discovery.supervisor` (`job_chain.subfleet_supervisor`), skipping walls and models at their quota for this slot. The pick's card, or its model without a card, goes through `split_supervisor_launch` and the rotation above, like an approve with `supervisor_profile`. No usable entry: the program refuses the approve (`[escalate] approve ... refused by the program`, the reason lists each entry); no sub-fleet starts and no default model runs. The child config, its `result.json` and the `status` tree item carry `model_chain.supervisor`. When the slot already runs the pick (`job_chain.runs_pick`): if the slot is already in the fleet's own tmux -> adopted in place as before, no rotation; if it is still in a normal pane -> it is also recorded as `rotating` (`launch` empty, no `split_restore`) and takes the three steps above as a same-model rotation (no `split_launch`, the handoff note is still `SPLIT_PROMPT`); the new generation starts in tmux and is then adopted (see the worker-area mapping item below). Both may carry `worker_profiles`.
- **Approval reserves the slot**: while a split request is approved or rotating, the slot belongs to the split. (1) within one decision the split answers run first (`on_supervisor` sorts stably by kind), so the order inside the decision does not matter; (2) while the request is `approved` / `rotating` / `delegated`, a `[blocked]` ruling for the same slot and same task (the `item` of the `blocked` record) is not sent: it records `blocked.superseded` and appends one line to the end of the supervisor's next message saying it was superseded by the approved split and needs no action (`fx:split:notes`, appended the same way as the section 37 notes, without starting a turn of its own); (3) if the worker is mid-turn at approve time (Claude, `state=busy` with a delivery in flight), a one-line program prompt `midturn.SPLIT_APPROVED_PROMPT` is registered on the same PostToolUse channel as the section 44 nudge (`Fleet.split_hold`, the same `split_*` fields, handed over once): finish the current step, start no new one, end this turn with a report, no split plan, no handoff note; an idle worker gets nothing, its next delivery is the rotation handoff or the adoption; (4) while the request is `approved` / `rotating`, any dispatch to that slot is rejected as a whole with a correction (`dispatch: the approved split of M0 hands worker-1 over to sub-fleet M0 ...`, counted as a consecutive rejection, like "approve and dispatch in the same reply"). Known gaps: during `delegated` the program does not block dispatches; they queue as before and go to the new generation after the collect (the same shape as defect B; the prompts and correction texts say not to dispatch until `[Sub-fleet M0 completed]`, the program only blocks approved / rotating); deliveries that were already queued for the slot before the approve (e.g. an earlier contract notice) are not withdrawn and go first, as in the section 20 gap; the `item` of a plain task's `blocked` record is the **last** dispatch id for that slot, so a Blocked reported while M0 runs and M3 is queued is recorded as M3, and splitting M0 does not supersede that ruling.
- **The board marks a slot reserved by a split**: before, `worker_board` printed only the slot's `state`, so a split slot in `approved` / `rotating` showed as `idle` in the supervisor's routing prompt (service) and in the `Worker status now` at the end of each worker report (task); the supervisor dispatched to it and was rejected by `escalation_decision`, counted as a consecutive rejection. Now `worker_board` reads `fx:escalations` once and appends `, split M0 approved: it is becoming sub-fleet M0's supervisor, do not route to it until [Sub-fleet M0 completed]` to that slot's line (the same for `rotating`); nothing is added for `delegated`, since the slot's `state` is already `delegated`.
- **Worker-area mapping**: the supervisor of a sub-fleet is shown at the position of its parent worker. an approve (with or without models; the backend has `tmux_view_info`, i.e. cmux / fleetmux; WezTerm is already refused in `subfleet.check`) sets `tmux_view=1` on the slot's agent hash; `tmux_slot(cfg, slot, info)` is true when it sees the marker (except the `supervisor` slot; split slots inside a sub-fleet count too, because the marker belongs to the slot, not to the roster; rule: the supervisor of any sub-fleet is mapped at its parent worker's position, and the same for a grandchild fleet, i.e. the supervisor of M0.<n> is mapped at a worker position of the M0 page). `Fleet.spawn` passes this slot's hash to `tmux_spawn_args`, so the next generation started by a rotation (`finish_rotation`), `RuntimeV2.respawn` or `--resume` starts in the fleet's own tmux (`<run>/tmux.sock`, session `<slot>-g<generation>`). `spawn_child` -> `parent_attach_view` -> `parent_view` detects that the new pane is a tmux session -> no placeholder, no release, `subfleet.json` `view: 1`; the child daemon's first tick runs `attach_child_view` to open a second client (fleetmux: `view-<session>` at the top left of the child page). Hook mailbox redirection (section 19, `adopt.hook`) still routes by the parent slot's `delegated` + generation, regardless of whether the pane moved. Collect: the child side detaches its client and the parent tab turns back into a worker in place; the marker is not cleared and the slot stays in tmux (on the hook channel the next generation started at collect is also in tmux, so a second delegation needs no rotation). A revise / reject after the approve does not clear the marker either; the next generation still goes into tmux, which is harmless.
- **Window size with two clients**: the parent page tab and the child page view are two clients of the same tmux session with different sizes; tmux has only one window size, and `window-size latest` (`cmux.py` `TMUX_CONF`, already set, also the tmux default) follows "the client with the most recent input / size change". Switching pages or tabs produces neither, so the visible client keeps the size of the invisible one and the extra rows/columns are filled with `-`; when the child page view has just attached (the attach itself counts as activity) the parent page immediately looks like this too. A same-size SIGWINCH or `switch-client -c <tty>` makes that client the latest, `refresh-client` does not. Fix: (1) fleetmux server (`view::nudge_shown_tmux`, at the end of `apply_pty_sizes`): after every layout pass, for panes that have just come into view (the selected tab of each group on the current page, or the zoomed one) and, after a size change, for all panes in view, if the foreground program is `tmux`, send one SIGWINCH to its foreground process group; never to any other program. (2) the same server's tmux watch (`boot::tmux_watch` runs `view::tmux_watch_tick` every 100 ms): when the foreground program of a pane on any page becomes `tmux` (a client attached, often later than the layout pass) or a tmux pane's PTY size changed (e.g. the child daemon's `arrange` moves the view into the supervisor group, the kernel sends it SIGWINCH and it takes the window), it sends one more SIGWINCH to the tmux clients in view 300 ms later, giving that client's own resize time to reach tmux first. Who owns the size is decided only by the server, which knows which page is visible. (3) cmux backend `attach_view`: when the new client is not in `current-workspace`, it waits for it to appear and then runs `switch-client` on the existing client (`cmux.tmux_give_back`). The fleetmux backend does not give the size back this way; (2) covers it. Test: `tests/test_fleetmux_pages.py::test_the_visible_tmux_client_sizes_the_window_after_attach_and_page_switch[|M0]` (real fleetmux + real tmux, in the order of `start_adopted`: child page worker -> view attached before the first worker -> `arrange`; the parent page / child page is the current page at attach time, the visible client has no fill without a page switch, then the pages are switched back and forth four times; asserts `window_width == client_width`, `window_height == client_height - 1`; needs `FLEETMUX_GATE=m4`). Boundary: cmux has no hook for workspace switching, so after switching over a key (or a click) in that tab is needed before the size changes; not tested on real cmux; wezterm does not use two clients.
- **`allow_profile_live_switch`**: this path does not go through it. It governs the §12 task-level "Phase 5 live switch" of `profile_assignments` (`switch_profile` / `prepare_profile`); this path, like the §25 roster card change and the §27 rescue respawn, is a rotation the daemon schedules itself at an idle boundary (handoff note + new generation), and before changing the card it still checks the card library, pairing, host, quota and the CLI preflight. So it works with the default off and does not need it enabled.
- **Child worker cards**: `child_config` explicitly writes `worker_profiles = {worker-1: card, …}` for a sub-fleet started by escalate (`{}` when none is given). Before, the child config inherited the parent's `worker_profiles` unchanged; with the same slot names `worker-N`, the parent's own per-slot profiles leaked into the sub-fleet. `start_adopted` adds `apply_roster_launch` after `init_worker` (as `start_all` does), so a child worker's first generation starts on its card; §29 named sub-fleets therefore also start their first generation on the roster card instead of starting on the default and changing the card after READY.
- **Restore the original model on collect**: if the slot has `split_restore` at `collect` → it becomes `split_launch` and the slot's next generation starts with it (`finish_rotation` or `RuntimeV2.respawn`, effective in both places like `rescue_launch`). On the Claude hook channel (default) the next generation starts at collect → restored right away; on a same-session collect over the typing channel (§19) the session keeps the split's model and is restored only at its next rotation. `restore_model: false` → no `split_restore`, the new model stays. If starting the sub-fleet after the rotation is refused and the request reopens: a second approve (even with a different `supervisor_profile`) keeps the original model recorded first; if the supervisor changes it to revise / reject → `split_restore` becomes `split_launch` and the slot's next generation is restored. The restore does not pass the quota gate again (see boundaries).
- **Automatic numbering**: a dispatch item of a plain task without `id` → the program fills in the next free `M<n>` (inside a sub-fleet `<this fleet's number>.<n>`, e.g. `M0.0`), writes it into `fx:items`, and the dispatch's first line carries `[M<n>]`, so any dispatch can be split; one with `id` is handled as before (format check, same semantics). Contract tasks already require a number, unchanged. The old §20 rejection "a dispatch must carry an id" therefore hardly appears in plain tasks any more.
- **Configuration (roster yaml, hot reload)**: a top-level `split:` and each worker entry's own `split:`, keys `check_minutes` (a number ≥0, 0 = no nudge for that worker), `window_minutes` (a number >0), `min_calls`, `min_distinct` (integers ≥1), `restore_model` (boolean); resolved per key: worker entry → top-level table → built-in (40 / 10 / 15 / 2 / true). Hot reloaded with the §16 roster and effective at the next tick; `roster.changed` carries the old and new `split` values; any invalid value rejects the whole file as in §16 and keeps the old values. A run without a roster file uses the built-in values. `restore_model` is fixed by its value at approve time; editing the roster later does not flip an in-flight split.
- **Display**: `split_check_minutes / split_window_minutes / split_min_calls / split_min_distinct / split_restore_model / split_source` of `fx:agent:<slot>` (e.g. `check_minutes: worker; window_minutes: roster; …`, like `handoff_source`) and `split_last`; `status` has an extra `split` block per slot. Audit / log: `split.checked`, `split.nudged`, `split.unknown`, `escalate.rotate`, `escalate.rotated`.

The tests run the chain on two real v2 fleets with a fake terminal world.

**Verification**: new `tests/test_long_split.py` (the nudge picks the task by the `[M<n>]` of the running delivery, not the last queued one; after a split reopens, a second approve keeps the original model and a reject restores it at the next generation; the `split` block of status; roster `split:` resolution order and whole-file rejection of 8 kinds of bad values, hot reload + status fields + bad values keep the old ones + `check_minutes: 0`; in-window call / distinct / changed-file counts for Claude / Codex / pi sessions, unknown for opencode / dsh and a missing file; the probe reports activity when due even if the file did not change; real private Redis v2: a flowing Claude worker is nudged only once, PostToolUse hands it over only once, no message is written and the clock is not stopped, the supervisor gets `[long-running]`, the worker sends its escalate normally; a registered nudge is not handed to another delivery; a slow one is not nudged and is looked at again after 40 minutes; no nudge when not due, with `check_minutes: 0`, or when `subfleet.check` refuses; approve shape errors count as consecutive rejections; unknown card / pi card as supervisor / fable quota full / child worker card over quota / unknown child worker card -> a refusal that is not counted and stays pending; a valid approve records `rotating`; automatic numbering; two real v2 fleets: approve with models -> the worker writes a handoff note -> new generation claude-opus READY -> the sub-fleet adopts the new pane, child workers start on claude-haiku / claude-sonnet -> completion and collect -> the slot's generation 2 is back on the original launch, with `allow_profile_live_switch` off; an approve without models adopts in place and the parent's per-slot profiles do not leak into the sub-fleet); in `test_escalate.py` "plain tasks must carry an id" changed to automatic numbering, `test_pi_decision_schema.py` / `test_pi_decision_schema_v2.mjs` add the two optional keys of the pi supervisor schema (TypeBox run in node); the dispatch texts of five existing tests gained `[M<n>]` from automatic numbering. Worker-area mapping: the e2e of `tests/test_long_split.py` now runs in a fake world with the two-client primitives (`ttree`): after an approve with models the slot has `tmux_view`, the rotation starts in `worker-1-g1`, `view: 1` with no placeholder, the child-side view gets the role change, during delegation `delegated_route` points to the child supervisor, after the collect the next generation `worker-1-g2` is still in tmux and the hook goes back to the parent slot; without models in a normal pane -> same-model rotation into tmux, then adoption (`runtime_launch` unchanged, no `split_restore`); without models and already in tmux -> adopted in place without rotation; `tests/test_subfleet_tmux.py` covers the marker version of `tmux_slot` / `tmux_spawn_args` and `spawn`; `tests/test_subfleet_fleetmux.py::test_task_subfleet_m0_on_fleetmux_delegates_and_returns` changed to the new semantics (fake + real fleetmux binary, real tmux: rotation into tmux -> a live tab with an unchanged pid on the parent page + a view at the top left of the child page -> after the collect generation 2 is still in tmux and still on the parent page).

**The supervisor opens a sub-fleet directly**: a service supervisor has three choices: start a sub-fleet, dispatch to a worker (the existing `dispatch`), or answer itself (the service decision `answer`, section 5). The switch `supervisor_split` enables the first choice. Switch `supervisor_split` (`start --supervisor-split`, config boolean, default off; when off the item does not appear in the supervisor template, and a decision carrying `split` is invalid as a whole, counted as a consecutive rejection with an explanation that the switch is off). When on, a dispatch item of a service decision may carry `split: {reason, proposal: [{instruction, output, kind?}], workers?, note?, supervisor_profile?, worker_profiles?}`: the program records the request as dispatched to the `to` worker but does not deliver it, runs `service_split_intake` (number M<n>, rewrite the piece ids, section 44 limit checks) and **sends no** `[escalate]` question (`message_id` empty, `escalations_missed` does not chase it), then immediately approves it with `escalation_apply` using the supervisor's parameters; from there it is exactly the same path as a split requested by a worker (rotation / adoption / `delegated` answer / `<id>.done`). If the program refuses (limits, `split_judge: off`) -> it takes the refusal path, hands the request to that worker as a plain dispatch to answer itself and tells the supervisor, which amounts to an automatic downgrade to tier two. When to use which tier is not in the code: it is written in that fleet's supervisor role file (`supervisor.md` in the `--roles` directory, section 4) and tuned by the user through reviews. Audit `split.by_supervisor`. Verification: `tests/test_supervisor_split.py` (7 cases: shape check, invalid when the switch is off, the template shows it only when on, config check, approval sends no [escalate], a limit refusal downgrades to a plain dispatch, real v2 parent and child fleets start a sub-fleet and answer `delegated`).

**Jobs use the same path (§58).** A job submitted with `fleetmux task` to a service fleet goes through this machinery, started by the program instead of a worker or the supervisor. The program builds the split record itself with an empty proposal, so the sub-fleet's supervisor plans the pieces. The job's record carries `job: <job id>`. Because of that marker, every branch that would hand the work back to a worker or ask the supervisor puts the job back in the job queue instead. `supervisor_split_check` and `escalate_check` are unchanged and still require a proposal.

## 45. A new session before every dispatch: roster `fresh: always`

Problem: section 37 `dispatch[].fresh` only works when the supervisor remembers to write `"fresh": true`. When it forgets, the worker takes the next item with the old context. This section makes a clean session a per-worker roster setting that the daemon enforces. It extends section 37: the rotation is still `Fleet.fresh_rotation`, with no second rotation path. Handoff (section 4), `handoff_ratio` rotation, section 34 mid-turn rotation and generation labels (section 3) are unchanged.

Relation to §51 `fresh_after_result`: `fresh: always` is set per worker row, rotates before each dispatch, and lets the supervisor opt out for one dispatch; `fresh_after_result` is set per named sub-fleet entry and rotates after each published result, with no opt-out.

- **Roster key**: a worker entry may set `fresh: always | off`. The default `off` behaves exactly as before. Only these two strings are accepted (YAML 1.1 reads a bare `off` as false; that also counts as `off`). `true`, `yes`, numbers and other strings reject the whole roster with `workers[i] (<name>): fresh must be "always" or "off"`; the `parse_entries` key hint lists `"fresh"`. `roster.fresh(raw)` returns `{name: 'always'}`. It goes into the run's `config.json` as `worker_fresh`, the same way as `pi_models` (`start --roster` writes it; the §16 hot reload updates `self.cfg['worker_fresh']` and `persist_roster` writes it back to config.json / latest.json). The daemon reads it on every delivery, so a hot reload applies to the next dispatch. `roster.changed` records `fresh always|off; applies to its next dispatch` in its results.
- **Behaviour**: `deliver()` checks at the §37 decision point (after reading usage, before comparing the ratio). It acts only on the next **dispatch** of an always worker: in task mode a message with the `[Supervisor instruction ...]` head; in service mode a message for an existing `fx:req:<id>`. Contract notices, peer notices and continuations do not count. If the current generation has delivered work (`delivered > 0`), the daemon first runs `fresh_rotation` (save the screen, generation +1, no handoff turn, no handoff note, no relay text in the first message) and delivers the dispatch on the next tick after READY. If `delivered == 0` (including a generation that was just rotated in), it delivers directly.
- **Difference from §37**: §37 `"fresh": true` requires an idle target with nothing queued, or the whole decision is rejected. `always` never rejects. The supervisor may queue work on a busy always worker (with or without `"fresh": true`; `fresh_refusal` always passes an always slot). The rotation happens when the queued item's turn comes and the slot is idle. An always slot never gets the §37 `fresh_pending` mark and never gets the over-ratio `[Hint]`.
- **Explicit `"fresh": false`**: no rotation for that one dispatch (use it when the worker should keep fixing or rewriting its own work). `mark_dispatch` records the supervisor's explicit true / false for an always slot together with the instruction text in `fx:fresh:intent:<slot>` (at most 32 entries). `deliver()` matches a record when its instruction text appears in the pending text, and deletes it once used. Two queued dispatches to one worker, one plain and one `false`, are handled each on its own. When the slot is idle with an empty queue (`idle_rotate`), leftover records (cancelled dispatches) are cleared.
- **Guards from §37 postpone, never skip**: a pending profile swap (the existing `profile_swaps` return at the top of `deliver()`), adoption by a sub-fleet, running Codex native sub-agents, or `clear_required` keep the dispatch queued and never deliver it to the old session. Audit `dispatch.fresh_deferred` (`reason`; logged once per generation and delivered count). The rotation and delivery happen once the guard clears. Pause, `max_busy` and non-idle states follow the normal rules (no rotation if `deliver()` is not called). Exception: when a task's live profile holds the slot runtime and the roster swap is deferred (`roster_swap_deferred=1`, §25), the `profile_swaps` return already lets delivery through; always does not wait for it and rotates as usual, because waiting would block until the task ends.
- **New session fails to start**: as in §37. The half-started session is deleted, the old generation is restored, the dispatch goes to the old session, the supervisor's next message carries `[Fresh failed] ...`, and the audit records `dispatch.fresh_failed`. `fresh_rotation` returns False; after the restore `deliver()` sets `fresh_always_failed=<generation>:<delivered>` so the same dispatch is not retried on the next tick (audit `fresh_skipped: fresh_failed`). Once the old session takes the dispatch, `delivered` changes and the next dispatch tries again.
- **§28 clear_after_result slots** (Claude service only) are already cleared after every item; always adds no rotation (audit `fresh_skipped: clear_after_result`). §48 retired the typed `/clear` channel and `start` refuses `--clear-after-result`, so this only applies to runs started before §48.
- **Audit**: `dispatch.fresh` gains `source`: `always`, `explicit` (an explicit `"fresh": true`, on an always slot or a plain §37 slot), `after_result` (§51) or `stopfail` (§24). `dispatch.fresh_skipped` on the always path uses `reason` = `explicit_false | delivered_0 | clear_after_result | fresh_failed` (the §37 text reasons are unchanged). `dispatch.fresh_deferred` uses `reason` = `adopted | clearing | native subagents still running`.
- **Display and prompts**: the §32 / §37 worker status board (service routing message, end of a task report) ends an always worker's line with `, fresh: always`. The "Fresh session or reuse" subsection of the five supervisor prompts says that a worker marked `fresh: always` gets a new session for every dispatch (busy workers still take queued work, nothing is refused, no `[Hint]`), and that `"fresh": false` keeps the session when the worker should continue its own work. The pi `fleet_decision` contract already accepts a boolean `fresh` and is unchanged.
- **Scope**: task and service mode. Claude / Codex / pi / opencode / dsh workers all go through the generic `Fleet.deliver` path (v2 Claude hook delivery also enters `v2.deliver` from there).
- **With §51 on the same slot**: after a result, §51 sets `fresh_pending` + `result_fresh` and `idle_rotate` (or the §37 branch of `deliver()`, if a dispatch arrives first) rotates the slot. The always branch then sees `delivered == 0` and skips (`fresh_skipped: delivered_0`), so the slot rotates once per item, not twice. An explicit `"fresh": false` cannot keep the context on such a slot, because §51 already rotated after the result.

**Known gaps**: each dispatch costs one more CLI cold start (about 10-20 s to READY); the slot is `starting` meanwhile and does not count toward `max_busy` (same as section 37). Explicit true / false is matched by instruction text: two queued dispatches with identical text on one slot are matched in order. A contracts item held back by `depends_on` and released later by the daemon carries no supervisor true / false (it rotates as always). Only the two dispatch heads above count as dispatches: in v1 task mode and in service mode other messages (contract / peer notices) go to the current session without a rotation but still add 1 to `delivered`; in v2 task mode `deliver()` adds the instruction head to every worker message, so a notice also counts as a dispatch and rotates first (one more cold start, no message lost). The tests (`tests/test_fresh_always.py`) run against a private real Redis server.

**Verification**: `tests/test_fresh_always.py` (30 items: roster always / off / default parsing and 7 bad values, the key hint lists `fresh`; two dispatches in a row, the second rotates first with `source: always`; explicit true on an always slot records `explicit`; a busy worker queues without rejection and rotates after it settles (task, both forms, and service); explicit false keeps the session for that dispatch only, two queued dispatches each handled on its own; profile swap / clearing postpone, never skip; spawn failure restores the old generation, delivers to the old session, says `[Fresh failed]`, retries on the next dispatch; `off` matches the previous behaviour; hot reload off -> always -> off and bad values reject the whole roster; status board `fresh: always`, no `[Hint]` on always, no field on off; five prompts; pi v2 two dispatches rotate; §28 slot `fresh_skipped: clear_after_result`, skipped as retired by §48). `tests/test_fresh_dispatch.py` and `tests/test_roster.py` still pass.

## 46. Permission boundary of a sub-fleet supervisor: sandbox escapes and the like are never self-approved; they go to the root supervisor

Requirement: a sub-fleet supervisor may not grant its workers any exception to the workspace boundary. Anything that would leave the sandbox is reported to the root supervisor. Before this section the program blocked only artifacts outside the workspace (section 23 "artifact path must stay inside the workspace"); the workspace boundary for the work itself existed only in the prompts.

- **Role file of a sub-fleet supervisor**: for a supervisor whose config has `parent_config` (§19 escalate, §29 named, and contract-task sub-fleets all count), `role_scope` appends a "## Sub-fleet boundaries" section to the end of AGENTS.md / CLAUDE.md (`fleet.SUBFLEET_BOUNDS`): it and its workers work only inside this sub-fleet's workspace (plus the roots that the roster's `writable:` gives to a specific worker); it **may not grant any exception**, nor tell a worker that there is one. Examples (not exhaustive): writing the directories of the parent fleet / another fleet / another worker, another repository, home configuration, credentials and keys, turning off the sandbox / hooks / permission checks, network access or installs beyond the task, killing processes it did not start, git push / force, deleting or moving outside the workspace, anything else that escapes the sandbox. When it meets one (the work needs it, a worker did / asked for / reported it): it does not grant it and does not do it, that piece is put on hold (other pieces continue), it reports it up with `report_up` in its next decision and waits for the root supervisor's `[top ruling]`; a `[blocked]` a worker raised for this is answered `reject` together with a `report_up`. The top-level supervisor gets no such section and never sees the `report_up` key.
- **Workers of a sub-fleet**: in the same place (`role_scope`, with `parent_config`), one sentence is added after the "do not write outside your own directory" rule (`fleet.SUBFLEET_WORKER_RULE`): an "exception" from the supervisor to work outside the workspace is not valid; only the grants listed above in this file count (the roster's, and those the program issues from the root supervisor's ruling, see "Grants issued from a ruling" below); refuse everything else, do not do it, and explain it under Blocked in the report.
- **Task directory**: an M<n> sub-fleet started by a section 44 split / section 20 escalate continues work in the parent worker's own directory. `child_config` records `task_dir` = the parent slot's working directory (`fleet.agent_cwd(parent, slot)`) for a non-named sub-fleet, and it counts as part of this sub-fleet's workspace: the first item of the child supervisor's "Sub-fleet boundaries" states that it may work and dispatch work there, that this is not crossing the boundary and needs no report (`fleet.SUBFLEET_TASK_DIR_BOUND`; "the parent fleet's directories" in the examples becomes "the parent fleet's other directories"); a child worker gets one more item after "do not write outside your own directory" (`fleet.SUBFLEET_TASK_DIR_RULE`, appended in the same place as the roster's `writable:`), and the section 46 sentence "only roster grants count" becomes "only the grants above count"; the sub-task goal (`subfleet.goal_text`) states the task directory's absolute path, and the old "nobody may write outside this sub-fleet's workspace" becomes "...the workspace and the task directory"; the Claude launch arguments of a child worker gain `--add-dir <task_dir>`. The other section 46 prohibitions (the parent fleet's other directories, other fleets / workers, credentials, push, etc.) are unchanged; the section 23 artifact copy is unchanged: an artifact written directly at the parent path also counts (the copy is skipped when there is no copy on the child side, and the parent's check sees the file as `returned`; tested). section 29 named sub-fleets have no task directory. Boundary: this is still the prompt layer, the program does not block writes (same as the known boundary of this section); Codex child workers are not in the v2 matrix (section 19), and no writable root was added to the Codex sandbox.
- **Reporting up (`report_up`)**: a child supervisor's decision (both the task and the service JSON accept it) may carry `"report_up": [{"what": "...", "why": "...", "who": "worker-N"}]` (`who` optional); only with RUNNING; a RUNNING carrying it may dispatch nothing (like `escalations`). A top-level fleet supervisor carrying it → correction "report_up is only for a sub-fleet supervisor", counted as a consecutive rejection. Each item writes one notice to `<parent run>/subfleet-notices/`, reusing the §20 interface-notice channel (intermediate levels pass it up unchanged with `os.replace`, without reading it):
  `{"kind": "report", "id": "report:M0:<6hex>", "origin_run": "<child run>", "task": "M0", "supervisor": "worker-1.1", "what": "...", "why": "...", "who": "worker-2"}`
  `supervisor` comes from the child config's `adopt.label` (the parent slot label `child_config` records when it starts the sub-fleet, i.e. the generation that really supervises). The sub-fleet records `fx:reports_up` (pending key → what); while anything is pending `others_active` is true, so a later RUNNING of the supervisor without dispatches is valid (the ruling's arrival gives it a turn by itself).
- **Top level**: `relay_notices` recognizes `kind == "report"` before the interface branch: it queues `[sub-fleet report] sub-fleet M0 (worker-1.1): <what> — <why> (worker: worker-2)` + how to answer + how to judge (approve only what the user's task explicitly allows; delete / move, push, credentials and keys, and anything only the user can decide are always reject; if the task cannot do without it, BLOCKED to the user) for the supervisor, and records `report:M0:<id>` in `fx:escalations` (`kind: report`, `message_id` = this message). The root supervisor answers in `escalations` with `{"task": "report:M0:<id>", "decision": "approve" | "reject", "note": "..."}`; `revise` is not accepted (correction). The `escalations` of service mode also accept the `report:` prefix. **No answer**: the same as `[blocked]` (`escalations_missed`): the first time it is sent back with "answer report:… first" without counting, the next miss counts as a consecutive rejection; **never auto-approved**. A late answer (the record is gone) is only logged, not an error. **Lost notice**: a `[sub-fleet report]` message cancelled while still queued (for example by the queue reconcile of `start --resume`) was never seen, so `escalations_missed` does not send back or count it, and `escalation_tick` asks again with the same text (`report_reask`, log `report.reasked`); the record stays open until the supervisor rules.
- **Awaiting the user**: when the root supervisor answers a `[sub-fleet report]` with BLOCKED, the root fleet does not pause (section 24). The report record gets status `awaiting_user`. An awaiting record is never nudged, counted, re-asked or approved by the program. The user rules with `fleetmux answer report:<task>:<id> approve|reject --note ...` (exit 0 accepted, 2 rejected, 3 pending). The supervisor may still rule on it later.
- **Ruling sent down**: `report_ruling` writes `{"kind": "report", "ref": "report:M0:<id>", "decision": "approve", "note": "...", "what": "..."}` into the `subfleet-vetoes/` of the **original sub-fleet** (the same directory as the §20 vetoes, straight to the origin, not through intermediate levels); the sub-fleet's `apply_vetoes` recognizes `kind == "report"` before the veto branch, clears that item from `fx:reports_up`, and queues `[top ruling] report:M0:<id> approve: <note>` + (what was reported) + "approve: do only as much as the note says and dispatch to tell the workers concerned; reject: do not do it, drop that piece or use another approach inside the workspace" for the supervisor.
- **Grants issued from a ruling**: a text approval relayed by the child supervisor is not trusted by its workers. So an approve goes through the existing grant mechanism and becomes a grant issued by the program, instead of yet another text exception:
  - **Reports carry paths**: a `report_up` item may carry `"paths": ["/abs/path", ...]` (non-empty, absolute, normalized; the `writable:` model has no access kinds, it just means "writable"); with `paths`, `who` is required and must be a worker of this sub-fleet (checked for both the task and the service JSON). The notice, the child-side `fx:reports_up` (now `{what, who, paths}`) and the open top-level `report:` record all carry it; the `[sub-fleet report]` message lists the paths and explains that an approve issues grants for them and may narrow them.
  - **Narrow only**: the root supervisor's approve may carry `"paths": [...]` (a new optional key of `escalations[]`, approve only, added to the pi supervisor schema too); each must equal or lie inside a reported path, otherwise the whole reply is corrected and counted as a consecutive rejection (`paths may only narrow what the sub-fleet reported`); answering with paths when the report had none also counts as widening. The ruling file of an approve with paths gains `who` / `paths` (without them, all reported paths). The child side's `report_ruled` checks once more: it grants only inside the reported paths and only to the reported `who` (any other `who` in the ruling file is ignored).
  - **Issuing**: `Fleet.grant` records `{report, paths}` in the `grants` field of that worker's `fx:agent:<slot>` (visible as `slots.<w>.agent.grants` in `status`; log / audit `grant.issued`, visible in history). `role_scope` lists "Grant issued by the program from the top fleet supervisor's ruling report:… : you may also write inside `…`" (`program_grants_notice`) after the "do not write outside your own directory" rule and before the roster grants, so every later generation of that worker carries it; for the generation that is running, `set_grants` rewrites `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md` in its directory in place: it replaces only the program grant lines after the "own directory" line and leaves the surrounding kit / domain-rule layout untouched (it does not reassemble the file, to avoid diverging from the several spawn layouts).
  - **How the running worker is told**: no separate delivery is queued (in task mode every delivery requires a report, which would waste a turn); the program records one line `[Fleet grant report:…] … re-read that file …` on the slot (`grant_note`), and the program puts it at the very front of the next dispatch the child supervisor sends it (before `advisory`; in service mode after the `[Request <id>]` header), only once. The trust anchor is the file: the worker rule says only grants in the file count; this line only points the way, and text the supervisor writes itself is still not valid (a supervisor's dispatch always starts with `[Supervisor instruction …]`, so it cannot forge the start of a delivery). The `[top ruling]` the child supervisor receives states that the program has issued a grant to worker-N and that it should dispatch that piece directly.
  - **Scope**: only for this sub-fleet run and only for that worker; when the sub-fleet ends (`Fleet.finish`, with `parent_config`: completion, BLOCKED, failure and stop/cancel all pass through it), `clear_grants` clears the field, restores the files, drops any pointer line not yet delivered, and records `grant.cleared`.
  - **Per CLI**: a Claude worker runs with `--dangerously-skip-permissions`, and only the role text restrains it, so rewriting the files + the pointer line take effect at once (it rereads CLAUDE.md as instructed; a Claude Code session does not reload CLAUDE.md mid-session by itself, untested whether it would); the roster's `writable:` was never mapped to Claude `--add-dir`, and grants do not add it either. Codex worker: the text arrives as well, but the sandbox's `writable_roots` exist only in the launch arguments and the roster's `writable:` is not mapped to workers either, so Codex cannot write there (Codex child workers are not in the v2 matrix anyway, §19). pi worker: no sandbox (`UNSANDBOXED_SCOPE`), same as Claude. opencode: the role is in AGENTS.md of the instance directory, which an in-place rewrite does not find, so it takes effect only at the next generation.
  - **An approve without paths** (needs that are not write paths, such as network access or installs): still only a text ruling, the program issues nothing; the `[top ruling]` says explicitly that the program issued no grant and the worker still does not accept your exceptions outside the workspace. No capability system is built for such needs.
- **The task clock stops while a report waits**: from the `report_up` until the last ruling is back (`fx:reports_up` empty again), the sub-fleet's time budget does not run. `Fleet.report_clock` writes `report_wait_at` into `fx:task` when the first report goes up and, when the last ruling arrives (in `report_ruled`, before the `[top ruling]` delivery takes its deadline), moves `deadline` by the time waited and adds it to `report_wait_s`. Every reader of the task deadline (`budget_exhausted` in both ticks, the delivery `deadline_at`, a waiting declaration's horizon, a split's remaining hours) goes through `fleet.task_deadline`, which counts a running wait. `status` shows `task.clock_paused_s` (all the time stopped so far) and `task.clock` (`paused: report_up awaiting the top ruling` or `running`). Cause: a task sub-fleet finished its code in about 30 minutes, then waited hours for rulings and ended `budget_exhausted`. A report the top never rules on keeps the clock stopped; the brake `max_hours` line of a budget card and a lifecycle `hard_timeout_min` still count wall-clock time.
- **The root supervisor's prompt**: the command table of `supervisor.md` / `supervisor_v2.md` gains a `[sub-fleet report]` line after the `[blocked]` line: answer in the escalations shape above; approve only what the user's task explicitly allows and write in the note how far the approval goes; delete / move, push, credentials and keys, and anything only the user can decide → reject, and if the task cannot do without it, BLOCKED to the user; no answer is sent back once and then counted as a consecutive rejection, never auto-approved. The three service-mode supervisor templates did not get this line (the `[sub-fleet report]` message itself carries how to answer and judge).
- **Fixed along the way**: `subfleet.collect`'s `[Sub-fleet M0 completed] worker-1.2 ran sub-fleet M0` used the label of the generation restarted after the collect; now the label is recorded on entering `collect` (the slot does not rotate while delegated), so the notice names the generation that really supervised (`worker-1.1 ran …`), while the second half "… is a worker again (new generation …)" still uses the current label.

**Known boundaries**: this is the prompt layer + a reporting channel; **the program does not block writes outside the workspace**: if a child supervisor grants anyway, or simply does not report, the program cannot tell (artifact paths are still blocked by section 23); a hard workspace boundary needs each CLI's sandbox / writable roots, separate work. When the top-level fleet is not running the notice is dropped (same as section 20 interface notices), and that piece of the sub-fleet waits until its own deadline. If the sub-fleet ends (deadline, stop, failure) before the ruling arrives, a top-level `report:` record that waits for the root supervisor stays open: the root supervisor is still sent back / counted until it answers, and the answer is written to a `subfleet-vetoes/` that no daemon reads any more (a direct sub-fleet could clear it at `collect`, but the top level never sees a grandchild's collect, so this was not done). A record that waits for the user (`awaiting_user`) is dropped when its sub-fleet is no longer active (log `report.awaiting_dropped`). While waiting for a ruling a sub-fleet is not judged stalled: `check_stalls` only looks at busy / starting slots, `lifecycle_close` does not manage a fleet with `parent_config`, and only the task's own `hours` deadline remains. The service-mode top level has an end-to-end test on real private Redis v2 (report delivered, record open across ticks, approve reaches `subfleet-vetoes/`); the Pi supervisor's `fleet_decision` schema has no `report_up` (a pi pane cannot be a sub-fleet supervisor, section 19; TypeBox objects do not forbid extra keys), but `escalations[]` gained `paths` (pi can be a top-level supervisor). Grants issued from a ruling: still the prompt layer, the program does not block writes, it only gives "approved" a program origin; the Codex sandbox writable roots and a running opencode generation do not follow (see "Per CLI" above); remote paths of the form `host:path` are not accepted (local absolute paths only); if after issuing the child supervisor never dispatches to that worker again, the pointer line is never delivered (the files are already updated). Not tested on real cmux / Claude sessions.

**Verification**: `tests/test_subfleet_boundaries.py` (a v1 contract fleet first as child, then as top, `report_up` -> notice shape -> `[sub-fleet report]` + an open escalation -> revise refused, approve -> ruling file -> `[top ruling]` and `fx:reports_up` cleared; reject sent down; a top-level supervisor carrying `report_up` counts as a consecutive rejection; 4 kinds of bad `report_up` shapes refused in task / service, non-RUNNING refused; service mode can answer `report:`; intermediate levels pass it up unchanged; real private Redis v2: no answer is sent back first without counting, a second miss counts, the record stays open; child supervisor / child worker role texts have it, the top level does not; the line in both supervisor templates); `tests/test_subfleet.py` adoption tests add: the child config's `adopt.label`, the boundary rules in the adopted pane's CLAUDE.md and the child workers' AGENTS.md, and the completion notice naming the generation that supervised (on the hook channel the slot rotates after the collect, and the new generation used to be named).

**Verification of grants**: `tests/test_subfleet_boundaries.py` covers a report with paths -> the top-level message lists the paths -> widening is corrected -> a narrowing approve -> the ruling file carries `who` / `paths` -> the child-side `grants` field, `grant.issued`, the running AGENTS.md / CLAUDE.md rewritten in place (kit frame untouched), the next generation's `role_scope` carries it too, other workers do not -> the next dispatch starts with `[Fleet grant ...]` and only once -> cleared after `finish` and the files restored; an approve without paths issues nothing; the child-side fallback does not widen and ignores another `who`; worker rules; 5 kinds of bad `report_up.paths` (task / service), 2 kinds of bad `escalations[].paths`, paths in an answer when the report had none. `tests/test_long_split.py` covers (both in-decision orders, a busy worker goes through PostToolUse, dispatch correction during the handover), `tests/test_blocked_ruling.py` covers (a ruling after rotation is `blocked.stale`), and the two pi schema tests add `paths`.

## 47. A bridge that gets no settlement receipt is not a failure: late receipts from a busy daemon still settle

Reported problem: typing into a pi supervisor pane while its turn is running gets the supervisor held, and the whole fleet stops dispatching.

**Trigger**: a pi or dsh slot's settled event is processed by the daemon more than 30 seconds after it was sent. This happens when many slots are busy and each daemon round takes 20 to 30 seconds. Typing into the supervisor pane is not the cause.

**The real mechanism**:
- pi session g0: the supervisor finished its `fleet_decision` call at 05:02:41.7 (candidate recorded) → turn ends → the extension's `agent_settled` sends `settled` → the bridge's `event(..., wait=True)` waits for the daemon's receipt, `wait_reply` default 30 seconds.
- At that time the daemon took only **one** event per main-loop round (`RuntimeV2.step` `count=1`) and then ran the whole `tick()` (the watchdog reading the screen slot by slot, etc.). At 05:01:59 the supervisor dispatched to 11 workers at once, busy slots went from 1 to 11, and a daemon round grew from about 1 second to 18–31 seconds (in the audit the Claude workers' `renewed` entries appear in batches at 05:02:20 / 02:51 / 03:15 / 03:33 / 03:51). The supervisor turn's `started` / `candidate` / `settled` queued one after another, and the daemon reached them only at 05:02:51 / 03:14 / 03:33 (lag 31 / 33 / 52 seconds; the previous 33 delivery candidates all lagged 0–3 seconds).
- At 30 seconds the bridge got `pending`, which the old code always treated as "settlement unconfirmed": `fault('settlement unconfirmed/held; no execution replay')` → `mark_uncertain` (the `uncertain` at 05:03:11.9) → when the daemon processed `settled` at 05:03:33 the message was already uncertain → `settle.late` (the supervisor's valid decision was dropped as a late answer) → at 05:03:51 it processed `bridge_fault`: the slot was not yet held → `hold()` pauses by default → `execution.held` + `service.paused`, the whole fleet stopped dispatching, and the pi slot has no hold exit (§24 ⑦-4), so only `start --resume` helped.
- Trigger: the daemon processes a pi / dsh slot's `settled` more than 30 seconds after it was sent. This happens when there are many busy slots (the watchdog reading screens slot by slot stretches each round to 20–30 seconds) and the slot's `started` / `candidate` from the end of the turn are still queued ahead of it. The 04:08 case went fine because only 0–1 slots were busy and the receipt arrived within 1 second, not because of typing or not. This is a third kind of fault that §35 does not distinguish: not a link loss, not a relay of a hold by the daemon, but the bridge itself turning "the receipt has not arrived yet" into a hold.

**What changed** (only the bridge and two plugins; the daemon is untouched):
- `agent_bridge.py`: a `settled` receipt of `pending` (no reply within the wait limit) → no fault, no `mark_uncertain`; it records this call's correlation id as `settling` and keeps the in-flight delivery and the lease. Each round of `renew_loop` first checks the receipt stream of that correlation id: when it arrives it is handled by the original rules (`committed` → send `completed`; `held` / `rejected` → the original fault); likewise, when the message becomes committed it sends `completed` as before, and uncertain / expired fault as before. The `settled` event itself is durable in the event stream (`pending` only comes after a successful XADD), so the daemon will process it sooner or later; while waiting the slot takes no new delivery (`idle_event` is not opened). If the daemon really stopped → the restarted daemon processes this event and then settles, which loses one decision fewer than the old immediate hold. The wait has no limit, consistent with §43 "health has other owners": link loss is covered by the heartbeat / lease (§35), a stalled daemon by §36.
- `pi_extension_v2.ts`: when the `settled` receipt is `pending`, or the extension itself gets no receipt within 35 seconds (the bridge processes commands serially, so this happens when an earlier `operator_input` is still waiting for the daemon, which is exactly "typing into the supervisor while the daemon is busy"), it is likewise treated as late and the status bar shows `已结算，等 fleet 确认` (settled, waiting for fleet confirmation); when the late `completed` frame arrives it reports idle once more (before, idle was reported only once after the `settled` receipt, when the delivery was still in flight, so it could not be reported and the slot would never get its next delivery); `completed` no longer resets an ongoing `manual` phase to idle.
- `dsh_bridge.mjs`: likewise reports idle once more on the `completed` frame. The opencode plugin already reported idle on `completed`, unchanged.
- Manual input mid-turn (not reproduced on a real machine: it was not the cause in the field): by the original §5 semantics, text in the pi supervisor's input box (pi 1.5.0 also goes through the `input` hook during a turn, `streamingBehavior: "steer"`) is turned into an `operator_input` and queued; the current turn is unaffected and it is delivered as a regular delivery after the turn settles. The existing behavior already does this; only a test proving it was added. "Queue" was chosen over "reject": §5 says text a human types in the terminal must not silently disappear, and in task mode with a task running, extra instructions go through the original queue, so queueing is the existing design.

**Verification**:
- New `tests/test_bridge_late_settle.py` (real private Redis + real AgentBridge + real daemon step/pump/tick; the test can stall the daemon to act as a busy daemon): ① a late receipt causes no fault, the message stays received, no `uncertain`; after the daemon is released: committed, the bridge sends `completed`, the slot is not held, the fleet is not paused, no `settle.late`, the supervisor's decision is dispatched to worker-1 and answered, and the supervisor can take its next delivery (old code red: `sup.faulted`); ② a late `held` receipt still holds the slot (old code red); ③ a mid-turn `operator_input` → accepted, the current delivery committed, the typed text then delivered as the next delivery (a confirmation test, green on the old code too).
- `tests/test_pi_extension_v2.mjs` (through `test_pi_v2_contract.py`, the fake bridge gains `settle_pending`): on `pending` no idle is reported and no link-interrupted status is shown; after the late `completed` idle is reported once and the next delivery is taken (old extension red: never gets the state / idle).
- Same mjs: after `settled` is sent the fake bridge does not reply and the extension times out at 35 seconds (cut to 30 ms in the test) → treated as late, idle reported after `completed` (old extension red: the handler throws an unhandled rejection).
- `tests/test_dsh_bridge_plugin.py::test_a_late_commit_reports_idle_again` (the fake bridge returns `pending`; old plugin red).
- Smoke test with `scripts/smoke_pair.sh pi claude task`: the daemon is stopped with `kill -STOP` for about 40 seconds after the supervisor writes its candidate.
