- **Security pack**: every security mechanism is a code gate hung on the local `security.yaml` (not in the roster); a fleet without security.yaml behaves exactly as before (the only approved exception: when a cleaned email body has several lines and the first line is `/...`, it is delivered to the supervisor as `email_body` data and no longer treated as a command; only a single-line `/command` is a command, contracts section 8 "email specifics" D-1). The frozen parts: the envelope gains `provenance` (`text` holds only what the user typed by hand), commands gain `/stop` and a third `/approve` argument, push events gain `security`, audits gain 17 `security.*` names, receipts gain 8, pending records gain `kind / tier / action_hash / action_text / request_id`, plus the new file `channels/security.py` (`parse_security` / `DEFAULTS` / `POLICY_KEYS` / `policy`). All other mechanisms (spotlighting, a tool-less reader, the `claude-pretool` pre-gate and `fx:guard:ticket`, the `/stop` hard stop, the `chain-` audit chain, out-of-band confirmation desk / second_channel / totp, sandbox) are in the code; details in contracts section 8 "security pack". With security on, PreToolUse authorizes the action only by a valid single-use ticket from the user; this is not the program / supervisor answering a delete dialog for the user (section 24 delete classification, D8); the hook baseline without a policy is unchanged. Seams added by P8a: `handle_inbound` takes optional `policy=None` / `stop=None` (return protocol unchanged), `actor.channel_audit` is the single channel audit entry (writing this run's existing v2 audit stream), `base.Channel.link_preview = None`, 17 audit names (including `security.tool_call`, audited but never blocked, guaranteed only on P8j's pi bridge path; other CLIs by capability, with no coverage claim); `/stop`'s `kill` ends the run, which must be started again, and the stop marker is `<run>/state/stop.flag`. **Security freeze batch 1 (contracts section 8)**: `audit_log.uappend` (default on; on macOS it tries to set `UF_APPEND` on the chain file; this guards against mistakes on a tamper-evident chain, it is not tamper-proof and the owner can revoke it; run `chflags -R nouappend` before deleting a run directory); `sandbox.codex_hooks` (default off; Codex is not treated as having a pre-gate until verified on a real machine); `security_source` is pinned when enabled; `fleetmux channel audit --verify`; the canary is planted at `start` and refilled at `start --resume` (a missing / broken / moved file refuses the resume); without a policy the `legacy_view` view is delivered (option M); `stage=allowed` only proves the hook let it through; the ticket key suffix is the local `action_hash`, or `route_hash` when routed; the epoch lives only in `fx:service.stop_epoch`, and any malformed shape fails closed. **Security freeze batch 2**: the stop protocol (contracts section 8 "hard stop `/stop`" Z1-Z10, #17); the confirmation schema (C1-C7, O1, O7); sub-fleet root confirmation and the ancestor chain (`guard.verify_ancestry` is the only check; a sub-fleet without taint counts as clean only when the strict chain check passes and no ancestor carries taint; dropping or altering taint is refused, malformed taint becomes `['unknown']`; X1). Known gaps of the section 53 hard stop `/stop`: (1) each stop request presses Escape at most once per slot; the stop request is persistent but a press on every slot is not guaranteed, and `sent` only means the backend accepted the key; (2) Redis Lua does not roll back; the publish protocol converges by validating before writing + writing the dedup record last + replaying with the same id, so the epoch may be bumped once too often; (3) `pause` / `interrupt` act only on this fleet; sub-fleets are handled only in `kill`'s explicit stop tail through the existing `subfleet.stop_children`, and receipts and docs never claim the whole tree stopped; (4) `Fleet.resume()` does not clear the epoch; every confirmation from before the stop is void after a resume; (5) pressing Escape on a `starting` slot may make the CLI in its start-up dialog exit; (6) in the `kill` beat the audit mirror still runs once; audits after the explicit stop tail are no longer mirrored (P8g's convention); (7) a fleet without `security.yaml`: `/stop` replies `unknown_command`, with no `fx:channel:stop*` key, no `stop_epoch` field and no marker file.
- **Worker sandbox**: set by `sandbox.mode` in `security.yaml`: `off` (default), `claude_native` or `os`. Without a security policy nothing changes. With `claude_native`, a Claude slot gets a `sandbox` settings block with `failIfUnavailable`; it may write only to its cwd, the run workspace and its writable roots; its network is limited to `sandbox.allow_domains` and the private Redis socket; paths in `sandbox.deny_read` cannot be read. With `os`, the CLI runs inside `sandbox-exec` on macOS or `bwrap` on Linux; with no wrapper on the machine, the slot is not started, and the daemon audits `security.sandbox_off` with reason `unavailable`. At daemon start every slot that runs below the policy is audited once as `security.sandbox_off` with its reason: `pi_no_gate`, `codex_hooks_unverified`, `no_native_sandbox` or `os_domains_not_enforced`. With a policy and `sandbox.memory: off` (default), Claude auto memory is turned off in the slot. Whether each CLI honours these settings is not verified on a real machine.
- **Security taint**: `start --resume --no-security` is refused when the run config carries `security_taint` (outside content reached the run). The resume stops and `config.json` is not changed. Resume without the flag, or with `--security PATH`. A sub-fleet takes neither `--security` nor `--no-security`; it follows its parent's policy.
- **Interface changes**: the P0 interface (contracts section 8, `channels/__init__.py`, `base.py`, `envelope.py`, `tests/test_channels_contract.py`) changes only through the supervisor. The commit subject starts with `contract:` and affected adapters are retested. All channel packages are implemented: the actor, lease, queues, CLI and supervision; Telegram, Feishu, WeChat, email and Slack; delete approval, exec push and the daemon-down step; split to a task sub-fleet; and the security pack. The adapter contract rows in `tests/test_channels_contract.py` fail only when an adapter module cannot be imported, and every adapter module is present.

**Verification**: `python3 -m pytest -q tests/test_channels_contract.py` is all green. Ids that start with `red` and tests named `test_red_*` keep their package labels; they pass now. No real network, no real credentials, no listeners opened.

**Known gaps**: residual risk of approving deletes from a phone: the supervisor, workers and daemon run as the same OS user, Claude slots are not sandboxed, and all of them can connect to the same private Redis socket, read and write the run directory and read process memory; such a process that really sets out to forge an answered pending record, or to read the nonce sent to the chat, cannot be stopped. The separate key, the one-time nonce and keeping the nonce out of prompts and audits guard against mis-taps, crossed items, replays and accidental borrowing; they make no claim against a deliberate forgery by a program with the same privileges; anything stronger needs a different OS user or a signed approval service, outside this phase. Inbound confirmation: the platform cursor advances only after the fleet answers (accepted, or deterministically refused; seen is only the actor's dedup claim and does not mean reliable receipt, see contracts section 8 "Inbound acknowledgement, idempotency and replay boundary"); there is no inbox stream, and transient failures never advance the cursor, have no cap and never go to dead letters, so an entry that keeps failing blocks the messages behind it until the fault clears (cap / dead letters / who decides = still open); messages older than the platform's retention (Telegram 24 hours) while the fleet is stopped cannot be recovered. Cursors (Telegram offset, WeChat `get_updates_buf`, IMAP `UIDVALIDITY` + UID) are stored only in the run's private Redis; a brand-new run, a first binding or a `UIDVALIDITY` reset has no usable cursor; how to persist across runs is still open. For email, the first cursor-less start and a `UIDVALIDITY` reset are settled (P5b): the cursor is set to the current highest UID, **no backlog is fetched and nothing is replayed**, and `channel.cursor_reset` is audited (`reason / old / new / skipped`), so the skip is not silent; mail that arrives during downtime under the same `UIDVALIDITY` is still fetched from the cursor. For the other channels, behavior without a cursor must likewise be visible in audits and `channel status`. Catch-up after an IMAP disconnect and whether Feishu redelivers events missed during a disconnect are to be tested on a real machine. Real-link acceptance (a real bot, a real Feishu app, a ClawBot QR scan, a Gmail app password) is outside the automated tests.

**Known limits and real-machine checks**: (1) **Slack acks before forwarding**: a Socket Mode frame is acked before the actor hands it to the fleet; if the actor process dies in between, that frame is lost (Slack does not redeliver acked frames); this loss window is accepted in this phase, and at-least-once is not claimed. (2) **Feishu's default transport is not covered by tests**: the default is `feishu.py`'s own `http_transport` (with response headers), while the tests only use an injected fake transport; real-machine acceptance must really send a file or image once and subscribe to `card.action.trigger`. (3) **Email IDLE not checked against a real server**: the real behavior of Python 3.14 `imaplib` IDLE against Gmail is untested and belongs to the real-machine receive acceptance after delivery; the AR authentication premise is likewise to be verified on a real machine. (4) **Out-of-memory (OOM) behavior of the hard-stop publish is not guaranteed** (contracts section 8 Z5): not measured with a small `maxmemory`; never claim "refused before the script starts, zero writes". (5) **WeChat backoff / email pause make `poll()` return `[]` at once**: spinning is prevented by the actor's `inbound_loop` (`poll(20)` per round, a healthy round sleeps a full 1 second, failures back off 30->300 seconds); WeChat's 3600-second no-API pause after `-14` lives only in the adapter instance's memory, so a separately created `send_direct` instance still calls the API and gets `-14` again. (6) **Feishu quoted originals and merged forwards are not fetched**: only one `file_refused` is returned ("the quoted Feishu original was not fetched; paste it directly if needed" / merged forward); no text is faked. (7) **`prompt_wait` overwrites `fx:service.reason`**: an earlier operator pause stays paused, but its reason text is replaced by the delete dialog's reason (existing behavior, unchanged in this phase). (8) **Throttling**: receipts (`reply`) and `user_only` items are never held, but they count toward the storm window, so a run of receipts can push ordinary items in the same conversation into storm merging; notify_bus's `is_too_old`, synchronous circuit breaker, metrics and business gates were not copied. (9) **Lease files are never deleted**: a normal release clears the holder, so `channel status` does not show the previous holder; only a crashed / killed holder leaves a record, for reclaiming (`channel.lease_reclaimed`). (10) **Default outbound push**: when the actor's `Channel` has no `ch.push`, `DEFAULT_PUSH` applies (escalation off), not all events. (11) **A Feishu merged forward gets two refusals** (recorded, not fixed): P3b already sends a generic refusal for `merge_forward` (Feishu merge_forward messages are not supported yet), and P8b's `feishu_unfetched` adds "merged forward", so the user gets two `file_refused`. (12) **The actor does not retry when the `/stop` marker cannot be written** (not done): when writing the marker fails, the user only gets a receipt ending in "please handle it on the computer"; the actor's inbound loop keeps no request in memory and does not retry each round. (13) **The parent fleet is stopped after a split spawn is accepted but before it is marked `delegated`** (recorded, not fixed): the spawn answer is already `accepted`, but the escalations block of the same beat has not yet moved the record from `approved` to `delegated`; if the parent fleet is then stopped **without `--resume`**, `split_settle_accepted` does not run and `split_finished` only accepts `delegated`, so no `<request>.done` follow-up is sent; a resume with `--resume` goes through S11 and does not have this problem. Future fix: let `split_finished` also accept records that are "`approved` + answer `accepted`".

## 54. Applets: a team's deterministic core, hot-loadable on a root fleet or a named sub-fleet

An applet is a deterministic program that runs as a background process started by its host service fleet's daemon (own session and process group, output in `applet.log`; it outlives a daemon exit and is adopted on resume) and carries a team's custom function, for example a release train. It is hot-loadable: a row in the host roster's `applets:` block attaches it and removing the row detaches it, through the roster hot reload (section 16), without restarting the fleet or touching its workers; the host is a root service fleet or a named service sub-fleet (section 29), and the host's own daemon starts, watches and restarts it. It never calls a model: when it needs model work it asks the host's supervisor for an **applet lease** on one worker slot, drives that worker directly and exclusively for the term, returns it when the job is done, and asks for a renewal if the term runs out first. When no worker is free the fleet wakes a reclaimed worker or hot-adds one (section 16, default worker chain section 52) up to a ceiling, instead of refusing. When a leased worker hands back a fix, its reply goes straight back to the applet: the fleet does not review it (the fix review was removed; replies an older host still held are released once by `applet_ops.drain_reviews`). The mechanism also reuses the daemon's supervision of its channel actor (section 53), the service request with `to` (section 53 "Direct to a worker"), the external-op decision pattern (section 29), clean sessions (section 37), program grants and `report_up` (section 46), and the human-only deletion rule; it adds a roster block `applets:`, the ops and keys under `fx:applet:<name>:*`, and owner fields on the slot hash. Concrete applets are separate projects built on the interface. The one exception is the built-in relay applet `applet-relay-1` (`fleetmux/relay_applet.py`, section 59): a root service fleet declares it itself when `relay.enabled` is true.

**Shown at the daemon's level.** An applet is a peer of the daemon, so the fleet window shows it where it shows the daemon: one tab per applet in the daemon area's tab row (the lower-left group: history, control, then the applet tabs, in roster order). The host daemon keeps these tabs in step with its applet rows on every tick (`fleetmux/applet_view.py`, the `applet_views` part of `Fleet.tick`, right after `applets`): a tab is opened when a row is declared, renamed when the state changes, kept while a detach drains and closed when the row is gone; a tab closed by hand is opened again within 5 s. The title is `<name> <state>` with a leading `! ` for `stalled`, `exited`, `crashloop` and `config_error`, so the state is seen without opening the tab even when the row is crowded. The tab runs a read-only viewer: a fixed header (state, phase, heartbeat age, pid, restarts and crashes, open leases, time of the last state change from the new host field `state_at`) redrawn every second above a live tail of `applet.log`; typed keys are dropped and the applet's stdin is `/dev/null`. A named or attached sub-fleet host opens its applet tabs in its own page, next to its own control tab; the root window shows that page from the sub-fleet's sidebar card. GUI change for this: the titled lower tab blocks take up to 24 title columns (the legacy fixed slots keep 15) and compute their share from the row's width less 7 columns, so the separator grip keeps a free run (`rust/crates/layout/src/hit.rs`). Details: applet-interface.md section 2.6.

**Verification (applet tabs)**: `tests/test_applet_view.py` (title per state; open beside control, rename on a state change, held rows keep the tab, detach keeps it while draining then closes it, roster order, a closed tab reopened, a previous daemon's applet tab closed and roster tabs untouched, nothing before the §31 clear, no dock without a history pane; header fields and refresh only on change; the terminal header above a scrolling region, on the alternate screen; on the real server, the tab's text holds the header once at the top through refreshes, resizes and log lines; the viewer only reads and drops typed keys, also as a real process; a sub-fleet's applet tab in its own page next to its control tab, on the protocol fake server; the root page's lower group keeps one history tab through load, a job sub-fleet that finishes (its record tab stays in its own page), unload and load again); `rust/crates/layout/tests/hit.rs` `titled_lower_fits_applet_titles_and_keeps_a_grip_run`.

- Design (roles, lifecycle, lease state machine, failure modes, security, open questions): [applets.md](applets.md).
- Developer reference (roster schema, process contract, wire protocol, SDK surface, compatibility promises): [applet-interface.md](applet-interface.md).
- Building and deploying a whole team (sizing, per-worker settings, networking, starter project, checklist): [applet-team-guide.md](applet-team-guide.md).

## 55. The fleet on another machine of yours: `fleetmux view` / `fleetmux remote`

The case: the person is on a network where the agent CLIs do not work or should not log in from (a cafe, an
airport), and has an always-on machine at home or at the office. The fleet runs there: every agent
CLI is logged in on that machine and every model request leaves from its network. The machine in front of the
person is a screen and a keyboard. Nothing in the fleet changes; these are two commands and one block.

- **`fleetmux view`** (Python; on the machine that runs the fleet): the live page of this HOME's running fleet in
  this terminal. `fleetmux attach` needs `--runtime D`; `view` reads `latest.json`, and when that run's daemon is
  alive and the run is on the fleetmux backend it execs `fleetmux attach --runtime <the run's runtime>`. No
  `latest.json` or no live daemon: one line (`no fleet is running in this HOME ...`), exit 1, without attach's dump
  of `server.log`. A run on cmux or WezTerm: one line naming `--backend fleetmux`, exit 1 (those screens are local
  windows, there is nothing to attach to). The runtime is found the way `start` found it (`manager_for` on the
  recorded config), so a run started by a release from before section 50, which recorded no session name, is not found;
  that run is watched with the `fleetmux attach --runtime D` line its own `start` printed.
- **`fleetmux remote [--host NAME] [--home DIR] [COMMAND ...]`** (Python; on the machine in front of the person):
  picks a reach entry of the machine, runs `ssh [-t] -- <ssh> '<bin> COMMAND ...'` once on it, and exits with ssh's
  code, which is the remote command's (255: ssh could not connect). No COMMAND = `view`. Any fleet command works
  as typed there: `fleetmux remote status`, `fleetmux remote task - < spec.json`, `fleetmux remote operator ...`.
  `fleetmux remote` knows no subcommand table; a new fleet command needs no change here.
  - **Terminal**: only the no-COMMAND form passes `-t`. With a terminal the remote tty would rewrite a piped
    stdin (`task -`, `request -`).
  - **Quoting**: ssh joins its arguments with spaces and the remote shell splits them again, so the command is
    sent as one string with every argument quoted (`shlex.quote`); JSON, spaces, `$` and `*` arrive as typed. A
    `bin` that starts with `~/` is sent as `"$HOME"/<rest quoted>` (a quoted `~` would not expand).
  - **Both ends run this release.** A far side that does not know `view` answers with argparse's
    `invalid choice: 'view'` (exit 2); `remote` then adds one line saying that machine runs an older release.
  - **Far fleet home**: `fleetmux remote --home <dir> <command>` runs the far command with `FLEETMUX_HOME=<dir>`,
    so `start` there can create a second fleet home. A leading `~/` in `<dir>` is sent as `"$HOME"/`, as for
    `bin`. Every other argument stays quoted, so a `~` in it does not expand on the far side: pass paths relative
    to the far home directory, or absolute. Commands that take `--config` do not need `--home`.
  - **Reach**: one connection procedure on every machine. The machine's `reach` entries are sorted by the route
    kind in `via`, in the order of `fleet.yaml#remote.reach_order` (shipped and built-in default `[thunderbolt,
    lan, overlay, tunnel, tunnel-overseas]`: direct cable, LAN, overlay network direct, ssh through a domestic
    relay, ssh through an overseas relay); entries of one kind keep their list order and a kind not in the order
    goes last (`remote.from_entry`). They are tried in that order with `ssh -o ConnectTimeout=5 <dest> true` (stdin
    closed); the first entry that exits 0 runs the command, once. A 255 from the command is never retried on
    another entry: the connection may have dropped mid-command, and a second run could repeat an `operator` or a
    `start` whose stdin is already consumed. No entry connects: one line naming the entries tried, exit 255.
- **`fleetmux result <id> [--wait <s>] [--config <cfg>]`** (Python; on the machine that runs the fleet): prints
  `fx:result:<id>` as one JSON line, exit 0. While `fx:req:<id>` is open it polls up to `--wait` seconds (default
  0), then exits 3 (pending). Neither key: exit 2 (unknown id). It only reads: `fx:results` keeps every entry,
  unlike `results --drain`. A peer fleet's liaison reads its answer with it over `remote`
  (`docs/distributed-development.md`).
- **Peer role pack** (`fleetmux/agents/peer/`): the liaison role (`liaison.md`), the dev role with its peer-answer
  section (`dev-peer.md`), two example rosters and an example first report. Peer request ids start with
  `peer.<sender machine>.`; the reply schema is `["status", "text", "owner"]`, `status` one of `ask`, `done`,
  `blocked`; each next report carries `to: <owner>`. Forwarding a `peer.` request is forbidden by the roles only;
  the program does not refuse it (`docs/distributed-development.md` open question 2).
- **Hosts** come from the `machines` block of `fleet-superpowers` (`docs/distributed-development.md` "Transport"):
  `machines.<name>.reach` (`{via, ssh}`, tried by the kind in `via`, see "Reach"; `ssh` is a `Host` alias or
  `user@host`, one word that does not start with `-`) and `machines.<name>.fleetmux.bin` (default `~/fleetmux-current/bin/fleetmux`, what
  `install_release.sh --activate` leaves there). An empty `reach` is an error: that machine takes no inbound ssh.
  **fleet.yaml#remote** (in `USER_BLOCKS`, set in the private file) keeps only `default: <machine name>` (shipped
  empty) and `reach_order` (a list of distinct route kinds; a private block without it uses `remote.REACH_ORDER`;
  a bad value is a ValueError naming the key). Without `--host` the machine is `default`, or the only machine whose entry is not `source: self`;
  otherwise an error lists them. A leftover `hosts` key is exit 2 naming the inventory. An unknown machine, an
  empty `reach`, or an inventory file that is not valid (`fleet_superpowers.InventoryError`) is exit 2 with one
  line; a missing file means no machines. The file is read by `fleet_superpowers.machines()`. Both are read once per
  command on a machine that may have no run, so neither is loaded into a run's Redis.
- **Not in scope**: the fleet opens no port, tunnel or VPN and stores no key; an applet loaded from a workflow may
  open its own tunnel or loopback listener (`docs/distributed-development.md`). How this machine reaches the other
  one (keys, a jump host, a forwarded port, a mesh VPN) is the person's own ssh configuration, which `ssh` reads
  as always. A second fleet home on the far side is addressed with `--home`.
  The window form (`gui`) is local only; `remote` shows the terminal page.
- **What it does not claim**: it changes nothing about a provider's terms or account rules. It only fixes where
  the logins and the requests come from: the person's own machine and network, through the official CLIs as
  before (section 11).
- **Tests**: `tests/test_remote.py` (the block: shipped with `default` and `reach_order` and no `hosts`, a leftover
  `hosts` refused; hosts from a `machines` dict: reach and bin, default / the only other machine, bad entries
  refused; entries ordered by kind, list order within a kind, an unknown kind last, the order read from the
  private block or built in; the first kind that connects is used and later ones are not probed; the probe argv; a dead first reach entry falls through to the next; a 255 from the command is never rerun; no entry
  connects = 255 and no command; the inventory file under a temporary HOME: used, unknown machine, empty reach,
  bad file, no file; the ssh argv with and without `-t`; the generated remote line run through `sh -c`
  against a stub binary under a temporary HOME with a space in the path and JSON / `$HOME` / `*` arguments; exit
  code passed through and the older-release line only for the view; `view` attaching, the two no-run cases, the
  backend line), `tests/test_peer_link.py` (`result`: found / pending / unknown exit codes, `--wait` polling,
  `fx:results` unchanged; `--home` through `sh -c`; peer ids and the reply schema pass `validate_request`; the role pack),
  `tests/test_fleet_config.py` (the shipped file has exactly the blocks).

## 56. fleet-superpowers: the per-machine resource inventory

Design: [distributed-development.md](distributed-development.md), section "Resource inventory".

- **File**: `~/.fleetmux/fleet-superpowers/fleet-superpowers.yaml` (per machine; `paths.default_home()`, so it does not follow `FLEETMUX_HOME`; `$FLEETMUX_SUPERPOWERS` names another file). Blocks `models`, `machines`, `quota`, `workflows`, plus the settings `hot.write_back_seconds` and `hot.refresh_seconds`.
- **Code**: `fleetmux/fleet_superpowers.py` (read, validate, locked atomic writes that change one entry and keep every other byte, one-time migration), `fleetmux/fleet_superpowers_refresh.py` (`refresh`), `fleetmux/fleet_superpowers_hot.py` (the hot blocks in Redis), `fleetmux/fleet_superpowers_cli.py` (the `fleetmux superpowers` command table; `fleet.py` forwards `superpowers` to it).
- **Capability keys** (`fleetmux/fleet_superpowers_capability.py`): refresh also writes the capability keys `hardware` (cores, perf_cores, mem_gb, disk_free_gb), `always_on` and `toolchains` into `machines.<self>`. Toolchains are found on the login PATH and only asked for their version. A stored `always_on` is kept. The relay places work by these keys (section 59). Tests: `tests/test_fleet_superpowers_capability.py`.
- **One name**: an entry of the `workflows` block is a workflow, with `kind: workflow`. The commands say the same: `show --workflows` / `--workflow <n>`, `load <workflow>`, `unload <workflow>`. `load` refuses an entry of another kind and prints the stored kind and the fix.
- **Route-aware reach probe**: `refresh` sends the one HTTPS request per probed CLI (claude, codex) the way sessions go: through the `HTTPS_PROXY` of the `session_env` route of a running root fleet on this machine (`fleet_superpowers_refresh.routes_of`), else direct with no proxy from the environment. The URL follows the login: codex logged in with ChatGPT (`discover` keeps `auth_method: chatgpt` from `codex login status`, nothing else) is probed at `https://chatgpt.com/backend-api/codex`, any other codex at `https://api.openai.com` (`fleet_superpowers_refresh.probe_url`); the setup cache has no codex models yet, so no codex row is probed today. Each probed model row records `probe_host` (the URL asked), `via` (the applet or `direct`) and `answered` (`2xx` to `5xx`, or `none`); `reachable` = the host answered, and `can_start_claude` follows it.
- **Model source**: every row of `machines.<self>.models` carries `source: card` (claude: copied from the model cards, never asked of the account, so the account may still refuse it) or `source: listed` (the CLI listed it). No per-model probe exists, so `refresh` spends no quota.
- **Machine name**: the first `refresh` needs `--name` (exit 1 with the suggested short host name and the command), because workflows name machines verbatim and a guessed host name (a `Node7.example` becoming `node7`) would not match them; later refreshes use the stored self name.
- **Daemon refresh**: each root fleet daemon refreshes `machines.<self>` 30 s after start (`START_DELAY`, so its applets are up; a held lock retries after `BUSY_RETRY`, 30 s) and then whenever its `updated_at` is older than `hot.refresh_seconds` (default 600, 0 = off), in a background thread (`fleet_superpowers_refresh.daemon_tick`, tick part `superpowers_refresh`). A non-blocking lock on `<file>.lock.refresh` lets one daemon per machine refresh at a time; probe answers younger than the interval are reused (a `none` never), so no host is probed more often than that; the write goes through the usual file lock. A machine without a self entry is skipped.
- **Cold blocks**: `machines` and `workflows` are read fresh by each command and never enter Redis.
- **Hot blocks**: `models` (the section 52 cache, formerly `<fleet home>/discovered.yaml`) and `quota` (formerly `quota.json`) are loaded into the fleet's Redis at every daemon start and resume, read and written there while the fleet runs, and written back every `hot.write_back_seconds` (default 300) and once at daemon exit.
- **Migration**: a hot block empty in the new file is read once from its old file and written in; the old file stays. `discover.load` / `discover.save` use the `models` block unless `$FLEETMUX_DISCOVERED` names a file.
- **Reach entries**: `machines.<name>.reach` is a list of `{via, ssh}`; `via` is a route kind and the picker tries the entries in the one order of kinds of `fleet.yaml#remote.reach_order` (section 55 "Reach"), never by list position or by who added them. `superpowers add <name> --ssh <dest> --via <kind>` appends one entry to a copy or to this machine's own entry (the owner registers its own ways the same way) and refuses a `via` outside the order, listing the kinds; `--remove-reach` removes one from either. A stored label outside the order (hand-written, or an older `jump` / `reverse`) is valid in the file and tried last. `import` (and so `sync`, on both sides) replaces a copy whole except `reach`, which it merges: the entries already there first, then the owner's new ones, each exact entry once, so a repeat sync changes nothing. `refresh` rereads the self entry's `reach` under the file lock before it writes (`write_self(keep_reach=True)`), so an `add` made while it probes is kept.
- **Cross-machine commands** (`fleetmux/superpowers_peer.py`): `sync`, `save --also`, `load`, `unload`, registered in the command table. `load` checks the commit and the checkout (the checkout may be after `source.ref` when `source.ref` is an ancestor of HEAD and the pinned files, what this machine's applets run, are unchanged between them and in the working tree; `superpowers_peer.pinned_paths`) (`source.repo` is absolute or `~/...`: expanded in this user's home, and fetched as `<reach entry>:~/...` so git resolves it in the home of the user on `source.from`), picks the host fleet from `machines.<me>.fleets` (a sub-fleet has its parent's name, so each row carries `root: true|false` and a sub-fleet row `parent` (the root's name) and `sub` (its id); with several rows of that name the host is the root service fleet, `root: true` and mode `service`, or for a row without `root` from an older refresh mode `service` without `parent_config`, and a refusal when more than one remains), refuses a host roster outside its run directory (a run started with `start --roster` follows its run copy, section 16 "Run copy"), refuses (before writing) while the host's pid record `<run>/applets/<name>/pid` of an applet it would add names a live process (it never stops a process it did not start), appends the rows in place and waits until each added applet is `up` in a new process (a live recorded pid that differs from the one read before the write; a state `up` left by an earlier load does not count); `unload` restores the roster byte for byte from `<run>/superpowers-load/<workflow>.json`, then waits until each removed applet's recorded pid is gone and its state is no longer running (bound: per row `detach_wait_s` + `stop_grace_s` + `STOP_EXTRA_S` = 5, a host sub-fleet `drain_s` instead of `detach_wait_s`, or `--wait`), and at the bound fails naming them and keeps the record so a second `unload` waits again. Without that wait a `load` right after `unload` could put the row back before the daemon saw it go, which cancels the detach and keeps the old process. A loaded workflow survives a restart of the host fleet: `start` of a root fleet loads again each record of the run `latest.json` named before (a stopped root run), and `start --resume --roster` each record of its own run when the copy changed (`superpowers_peer.reapply`, called after `roster.copy_into_run` and before the daemon starts). Only records whose rows were in the old copy, in chain order (`before` = the previous `after`), with the `load` checks (workflow, source checkout, `roster.parse_entries` of the result); each record is written again against the new copy so `unload` restores the right bytes. A record that fails goes to `<run>/superpowers-load/skipped/<workflow>.json` with its reason, is listed in `fleetmux status` as `superpowers_load.skipped`, is tried again on the next start, and is cleared by `load` or `unload` (also when the workflow no longer validates). Any failure of one record, an unreadable inventory included, skips that record and never stops the start. A task-mode start loads nothing (a task fleet hosts no applet) and keeps every record as skipped for the next service start. `start` removes the old run's records once `latest.json` names the new run. `load` and `unload` hand over from a stopped run to the root run its fleet home's `latest.json` names (`superpowers_peer.current`; the inventory row lags about 30 s after a restart), and remove stale records left in the stopped run with a note.
- **Quota path**: the treasurer outside this repository keeps writing `quota.json` (config `quota_file`, else `brake.quota_file()`). The daemon ingests it into the hot `quota` block once after the start-time load and on every tick when its size or mtime changed and its content differs (`fleet_superpowers_hot.ingest_quota`); a missing or unreadable file keeps the last block. Every brake reader in `fleet.py` and `runtime_v2.py` goes through `Fleet.quota_reading()` (`fleet_superpowers_hot.quota`), which reads the hot block only; `QUOTA_STALE` still makes an old pool reading unknown.
- **Releases** (`fleetmux/releases.py`): three releases can differ on one machine. The CLI's (`releases.own`, the code of the process), the installed one (`releases.installed`, the target of `~/fleetmux-current`, which `install_release.sh --activate` moves; the daemons then switch their runs to it, see "Release drift" below) and the running one (`releases.running`): the daemon writes `<run>/release` at every start and resume, so a `start --resume` from another release updates it (an unknown release removes the file); a daemon started before that file existed is read from the release directory in the run config's `python` path, else unknown. A release is the directory name under `fleetmux-releases`, else the checkout's short commit; `releases.same` treats a sha and a longer sha that starts with it as one release. `refresh` writes `machines.<self>.fleetmux.release` = installed (before: the release of whichever process ran refresh, so a daemon of the old release wrote the old one) and `fleets[].release` = running. Checks:
  - `superpowers load` reads the running release of every host fleet it loads into, now (this machine in process, a far one with `superpowers releases --json` over its reach), and refuses before writing when they differ, or when one is unknown and more than one machine is loaded; the message lists machine, fleet, release. `--allow-mixed-release` (emergencies only) prints the same list and loads. Only the calling machine compares: the far half (`load --machine <self>`) does not call back, and the forwarded command carries no new flag, so it works on a far side of an older release. A far side whose `superpowers releases` fails, or that does not connect, counts as unknown. `load --machine <other>` still compares every machine of the workflow.
  - `sync` warns when the two machines have different releases installed; `save --also` only stores a workflow and does not compare.
  - Every command that works on a fleet's config (`fleet.main` after `pin_paths` except `chrome` and `peer`, which worker sessions call through the CLI path of their start, `fleetmux applet`, `superpowers load` / `unload`) prints one stderr warning when the CLI's release differs from the running daemon's, once per run and process; silent when either is unknown or no daemon holds the run's lock. It never refuses. Internal entry points (daemon, actors, hooks) do not check: their stderr is a channel.
  - `fleetmux status` adds `release: {running, installed, restart_needed}` and prints one stderr line while `restart_needed`; `superpowers show --self` (without `--json`, whose output `sync` parses) adds one `#` line per fleet whose release differs from the installed one. Both, and `superpowers releases` without `--fleet`, skip a row whose run has no live daemon lock: right after a restart the stored row may still name the stopped run.
- **Release drift** (`fleetmux/release_drift.py`, `fleet.yaml#release_drift`): a root daemon (`Fleet.tick` part `release_drift`; never a sub-fleet) compares, every `check_interval_s` (600), the release of every process of its run with the installed one: itself, each live sub-fleet daemon (`<sub run>/release`), each live applet process and the fleetmux server (the `<releases root>/<release>` path in their command lines: `~/fleetmux-releases` or `$FLEETMUX_RELEASES`, whatever its name). A process whose release cannot be told never counts as drift. It switches the run when all hold: the drift has lasted `grace_s` (900) since this daemon first saw it; the installed release passes its self-check (directory complete: `fleetmux/fleet.py`, `.venv/bin/python`, `bin/fleetmux` for the fleetmux backend; its venv runs `fleet.py --help`), else no switch and one notice; no `blackout` window (`HH:MM-HH:MM` in `timezone`, '' = the machine's; default none) is open; no worker of the run (root: busy, handoff or leased; each live sub-fleet from its own Redis, its supervisor included: that is a root worker pane, which the root only shows as `delegated`; an unreadable sub-fleet Redis counts as busy) is in a turn: it waits up to `idle_wait_s` (600) for all to be idle, then defers to the next compare, never a hard switch; fewer than `max_failures` (2) switches to that release failed (a refusal at `preflight` / `backup` that stopped nothing, such as no single root daemon or an unreadable `fx:jobs`, is not counted: it says nothing about the release), else only one notice; `<fleet home>/start.sh` exists, else one notice. A `blackout` or `timezone` value that does not validate holds the switch (audited `release_drift.config`). `enabled` (default true) is the master switch.
  - The switch runs in a separate process in its own session (`python -m fleetmux.release_drift switch <run>/release-drift-plan.json`), because it stops the daemon that starts it. Order: `<run>/jobs.json` and the job-runs index from a `fx:jobs` snapshot taken before any daemon stops (an absent key is an empty snapshot; a Redis that cannot be read stops the switch before anything is touched); SIGTERM to the root daemon first (while it lives it collects every sub-fleet daemon that exits; a root that has not exited after `stop_wait_s` ends the switch with nothing else touched), then the sub-fleet daemons (`stop_wait_s`); Redis `shutdown save`; the run runtime's `fleetmux server` / `gui`; nothing of the run left alive, else it does not start; `FLEETMUX_REL=<installed dir> <fleet home>/start.sh --resume <run>` (its output to `<run>/release-drift.log`; its environment is the daemon's without any PYTHONPATH entry inside the old or the new release directory, so the started release imports its own fleetmux, while the switch process itself imports its release through PYTHONPATH) continues the same run (never `fleetmux stop`, which records jobs as cancelled); jobs.json is written again from the snapshot. Then, up to `verify_s` (300), every process of the run must be up on the new release (a sub-fleet that ended with `result.json` is skipped). A failed start or check goes back the same way with `FLEETMUX_REL=<old release dir>`, counts one failure of that installed release in `<run>/release-drift.json` and starts a new grace. `~/fleetmux-current` is never moved back.
  - Notices go to the root supervisor (a queued message) and to the user (the run's human notice route: `lifecycle_close.notify` for a managed root, else `notify_route.unmanaged_send`), event `release_drift`: one before the switch (from, to, the processes to replace, open jobs) and one after it from the next daemon of the run, read from `<run>/release-drift-outcome.json` (replaced processes, jobs back of the snapshot, or the failure reason, whether the run is back on the old release and the failure count). The switch process writes its steps to `<run>/release-drift.log`.
  - An applet of another release than the resuming daemon is not adopted (applets.md 2.3): the owner record carries `release`; not adopted counts as one crash and starts it on the daemon's release.
  - `install_release.sh --activate` prints, last, every running root fleet of the machine (`latest.json` of the default fleet home and of `fleets/*`, with a live daemon lock) whose release differs from the installed one, one line each: when that release's directory (beside the installed one, else from the run's `python` path) has `fleetmux/release_drift.py`, that its daemon switches it within `check_interval_s + grace_s` (25 minutes by default), or that the switch is off; otherwise (a release from before the automatic switch, or a directory that is not found) that it will not switch by itself, with the `start.sh --resume <run>` to restart it by hand.
  - `start.sh` contract: it starts the release named by `FLEETMUX_REL` (else `~/fleetmux-current`) and passes its arguments (`--resume <run>`) to `start`.
- **Tests**: `tests/test_fleet_superpowers.py` (read), `tests/test_fleet_superpowers_write.py` (validation, splice, lock, rename), `tests/test_fleet_superpowers_cli.py` (refresh, commands), `tests/test_fleet_superpowers_hot.py` (migration, Redis, write-back, daemon call points), `tests/test_superpowers_peer.py` (two machines over a stubbed ssh: sync, save --also, load, unload), `tests/test_superpowers_load_persist.py` (a loaded workflow across a fresh start and `start --resume --roster`, skipped records, load and unload right after a restart), `tests/test_releases.py` (release sources, the CLI warning, `status` and `show --self`), `tests/test_release_drift.py` (compare, conditions, switch order, rollback, failure stop), `tests/test_applet_host_release.py` (adopt by release), `tests/test_install_release_drift.py` (the activate hint).

## 57. Session environment from an applet

An applet can fix a network problem by serving a port on loopback, for example a proxy. The fleet then starts the
agent sessions of the listed CLIs with that applet's environment. The full design, its launch-path table and its
open questions are in [distributed-development.md](distributed-development.md), section "Session environment from an
applet". The code is `fleetmux/session_env.py`; the launch and daemon code only call it.

- **Declaration.** An applet entry carries `session_env: {port, vars, clis}` (applet-interface.md section 1.1).
  `fleet.yaml#session_env.verified_clis` ships as `[claude]` and is not in `USER_BLOCKS`. A roster that routes
  another CLI, or routes one CLI through two applets, is refused whole (section 16).
- **Scope.** The root writes the union of its own declarations and those in its sub-fleets' roster files into
  its run `config.json` as `session_env`, at `start` and on every roster reload. A child config copies the key at
  up and never recomputes it.
- **Environment.** For a routed CLI the fleet removes inherited `ALL_PROXY`, `HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY` and
  `NO_PROXY` in both cases, sets `vars`, and sets `NO_PROXY` and `no_proxy` to the applet's value plus
  `127.0.0.1,localhost,::1`. It does this in `fleet.actor()` after the per-CLI update and before
  `sandbox.os_wrap`, in both `env` dicts of the generation settings file, and in the explicit `env` of
  `advisor.run_claude()` and of the channel reader's `run_cli()`. The daemon's own environment and the pane
  command line never carry the variables. Unverified CLIs are never routed, even when a config lists them.
- **Fail closed.**
  1. **Launch wait.** `fleet.actor()` connects to `127.0.0.1:<port>` before exec. While it is closed the slot
     hash has `route_wait=1` and the actor probes again every `wait_interval_s`. When it answers the actor clears
     the flag, moves `startup_deadline` forward by `profile_ready_timeout` and execs. A retired generation or a
     dead slot ends the wait.
  2. **Daemon.** A starting slot with `route_wait=1` is no startup timeout (as `advice_hold`): no retry spent, no
     new generation.
  3. **No model-chain step.** A refused proxy shows transport errors (`ECONNREFUSED`, `connection error`). They
     match no pattern in `fleet.yaml#walls`, so they never move a slot down its chain (section 23).
  4. **No delivery.** Before `Fleet.pump` hands queued work to an idle routed slot (supervisor or worker), it
     probes the port. Closed: `route_wait=1`, the queue stays as it is, no attempt is spent and no generation
     starts. The first pump after the port answers clears the flag and delivers the oldest item first.
  5. **Mid-turn loss.** Before the StopFailure backoff release (`stopfail_tick`) and before a watchdog Escape or
     rotation (`Watchdog.check`, `Watchdog.rotate`), the daemon probes the port. Closed: `route_wait=1`, the slot
     stays held, nothing is re-delivered and no generation starts. A StopFailure while the port is closed spends
     no attempt (task mode) and adds nothing to the streak (service mode). The release follows on the first tick
     after the port answers.
  6. **Bridge CLIs.** No bridge CLI is verified, so `Fleet.network_up` is unchanged. Change it to answer per
     slot when one is verified.

## 58. Jobs on a root fleet: each job runs in its own task-mode sub-fleet

Decision: both modes stay; work that has an end and a deliverable runs as a task. On a service fleet, submit it with `fleetmux task`: the program runs each job in its own task-mode sub-fleet. The program does every step below; nothing depends on the supervisor's prompt or judgment.

- **Both kinds of `start`.** A task-mode `start` while a root fleet runs becomes a job (its stub's `task`, §19); a service-mode `start` becomes a service sub-fleet of a root service fleet instead (§29 "Attached service sub-fleets"), which takes requests, not jobs. A task root takes jobs only, while its own task runs (§19 "Task root").
- **Entry.** `fleetmux task <spec>` against a root service fleet (transport v2) is a job. The daemon validates the spec and answers `{"status": "accepted", "job_id": "job-<10 hex>", "task_id": <same>}` at once. `fleetmux request` and typed supervisor text stay as in §5. On a task-mode fleet `fleetmux task` is the fleet's own task (`Fleet.submit`) while none runs, and a job while its own task runs (root only; §19 "Task root"); `task --job` is only ever a job. `Fleet.submit` itself still refuses a service fleet.
- **Job spec.** Only these keys are accepted:

  | Key | Meaning |
  |---|---|
  | `goal` | Required. The sub-fleet's task. |
  | `hours` | The sub-fleet task's time budget. |
  | `artifacts` | The sub-fleet task's artifacts (paths in the sub-fleet's workspace). |
  | `budget` | The sub-fleet task's budget card (§23). |
  | `lifecycle` | A task lifecycle for the sub-fleet, or null (default). |
  | `workers` | Number of sub-fleet workers. |
  | `supervisor_profile` | Card the slot runs on as sub-fleet supervisor (a claude card). |
  | `worker_profiles` | One card per sub-fleet worker. |
  | `name` | The sub-fleet's display name: its title, sidebar card and `status` row show `<name> · M<n>`. |
  | `roster` | Absolute path of a roster YAML. The child's workers, role files and cards come from it, as for a named sub-fleet (§29). The child follows its own copy (`<child run>/roster.yaml`), hot (§16). Not with `workers` / `worker_profiles`; no `subfleets`, `channels` or `applets`. |
  | `roles` | Absolute path of a directory of `<agent>.md` role files, appended to each child agent's rules as `start --roles` does for a root. |
  | `supervisor` | Model chain for the sub-fleet supervisor ("Model chains" below). |
  | `worker` | Model chain for the sub-fleet workers ("Model chains" below). |
  | `tree` | Absolute path of a tree JSON file. It seeds the job sub-fleet's decomposition tree, renumbered into the child's level (section 62). A relative or missing path is refused at entry. A section 60 redo of the job does not carry it. |
  | `deliver` | With `lifecycle: dev_task`, required: the same block a root dev_task task spec takes (`repo`, `feature_branch`, `deploy_branch`, `test_cmd`, `test_timeout_s`, `queue`); an older spec's `review_file` is accepted and ignored. |
  | `review_file` | Removed. An older job spec may still carry it: accepted, never carried to the child. |
  | `overrides` | Fleet config keys of the job's sub-fleet that replace the root's values (`subfleet.OVERRIDES`: `supervisor_split`, `request_timeout`, `max_busy`, `max_attempts`, `worker_idle_reclaim_min`, `stall_*`, `handoff_ratio`, `handoff_timeout`, `window`, `claude_window`, `clear_timeout`, `lease_reclaim`, `worker_model`, `claude`, `worker_kit`, `worker_domains`, `brake_notify`, `allow_profile_live_switch`). A join stub sends the options its `start` typed, plus `worker_kit: null`, `worker_domains: []`, `supervisor_split: false` when not typed, so the root's kit, domains and supervisor split never reach the job. |

  Any other key is refused. These problems are refused at entry, so a job that can never start never waits forever:
  - **Run problems.** Not a v2 root fleet, a task root with no task running, a backend without sub-fleets, a run without `adopted_supervisor_hook_delivery`, no Claude Code worker.
  - **Spec problems.** Unknown cards (also the roster's), a supervisor card that is not a claude card, more workers than a `limits.child_workers_max` set in the root config or roster, a worker-card count that differs from `workers`, a relative or missing `roster` / `roles` path, a bad `deliver` shape (`lifecycle_deliver.validate_deliver`), `deliver` without dev_task, dev_task without `deliver`.
- **Configuration.** One block, `fleet.yaml#job_subfleet`, which the private file may override as a whole. A job spec overrides any key for its own job.

  | Key | Default | Meaning |
  |---|---|---|
  | `workers` | 2 | Sub-fleet workers. |
  | `supervisor_profile` | null | null: the supervisor chain picks the model ("Model chains" below). |
  | `worker_profiles` | [] | []: the fleet's default worker model. |
  | `hours` | 4 | The sub-fleet task's time budget. |

- **Record.** The job is a request hash `fx:req:<job id>` (status `job_queued`, later `dispatched` to its slot) plus a row in `fx:jobs`. A service root mirrors `fx:jobs` to `<run>/jobs.json` on every change and names itself in `<fleet home>/job-runs/<run dir name>.json` while it holds open jobs, so a later `start` sees them while the run's Redis is down. Every published job result is also kept in `<run>/job-results/<job id>.json`. It is never in the request queue `fx:reqq`. Because of that, the supervisor never gets a routing prompt for it, `request_timeout` never expires it, and a resume or a lost slot never replays it as a request. While the hash exists, `fleetmux result <job id>` answers pending. A request whose `to` names the slot of an open job is refused.
- **Pause.** A root pause stops `Fleet.job_tick`. Pause review (section 24) lifts a pause whose cause is gone; queued jobs start on the next tick. A sub-fleet marked as waiting for a human (section 24 "Supervisor BLOCKED on a sub-fleet notice") does not pause the root, so jobs keep starting.
- **Slot.** `Fleet.job_tick` is a part of the daemon tick and runs outside v2 transactions. It takes queued jobs, oldest first. For each job it takes the first active worker that passes all of these checks:
  - no applet lease;
  - no split in `approved`, `rotating` or `delegated`;
  - no request dispatched to it and nothing queued for it;
  - `subfleet.check(ready=True)` with the job's worker cards;
  - the `supervisor_profile` gate, when one is set.

  **No such worker: the program adds one.** It reuses the applet supply writer (`applet_ops.write_worker_row`): one `- {name: worker-<n>}` row goes into the root roster (and the night roster), and the roster reload (§16) starts the slot. The job records it as `hot_slot`; no other job, request or dispatch may take it, and the next `job_start` makes it the job's sub-fleet supervisor once it is idle and READY. One added worker per job. When the job ends (result published, failed after `JOB_TRIES`, or gone), the row is removed and the reload retires the slot once it is idle. If another worker comes free first, the job takes that one and the added row goes at once. If the reload refuses the file with the new row, the row is removed and the job waits. No worker is added, and the job waits in the queue as before, when: a reclaimed worker can be woken instead (never a reserved sub-fleet supervisor slot, section 68), the run has no roster file (or no block-style `workers:` list), a tree-wide cap is reached, or `limits.job_hot_add_max` (root roster or config, hot; default no cap, 0 allowed) workers are already added for open jobs. The reasons show in `status` under `waiting` (and `added_worker`), and the next tick tries again. A queued job is never handed to the supervisor or to a worker.
- **Caps.** Jobs have no default `max_children`, `max_agents_total` or `child_workers_max` (`subfleet.limits(cfg, job=True)`: None = no cap). A root config or roster `limits:` value caps jobs as before. A capped job waits before it takes a slot: the count includes jobs whose sub-fleet is approved but not spawned yet, so capped jobs start in submit order. Supervisor and escalate splits keep the defaults 3 / 12 / 3. `max_depth` is unchanged. While a job is queued the service lifecycle reclaims no idle worker, and it starts the next generation of one reclaimed worker per queued job (`lifecycle_hold.service_tick`); a reclaimed slot that a declared sub-fleet reserves is not woken for a job (section 68).
- **Numbers.** A job takes the next free `M<n>` that has no `sub/` directory. A failed spawn leaves its directory, so a retry gets a new number and never hits `max_attempts` on the old one.
- **Sub-fleet.** The program writes the §44 split record itself: `M<n>`, `proposal: []`, `request` and `job` set to the job id, no `[escalate]` question. It then approves the record with `escalation_apply`, using the job's workers and cards. From there the §44 path runs unchanged: rotation when needed, the spawn request with the job's `hours`, `lifecycle`, `artifacts` and `budget`, adoption, and a task-mode child with the job goal as its task. Approval does not depend on `supervisor_split` or `split_judge`.
- **Refusals.** None of them go to the supervisor or to a worker. If the program refuses the approve, or the spawn is rejected (for example, the slot is no longer idle), the split record and its `M<n>` row are dropped. The slot gets its own model back next generation. The job returns to the queue. After `JOB_TRIES` (3) such returns, the job is published `failed`.
- **Lifecycle inside the child.** The child config carries `job: <job id>` and the job's lifecycle. Because of that marker, `Fleet.submit` in the child validates and keeps `deliver` like a root run, and `lifecycle_close.tick` runs the mode's running checks (`hard_timeout_min`) and the completion gate (`completion_problem`, which calls `lifecycle_deliver.deliver`: clean feature worktree, `test_cmd`, fast-forward of the deploy branch, queue entry). A failed gate turns the child task blocked before result.json is written; the gate record goes into result.json as `delivery`. Closing, hold and notifications stay with the parent (`lifecycle_close.managed` still skips sub-fleets).
- **No review step.** The gate looks for no verdict file and counts no review rounds; the program records no review slot and writes no `REVIEWER.md`. Review is a step of the supervisor (the merge read in `supervisor.md`), not of the program.
- **Recheck (`fleetmux task --recheck <task id | job id> --config <config>`, `lifecycle_recheck`).** Runs only the delivery gate again on the files as they are now, for a dev_task task or job that the gate blocked. It dispatches no work.
  1. The CLI reruns the gate from files: the run's own task from its Redis (the daemon holds it), a job from the gate record in its sub-fleet's `result.json` (the record keeps `spec` and `slots`; records written by older releases lack them and are refused).
  2. The daemon checks the target again and, for a passing record, checks that its sha is still the feature branch tip and re-reads done_when (git + queue); the test run is taken from the CLI's record. It stores the record. On pass, the own task turns completed and the next tick closes the run; a job's sub-fleet result turns completed and the job result is published again as `ok` (`publish(replace=True)`). On fail, the task or job stays blocked with the new reason.
  3. Refused: a running task or job, a task blocked by anything other than a failed gate (supervisor BLOCKED, cancelled, timeout). On a join stub, `--recheck` is forwarded to the root.
- **Order.** The sub-fleet record keeps the job's submit time (`submitted`); `subfleet.children_dirs` sorts by it, so the sidebar lists job sub-fleets in submit order even when their spawns run in another order.
- **Result.** A job publishes one result, when the sub-fleet is collected. There is no early `delegated` answer and no `.done` id. Status `ok` for `completed`, `blocked` for `blocked`, `failed` for every other outcome (budget exhausted, failed, cancelled). The result object carries `reply` (the child's reason), `job`, `subfleet`, `outcome`, `reason`, `run`, `workspace`, the child's `artifacts` and, after a lifecycle gate, `delivery`. A non-ok result also carries `error`. The slot returns to the pool through the normal collect.
- **Notices.** The supervisor gets two notices for a job: one when it is accepted and one in the collect notice when its sub-fleet ends. On a service root both notices are bound to the job id with kind `followup`, so they are delivered after the job's request hash is gone. On a task root the accept notice is a plain supervisor message.
- **Guard.** A service decision is invalid (a strike, with the reason sent back) when it does any of these:
  - dispatches, answers or skips a job id;
  - answers the `M<n>` of a job's sub-fleet in `escalations`;
  - dispatches to the slot that supervises a job's sub-fleet.

- **Stop.** A plain stop of a service root never ends its jobs. `stop --final` cancels every open job: each is published `failed` with outcome `cancelled` and never resumed (section 60). No job starts while the root stops. A running job's sub-fleet gets a suspending stop (`{"kind": "stop", "suspend": true}`): the child keeps its task `running`, writes `result.json` `suspended` and exits; the root collects it as `suspended`, publishes nothing and keeps the row as `suspended` (same `M<n>`, `run`, `suspended_at`). A sub-fleet that already ended by itself is collected and published as before. A return to the queue caused by the stop or by the next resume does not count toward `JOB_TRIES`. While a job is open, the service lifecycle does not count the fleet as idle (`idle_stop_hours`).
- **Restart.** The next `start`, `start --resume auto` or `start --resume <dir>` in that fleet home continues a service root run that was stopped (or died) with open jobs, instead of starting a new run (section 17 "Entry"). After the rows are reconciled, section 60 compares the `jobs` kind of the baseline snapshot with `fx:jobs`: a job that ran and is now suspended is listed as restored by the program, a job that is gone without a result is listed as lost, and a cancelled job is listed in the report only. The supervisor rules on each listed item.
  - The resume reconciles the rows first (`Fleet.job_resume_reconcile`): rows a newer run took over (`jobs.carried.json`) are dropped; a row in `jobs.json` that the reloaded Redis lost is put back, never one with a result in `fx:result` or in `job-results/`; cancels written while the root was down (`job-cancels/`) are applied; a running job sub-fleet whose delegated session is gone (a reboot or the stop ended the fleet's tmux) is suspended. When its daemon still runs it gets a suspending stop and is collected as `suspended` once it exits; nothing is relaunched or handed on while it lives.
  - Queued jobs start again in their original `created` order. A suspended job is handed on by `job_start` like a queued one, to the same `M<n>`: `subfleet.readopt_child` delegates a fresh slot the way `spawn_child` does, reloads the child's Redis from its AOF, moves the child task's deadline forward by the suspended time, rewrites the child config's `adopt` block (`readopt: true`) and launches `daemon <child config> --resume`. The child's resume adopts the new session as its supervisor (role change, READY) and gives it the resume brief (section 17); the resumed child also runs section 60 under its own fleet key `job-<job id>`, and its reconcile ledger is handed to the adopted supervisor once it says READY (the root's `jobs` kind stays the authority on whether the job runs); its workers get their next generations; its workspace, worker directories, branch and outputs are the same. Its own sub-fleets were stopped (not suspended) with it; the brief lists their work as not done.
  - Never twice: a job starts no sub-fleet while one of its sub-fleets is active or its daemon holds its lock; exactly one suspended sub-fleet is handed on; anything else fails closed.
  - Not carried: a job whose roster, roles or repository is gone, or whose suspended sub-fleet lost its config or Redis directory, is published `failed` (outcome `not_carried`, the reason in `error`) and reported once on the root's outbound channel (`lifecycle_close.notify`, guarded by `fx:jobs:notified`). Section 60 also lists such a job, and a started job that a new run publishes `not_carried`, as an errored item in the restart ledger; the supervisor rules on it, and `redo` submits it once as a new job with the spec it was submitted with.
  - A run that cannot be continued (its config unreadable or not v2, this release refuses it, its Redis directory gone or its Redis does not load) hands its open jobs to the new run: on first boot the new run takes each never-started row over (queued, or starting with no sub-fleet) with its id and `created`, and publishes each started one `failed` (`not_carried`: its sub-fleet belongs to the old run). The old `jobs.json` becomes `jobs.carried.json`, the tombstone that keeps the old run from starting them again. A retryable refusal (a rejected flag, security, the backend) exits non-zero and leaves the jobs in place.
- **Cancel.** `fleetmux task --cancel <job id> --config <root config>` (event `job_cancel`), and `stop` on a join stub, cancel one job: a queued, starting or suspended job ends at once with a `failed` result (outcome `cancelled`) and is never requeued; a running one gets the cancel recorded on its row and its sub-fleet a plain stop, so neither a root stop nor a restart can suspend or resume it. While the root is down, the stub writes `<root run>/job-cancels/<job id>.json`, applied before anything is resumed.
- **Cancel without the root.** `fleetmux cancel <job id>` sends nothing to the root. It stops that job's sub-fleet with `stop --config <sub-fleet config>`, after checking that the sub-fleet's `parent_config` is this root. The root then collects the sub-fleet and publishes the job `failed` with outcome `cancelled`, so it also works on a root started from an older release. A job with no sub-fleet yet is reported as pending; `--wait` retries every 15 seconds.
- **Status.** `fleetmux status` of a service fleet, and of a task root with open jobs, has `jobs`: one row per open job with `id`, `state` (`queued` / `starting` / `running` / `suspended`), `slot`, `subfleet`, `goal`, `waiting`, `created`, `name` and `added_worker`.

**Known boundaries.** The restart rules above are for service roots: a task root's stop is its task's end, and its jobs fail with a result (section 19 "Task end"). A plain `start` that continues a run applies only the options `--resume` applies (`--roster`, `--max-attempts`, `--lease-reclaim`, `--extend-hours`, `--no-restore`, security, `--advisor`) and a changed `--supervisor-model`, `--supervisor-pi-model`, `--supervisor-pi-thinking` or `--supervisor-profile` when the run's supervisor CLI reads that option (section 60); every option it does not apply is printed and listed as an errored item in the reconcile ledger. A job sub-fleet's own sub-fleets are stopped, not suspended, with it. On a task root, the root task's `hours` deadline keeps running while its COMPLETED is held for jobs; a root BLOCKED or timeout cancels its open jobs; a `start --resume` of a task root does not pin a job split that was approved or rotating (`split_pin_on_resume` is service-only). The handoff prompt of a rotating job slot (`handoff.SPLIT_PROMPT`) asks for an approved split plan, which a job does not have. An applet lease can still take a slot that a job holds in `approved` or `rotating`. There is no `reviewer` key and no review step: a job's work is read once by its supervisor when it merges. Uncapped job sub-fleets still count toward the default caps that supervisor / escalate splits and applet hot-add see (a busy tree of jobs can refuse those). The dev_task gate runs synchronously in the child daemon's tick, as in a root run (up to `test_timeout_s`).

**Model chains** (fleetmux/job_chain.py, fleet.yaml#job_subfleet `supervisor` / `worker`). The program reads the
configuration and picks the job's supervisor and worker models itself. A job roster row with `pi_model` or
`pi_thinking` is refused at submit (`job_chain.submit_problem`).

- **Keys.** `fleet.yaml#job_subfleet` has two more keys, `supervisor` and `worker`. Each is a model chain written like
  `discovery.supervisor` / `discovery.worker`: a list of model patterns, highest priority first. null (the default)
  means: use the discovery chain. [] is an error. A job spec may give its own `supervisor` / `worker` chain.
  `overrides.worker_model: X` in a spec counts as the spec worker chain `[X]`.
- **Layers.** Resolve each role in this order: job spec, `job_subfleet`, `discovery`. Take the highest layer that
  gives a chain or a card for the role. A chain there voids a card in a lower layer; a card there voids a chain in a
  lower layer. A value of null counts as not given.
  - Supervisor: one layer may not give both a chain and `supervisor_profile`. Refuse it.
  - Workers: one layer may not give a worker chain (`worker` or `overrides.worker_model`) and a non-empty
    `worker_profiles`. Refuse it: `worker_profiles` cover every slot, so the chain would never be used. Only roster
    rows mix: a row with a `profile` runs its card, the other rows run the chain. A spec with a `roster` ignores the
    block's `worker_profiles`.
  - A spec that gives both `worker` and `overrides.worker_model` is refused.
  - `job_chain.effective` returns both chains, both card settings and the layer of each. Use only it.
- **Resolve.** Resolve a chain with `discover.chain` only: the probe cache, `only_via`, `cli_order`. Pass no extra
  models. A model that no pattern names is never used.
- **Pick a worker** (`job_chain.pick_worker`). Take the first chain entry that passes all of these:
  - no wall recorded for it in this run (`brake.wall_action`: account wall, model-pool wall);
  - its CLI is claude or pi (section 51);
  - not pi when the job has card slots (section 51: a pi sub-fleet takes no cards);
  - the cli_pairs row `claude supervisor x <cli> workers (task)` is verified with no missing adapter;
  - the caller's `refused` callback lets it through (pi start gate, model quota).
  Write the result into the child config: `worker_cli: pi`, `pi_model`, `pi_thinking` (from
  `discovery.worker_thinking`), or `worker_cli: claude`, `worker_model`. A Claude worker takes its effort from
  `discovery.worker_thinking` at launch, as before.
- **Pick the supervisor** (`job_chain.pick_supervisor`). Take the first chain entry with no wall that runs official
  Claude Code and that `refused` lets through. Skip every other CLI and record why: a job supervisor is an adopted
  Claude Code pane. Use the Claude card for that model when there is one: the card with id `claude-<model>` first,
  else the first card by id. Without a card, start the slot with the overrides a card would give
  (`worker_cli: claude`, `worker_model: <model>`).
- **Submit.** `job_chain.submit_problem` refuses a job at submit, with every reason in one message, when:
  - a roster row sets `pi_model` or `pi_thinking` (the message points at the spec `worker` chain);
  - the layers are invalid (see Layers);
  - the job has slots without a card and the worker chain has no usable entry;
  - the supervisor follows a chain and the chain has no usable Claude entry.
  Submit ignores walls and quotas. For each pattern that resolves to nothing, `job_chain.explain` gives the reason:
  CLI not in `cli_order`, not installed, version too old, not logged in, `only_via`, gateway offline, no matching
  model, or no probe cache (`run fleetmux setup`).
- **Record.** `job_chain.trace(pick, source, switched)` gives one slot's record: `cli`, `model`, `source` (`spec`,
  `job_subfleet`, `discovery` or `card`), `profile`, `skipped` (pattern, cli, model, reason of every entry passed
  over before the pick) and `switched` (wall switches). Store it as
  `model_chain = {'supervisor': trace, 'workers': {slot: trace}}`.
- **Walls.** A job's sub-fleet supervisor that hits a wall stops, as before. Record
  `supervisor wall: stop (adopted pane cannot rotate)` (`job_chain.SUPERVISOR_WALL`). Workers move down the job's
  own worker chain through the existing wall path.
- **Private files.** A private `job_subfleet` block replaces the shipped block whole. A private block that sets
  `supervisor_profile` and has no `supervisor` key keeps its card: the missing key is null.
- **Tests.** `tests/test_job_chain.py`: block keys and shape, layers, submit refusals (incident replay with a
  `pi_model` roster row), worker and supervisor picks, walls, the `refused` callback, card slots, `explain` checked
  against `discover.chain` over eight cache states, and the trace shape. The probe cache, pair table and cards are
  stubbed in every test.
- **Wiring: submit** (`Fleet.job_submit`). The spec keys `supervisor` and `worker` are in `JOB_KEYS`. After the
  spawn fields are checked, call `job_chain.submit_problem` with the raw roster rows and `refused=Fleet.job_pi_gate`
  (the section 51 gate: the pi binary and `--allow-unsandboxed-pi`). A problem refuses the job; nothing is queued.
  `Fleet.job_settings` takes the cards and both chains from `job_chain.effective` and stores them in the job's
  `settings` (`chains`, `chain_source`).
- **Wiring: workers at start** (`Fleet.job_worker_pick`, called by `Fleet.job_start` before a slot is taken).
  Slots with a card keep it. For the other slots call `job_chain.pick_worker` with this run's walls (`Fleet.walled`)
  and a `refused` callback: the section 51 pi gate, then the model quota of those slots
  (`model_usage.default_problem`). No usable entry: the job stays queued, `waiting` gives the reason, and no worker
  is added. Otherwise store `worker_overrides`, `job_chain` and the worker traces in the job record (`chain`);
  `Fleet.job_spawn_args` passes them to the spawn. `subfleet.job_worker_cfg` builds the child's worker keys from the
  pick: it drops the parent's `worker_cli`, `worker_model`, `pi_model`, `pi_thinking`, `pi_models`, `pi_thinkings`
  and `pi_home`. `subfleet.check` runs the section 51 pi gate for a pi pick and counts the quota on those keys.
  `subfleet.child_config` writes the pick after the forwarded `overrides`, so neither a slot's own model nor the
  root's `worker_cli` replaces it, and writes `job_chain` and `model_chain`. A job queued by an older daemon (no
  `chains` in its settings) runs as before.
- **Wiring: supervisor at start** (`Fleet.job_supervisor_pick`, per candidate slot in `Fleet.job_start` and for
  the added-worker check in `Fleet.job_hot_add`). A supervisor card (spec or `job_subfleet`) goes through
  `Fleet.split_supervisor_launch` as before. Otherwise call `job_chain.pick_supervisor` with this run's walls and a
  `refused` callback that runs `Fleet.card_gate` on the model card of each entry for this slot: a model at its
  `max_concurrent` is skipped and the next entry is taken. When the slot already runs the pick
  (`discover.place`), approve with no model: the slot is adopted in place. Otherwise approve with the pick's card
  (`supervisor_profile`), or with `supervisor_model` when the model has no card. `split_supervisor_launch(slot, None,
  model)` builds that launch: `profile_id` empty, overrides `worker_cli: claude`, `worker_model: <model>`; it runs the
  model card's `card_gate` and the CLI preflight like a card launch. `model_usage.slot_card` counts a launch with an
  empty `profile_id` by its own overrides, so the model's ticket is held. No usable entry for any slot: the job stays
  queued with the reasons in `waiting`.
- **A job with no chain and no card.** The supervisor is the first usable Claude entry of `discovery.supervisor`; the
  slot rotates once unless it already runs that model. The workers run the first usable entry of `discovery.worker`.
  A forwarded `start --worker-model X` is the chain `[X]` and must resolve on this
  machine. To keep a slot's own model, set the chains in `job_subfleet` (the tests use the fixture
  `job_chains_as_before`).
- **Every sub-fleet walks the supervisor chain.** A section 44 split approved without
  `supervisor_profile` takes its supervisor from `discovery.supervisor` through the same `Fleet.supervisor_chain_pick`
  (see section 44); with no usable entry the approve is refused. A named sub-fleet follows section 29.
- **Fable has no slot cap.** `models.fable.max_concurrent` is null, like opus and sonnet.
  Astra and Sol keep 1. Tests that need a full model use a stubbed card (fixture
  `fable_capped`), not the shipped value.
- **The supervisor chain is walked in order**: when Astra and Fable are not usable, take Opus, then
  Sol, then DeepSeek Flash. Only Claude entries can supervise a job, so non-Claude entries are skipped with
  their reason. There is no allowed-model list.
- **Wiring: no fallback to a default model.** No usable chain entry at start (walls, quota): the job stays queued,
  `waiting` gives the reason, no worker is added for it, and nothing starts. A roster the child would refuse fails
  the job at once, with the reason in the published result; it is not queued again (`JOB_TRIES` does not apply) and
  the supervisor has no way to let it through. Check it twice, only for the first roster read of the run:
  - `subfleet.spawn_child` runs `subfleet.job_roster_problem` on the child config before the child Redis starts
    (the checks of `Fleet.reload_roster` that depend on the child's worker runtime: pi rows, pi models, cards and
    pairs). A problem raises a refusal that starts with `subfleet.ROSTER_REJECTED`; `Fleet.job_release` publishes
    the job `failed` on that marker.
  - In the child, `Fleet.reload_roster` of a job sub-fleet that has not accepted a roster yet (`fx:roster.accepted`
    unset) fails the child task (`Fleet.job_roster_refused`, also run by `roster_tick` when the task arrives later)
    and sets no `rejected` line.
  A later hot edit of the child's roster that the reload refuses keeps the old behavior: the roster in effect stays
  and the supervisor is told (`[Roster rejected]`).
- **Wiring: trace.** `model_chain` (`{'supervisor': trace, 'workers': {slot: trace}}`) is built in `Fleet.job_start`
  and stored in the job record. Read it in these places:
  - the child's `config.json` (`subfleet.child_config`);
  - the `model_chain` field of each `status` job row (`job_rows`; a join stub's `status` passes the row through);
  - the child's `result.json` (`save_result`);
  - the published job result (`Fleet.job_finished`).
  A card slot has `source: card`. Every switch is recorded once in `<child run>/model_chain.json`
  (`Fleet.chain_switched`, read back by `Fleet.model_chain` and `run_model_chain`):
  - a worker's wall switch (`Fleet.wall_switched`): `from`, `to`, `reason`, `at`;
  - a stop of the adopted supervisor on a wall (`Fleet.on_wall`): `to` null, reason
    `job_chain.SUPERVISOR_WALL`.
- **Wiring: walls in a job sub-fleet.** The child config carries `job_chain` (the job's effective patterns; null for
  a role that a card decides). `Fleet.wall_chains_load` replaces the `discovery` patterns with it, so a worker wall
  moves down the job's own worker chain through `wall_decide`, `wall_mark` and `wall_refused`, as for any run. The
  adopted supervisor still only stops.
- **Advisor and the worker a job adds.** Never hold the worker that `job_hot_add` adds for a
  queued job behind the advisor's first sheet. `Fleet.spawn_ready` treats it like a sub-fleet `supervisor_slot`: it is
  `hot_slot` of an open job in `fx:jobs`, so `advice_hold` stays 0 and `launch_ready` is 1. Reason: `job_start` adopts
  the slot only once it is idle and READY, and no delivery, so no first sheet, comes to it before that. Held, the
  slot waited for ever and the job stayed queued. Any other new worker is still held. Test:
  `tests/test_job_hot_add_advice.py`.

**Verification.** `tests/test_service_job.py`, `tests/test_job_cancel.py`, `tests/test_job_queue_restart.py`, `tests/test_job_queue_restart_start.py`, `tests/test_job_queue_restart_join.py`, `tests/test_task_root_job.py`, `tests/test_job_chain.py`, `tests/test_job_model_chain.py`, `tests/test_job_hot_add_advice.py`. Restart ledger behaviour for jobs: `tests/test_restart_scenario2_jobs.py` (section 60).

## 59. Relay development: one job across several machines
