  work in flight is delivered again where the usual rules allow (`RuntimeV2.redeliverable`). `stay` keeps its launch,
  `next` / `head` walk the role's wall chain (`brake.wall_action`, the pair table, the CLI preflight), a card uses the
  card resolver.
- `{"clear_wall": "<cli>:<model>", "in": "self" | <id>}`: drops that record from `fx:walls`; an account wall drops
  every record of the account. The chain then starts from its head again.

Every item uses the existing program checks (limits, card gates, the pair table, the budget line); the real-money and
delete confirmation rules are not touched. A failure is answered `refused: <reason>`.

### Pauses

- A start that fails before READY pauses a service fleet with the cause `{"kind": "startup", "slot": <slot>}` (the
  owner is recorded, never read from the reason text).
- While the run is paused by a slot failure or a wall (cause kind `startup` / `wall`), `Fleet.pump` delivers the
  supervisor's next message only when it is a heal notice (`heal.pump_paused`); nothing else is delivered or
  dispatched. Operator, budget and watchdog pauses stay closed; notices wait and go out on resume.
- When a reopened slot is READY, `heal.lift_owned` lifts exactly the pause that slot owns (startup cause of that
  slot, or the brake's `paused_by` wall of that slot), never past a budget line.

### No self-regeneration of a sub-fleet supervisor

In a sub-fleet the daemon never starts a new generation of its supervisor by itself: `RuntimeV2.regen_on`,
`Fleet.wall_chained` (any wall kind, any generation) and `Fleet.startup_retry_tick` exclude it; it stops and the
parent is told. A root fleet is unchanged (the checks use `parent_config` / `adopt`). Kept as before: a named
sub-fleet's context rotation before an up and the re-up of an autostart child after its delegated session was lost
(the latter now also tells the parent).

### The brake reads only new text

At every unpause (`Fleet.resume`) each slot's screen is kept as a baseline (`wall_baseline`, per generation). The
wall gate and the stalled-screen read then count a wall only when a wall line lies in a stretch of the screen that
is new or changed against that baseline (`brake.wall_after`, line alignment): text that was already on the screen is
old, while the same wall text printed after newer output is a new wall. A new generation has no baseline.

### A wall is named, never quoted

Any text a supervisor reads names a wall as `<slot> hit a <scope> wall (<cli>:<model>, resets <time>)`
(`brake.wall_phrase`; the reset part only when the CLI's line states a clock time or a short date, optionally with
a zone in parentheses: `brake.wall_resets`, nothing else of the line is taken). The brake reason itself is built
that way (`Fleet.on_wall`), so the pause reason, `[sub-fleet walled]` and a `[sub-fleet stuck]` that cites the
pause carry no CLI line; the raw line stays in the trip record's `wall` (brake.json, audit). Every notice to a
supervisor goes through one helper, `heal.tell`: it replaces any CLI wall wording left in the text (an API error
text, a held reason, a legacy child's pause reason, a sub-fleet's end reason) by `hit a <scope> wall[, resets
<time>]` (`brake.unquote_walls`) and queues it. Without this, a notice quoting a child's wall line put that line on
the parent supervisor's pane, where the parent's own wall gate read it as the parent's wall and paused the parent.

### Delivered text on a screen

A fleet delivery drawn on a pane is not the CLI's own output: a wall line inside it never counts, while the CLI's own
wall row after it always does. The extent of a delivered block is judged by position only (rows and indentation,
`brake.delivery_rows`), never by comparing screen text with what was delivered:

- `walls.delivery_screen` (fleet.yaml) lists, per drawing shape, `head` (the row that opens a delivered block),
  `body_indent` (every further delivered row starts at that column or right of it) and `cli_row` (a row the CLI
  draws itself, by its leading glyph).
- A block runs from a `head` row over the following non-empty rows at `body_indent` or right of it. It ends at the
  first non-empty row left of the body indent or matching `cli_row`, whatever that row is: an unknown row shape
  ends the block and is never swallowed. No head on the screen, no block.
- Any ambiguity counts as the CLI's own line: a row whose wall match starts within `brake.AMBIGUOUS_LEAD`
  characters of its text is not taken as delivered, so a plain CLI wall row at the body column still trips.
- The wall gate (`brake.screen_wall`), the position rule (`brake.wall_after`) and the stalled-screen read
  (`brake.own_text`) all skip the delivered rows; the position rule above is unchanged.

Shipped shape: the demo / scripted actor (a `U+25C0` head row, body at column 2, its own rows led by `U+25B6`,
`U+273B` and the rule). Claude Code is not measured and has no shape: one live capture shows the tail of a
delivered text drawn at column 2 (two consecutive rows; whether the second is a wrap or a line break cannot be
told), but its head row was scrolled off and the row that ends the block is not identifiable, so no shape can be
written from it. The same capture shows a turn that failed
at once: a `U+23FA Stop hook feedback` header row followed directly by the CLI's own wall under the result glyph
`U+23BF` at column 2 (continuation at column 5); `tests/fixtures/delivery_screens/claude-stop-hook-feedback.txt`
keeps that geometry, and that wall row must trip. Until a whole Claude delivery block is measured, every wall line
on a Claude screen counts, including one quoted inside a delivery. Codex, opencode and pi are not measured and have
no shape either. The shape list is part of the shipped walls block; a private walls block without
`delivery_screen` gets the shipped value and `[]` turns it off.

### Tests

`tests/test_parent_heals_child.py` runs real fleets (`fleet.py start`, real daemons, private Redis per fleet) with
scripted agents (`tests/parent_heals_actor.py`) on `tests/fake_fleetmux.py` (`tests/parent_heals_kit.py`): a dead
named sub-fleet supervisor slot, a job worker wall, an adopted supervisor wall, stale wall text after a resume, the
root supervisor path unchanged; a delivery quoting a raw wall line on the root's pane while its turn stays in flight
(the root does not brake); plus an in-process test of a level-2 parent and one that runs every notice builder on a
wall reason and checks that no notice quotes a CLI wall line. `tests/test_brake.py` checks the gate on drawn rows: a
quoted wall line inside a delivered block does not trip, and the CLI's own wall row (every wall shape in the
repository, under its CLI's block shape, under a headless indented stretch and at the body column of a block) does.
The fake terminal honours cursor addressing and erase-line, so panes read back as the rows a real terminal shows.

## 68. Idle reclaim prerequisites: the hook keepalive survives sleep, an unattended mailbox wakes its session, a reclaimed named supervisor slot is woken, jobs keep off reserved slots

The service lifecycle's idle reclaim (`worker_idle_reclaim_min`, `lifecycle_hold.service_tick`) is unchanged: an idle worker's screen is saved and its session exits; work for it later starts its next generation. Four mechanisms around it make the switch safe to keep on.

### 68.1 Hook keepalive on a clock that counts sleep

- Claude Code times a hook on wall time, so the time the machine slept counts. Python's `time.monotonic()` stops in sleep on macOS (section 35). A keepalive deadline on it fell after Claude Code had already killed the hook, and the session's mailbox lost its hook.
- `claude_hook.clock()` is a clock that keeps counting while the machine sleeps and never steps with the wall clock: `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` on macOS, `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` on Linux, wall time elsewhere. Its values are used only inside one hook process and are never stored.
- This is a deliberate exception to "every deadline is in awake time" (section 35). The hook's counterpart is Claude Code's own wall-time timer, not a fleet wait.
- The Stop hook (`fleet.claude_stop` -> `claude_hook_next` -> `claude_hook.wait`) and the asyncRewake fallback (`fleet.claude_rewake`) take their start and deadline from `claude_hook.clock()`. The rewake's short GRACE wait stays on `time.monotonic()`.
- Keepalive point: `claude_hook.deadline(cfg, started)` = `started + hook_timeout // 2` (`claude_hook.margin` = the other half, at least `MARGIN_MIN`). The hook timeout stays `claude_hook_timeout` (86400 s). The keepalive is sent if the machine is awake at any moment of the second half. A wake after the point finds the deadline passed and answers at once, before Claude Code's overdue timer.
- `claude_hook.wait` re-reads the clock between BLPOP slices of at most `POLL` seconds, so one long block cannot carry it past the deadline.
- Cost: two keepalive turns per idle Claude session per day instead of one.
- Limit: nothing runs while the machine sleeps. One continuous sleep that covers the whole second half (12 h with the default timeout) still loses the keepalive; 68.2 wakes the session then.
- A run picks up the new keepalive point from the release its hooks run from; the hook timeout in the session's settings is unchanged.

### 68.2 Wake of an unattended mailbox

- A delivery to a hook-channel Claude session goes to its generation's mailbox. `armed=false` right after a settle is normal: the next Stop hook takes the entry. If no hook is left (keepalive lost, Escape, a crashed hook process), the entry used to wait for the stall path or for `handoff_timeout`.
- `RuntimeV2.hook_unarmed_tick` runs every tick after the watchdog (none while the run is paused). `RuntimeV2.unarmed_idle(slot)` names a session as really idle only when all of these hold:
  - it is READY, the delivery was offered and received (`busy` / `handoff`), and its id is in this generation's mailbox;
  - there is no confirmation dialog, background task, API-error backoff or slash / queued-input wait;
  - no Stop hook is waiting, settling or yielded (`claude_hook.live`);
  - the last hand-over of this session has its Stop proof (the same test as the keepalive claim), so no turn can still be running;
  - the transcript ends at a finished assistant turn (`transcript_turn_done`), and the pane is alive.
- The same mark for `UNARMED_WAIT` (2 x `claude_hook.GRACE` = 20 s) wakes the session: `RuntimeV2.hook_wake` audits `hook.unarmed_wake` and calls `watchdog.rotate(reason='unarmed')`. A live rewake fallback acts after GRACE and wins first.
- The wake never types into the input box (section 48): the session is killed and resumed as the next generation (`claude --resume`, start prompt as a CLI argument, as in section 43).
  - A worker, or the direct supervisor (which also gets its revive summary), resumes its own session.
  - The untaken message goes back to the front of its queue with the same replay value, so no attempt is used (`UNARMED_RESUME` lead). The old generation's mailbox key is deleted.
  - An untaken handoff is offered again once the resumed session is READY (`handoff_again`, `RuntimeV2.offer_handoff_again`), so the model writes its note.
- Failure paths (no wait for `handoff_timeout`):
  - A resumed worker that never says READY falls back to a fresh generation from the skeleton note (`RuntimeV2.resume_fallback`).
  - A session with no resumable transcript gets the fresh successor of the frozen path at once.
  - A resumed direct supervisor that never says READY takes the existing supervisor startup-failure path (dead + blocked, or regenerated).
  - An adopted sub-fleet supervisor (its pane belongs to the parent fleet) is held and reported at once (rotate's adopted branch): the child daemon may not kill the parent's pane, and no channel lets the parent resume it without breaking the hook route.
  - A paused run wakes nobody.
- Latency: 20 s detection, plus launch, plus READY.

### 68.3 A reclaimed named supervisor slot is woken before its sub-fleet comes up

- A roster `subfleets:` entry (section 29) comes up through `named_subfleet.try_up`: an approved `subfleet.up` (`op_up`) or a due autostart (`tick`). It needs its `supervisor_slot` idle and READY. The idle reclaim can take that slot back while the sub-fleet is down, and nothing else wakes a reclaimed slot without a message or a queued job.
- `try_up`, after the limit and card checks: a slot in state `reclaimed` is woken once (`named_subfleet.wake_slot`):
  - `RuntimeV2.respawn` starts its next generation, the same call the lifecycle uses; the roster card is unchanged;
  - the generation is stored as `slot_wake` in `fx:subfleet:<name>`, and `subfleet.slot_wake` is audited;
  - the up waits (reason prefix `SLOT_WAKE`) until that generation is READY, then goes on as before (supervisor chain, rotation gate, delegation).
- The woken slot is `starting`, so it is not woken twice. After READY its activity is fresh, so the idle reclaim does not take it back within `worker_idle_reclaim_min`.
- Counting: the wake and READY fall inside the op's existing `SLOT_WAIT` (600 s from approval). A timeout answers `slot busy: supervisor slot woken: ...` and names the wake. The wait reason is kept as `blocked` for `status`. For autostart it is the `blocked` reason and one `subfleet.autostart_wait` audit (the text is the same each tick).
- A paused run does not wake the slot; the up waits with that reason.
- Cost: an up of a reclaimed slot is later by one launch + READY than an up of an idle one.

### 68.4 Jobs keep off the supervisor slots of declared sub-fleets

- `Fleet.job_slot_busy` refuses every slot that a declared sub-fleet names as its `supervisor_slot`: roster `subfleets:` entries and attached children (`named_subfleet.reserved`). It refuses the slot also while that sub-fleet is down. The reason reads `supervisor slot of named sub-fleet <name>` (or `attached`).
- So `job_start` takes another idle worker or, with none, adds one (`job_hot_add`, section 58).
- `job_hot_add` no longer waits for a reclaimed worker that is reserved: a reserved slot would never be given to the job.
- The job wake in `lifecycle_hold.service_tick` wakes only a reclaimed worker that no sub-fleet reserves. A reclaimed reserved slot is still woken for its own pending messages and by 68.3.

## 69. Switch baseline: what a normal start turns on, checked by every daemon

Each machine says once which feature switches a fleet of each shape runs with. `start` takes those values when no flag is typed, and every daemon compares its running switches with them and reports or restores what differs. Code: `fleetmux/baseline.py` (the block, the precedence rule, start / resume / attach), `fleetmux/baseline_check.py` (the daemon check, the user's approval, the release switch check). Tests: `tests/test_baseline_block.py`, `tests/test_start_baseline.py`, `tests/test_baseline_check.py`, `tests/test_baseline_upgrade.py`, `tests/test_install_release.py`.

- **Where**: the `baseline` block of this machine's fleet-superpowers.yaml (section 56), next to `models` / `machines` / `workflows`; never a release file, so installing or switching a release does not touch it. People write it; no code path writes it (it is no hot block, never flushed back). The daemons read it through a Redis read cache (`fx:baseline:cache`) that is refreshed when the file's stamp (mtime, size) changes, so an edited line counts at the next check.
- **Format**:

      baseline:
        version: 1
        check_every_s: 300              # the daemon check interval (default 300)
        machines:
          <machine>:                    # the key of this machine's `machines` entry with `source: self`
            root_service: {advisor: true, supervisor_split: true, worker_idle_reclaim_min: 30}
            job: {lease_reclaim: on}
        exceptions:
          - {machine: <machine>, shape: root_service, fleet: <name>, switch: worker_idle_reclaim_min,
             value: off, reason: "<why>", until: <YYYY-MM-DD or ISO date and time>}

- **Shapes**: `root_service` (a root fleet in service mode), `job` (a section 58 job sub-fleet, and a task-mode attaching start), `named` (a roster `subfleets:` entry), `attached` (a service-mode attaching start), `split` (a sub-fleet a worker split off). `subfleet.child_config` writes `baseline_shape` into every sub-fleet config, because `job` / `named` keys may be inherited from the parent's config. A root task fleet has no shape and is not checked. A sub-fleet is named by its own name (its roster entry) or its task id; an exception's `fleet` matches that.
- **Switches** (`baseline.REGISTRY`; any other key is an error):

  | switch | lives in | takes effect | the daemon does |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `advisor` | the run's roster | hot | restores it (`Fleet.set_advisor` with the roster write) |
  | `max_busy` | the run's roster / config | hot | reports it (no program writer; set `max_busy:` in the roster) |
  | `supervisor_split` | config.json | restart | reports it; the user may approve a restart |
  | `worker_idle_reclaim_min` | config.json (else the fleet.yaml mode table) | restart | reports it; the user may approve a restart |
  | `lease_reclaim` | config.json | restart | reports it; the user may approve a restart |
  | `worker_kit` (set / not set) | config.json | new run | reports it with what to do |
  | `worker_domains` | config.json | new run | reports it with what to do |
  | `kit_fleet` | config.json | new run | reports it with what to do |

  The daemon never edits fleet.yaml (the mode table) and never edits a run's config.json in place.
- **Precedence** (`Block.resolve`, used by start, start --resume and an attaching start): (1) a switch under a live exception takes the exception's value and is neither reported nor applied; (2) a new run: a typed flag starts as typed, and a value against the baseline prints one `deviates from baseline` line (start is never refused); (3) start --resume: a typed flag that resume applies today (`CONTINUE_APPLIED`) applies as before and is compared the same way; (4) a typed flag that resume ignores stays ignored; (5) everything else takes the baseline. start --resume writes the baseline value of `supervisor_split`, `worker_idle_reclaim_min` and `lease_reclaim` into the run config and prints one line per change, so any restart, also a keeper's `start --resume auto`, brings them to the baseline; `worker_kit`, `worker_domains` and `kit_fleet` are not changed by a resume (they need a new run).
- **The daemon check**: the tick part `baseline` (after `lifecycle`, isolated like every part) runs on every daemon, root and sub-fleets at any depth, every `check_every_s`. Each daemon checks only itself, against the table of its own shape. There is no switch that turns the check off; to keep a value, write an exception.
  - A hot switch with a writer (advisor) is set back at once and reported as restored. So an `--advisor` typed against the baseline at start is restored by the first check; to keep it, write an exception.
  - Any other deviation is reported once: which switch, the running and the baseline value, which fleet (name, shape, machine, config path) and what to do (the approval command, the new-run command, or the roster key).
  - Dedupe: one report per fleet, switch, running value and baseline value (state `fx:baseline:state` in the fleet's Redis). When the value is back, one `back to normal` report. A new running value is a new report.
  - Exceptions: a switch with a live exception is not checked. An exception whose `until` has passed is checked again and reported as usual. A date-only `until` is valid through that whole day in the local date of the machine the daemon runs on; from the next day it has expired. Exceptions are compared with the clock at check time; nothing writes them back.
  - A block that cannot be used (bad YAML inside it, an unknown key, an exception without `reason` or `until`): only the baseline is off. The root reports it once; nothing is checked; the tick and the other blocks (models, workflows, applets) go on. The next check is not left at the default interval: a tick checks at once whenever the inventory file's stamp (mtime, size) differs from the one last read, so a fixed block is checked on the next tick.
  - A block without an entry for this machine (`machines` without `source: self`, or no `baseline.machines.<self>`): the root reports it once; nothing is checked.
  - An inventory file without any `baseline` block: a root service fleet tells the user once per run (not its supervisor: only the user can define a baseline); task roots and sub-fleets write the audit row `baseline.none` only, as does a machine without the inventory file.
- **Where reports go**: a root fleet queues the report to its supervisor (marked information only: it has nothing to rule on) and tells the user through the run's outbound path (`blocked_scope.push`: the lifecycle channels of a managed root, else the run's channels, else counted as undelivered). A sub-fleet writes `<parent run>/subfleet-notices/baseline-<id>.json`; the nearest parent queues it to its supervisor (information only) and passes it up; the root tells the user. Approval always stays with the user.
- **Approval of a restart**: a restart switch that start --resume applies gets the answer key `baseline:<switch>` at its own fleet. The user runs `fleetmux answer --config <that fleet's config.json> baseline:<switch> approve` (the report prints the exact command with the run's own fleetmux) from a terminal outside the run directories. Against a fleet that has ended (its Redis cannot be reached) it prints `rejected` with one sentence saying so and exits 2, with no traceback. It is refused when run from an agent pane of that fleet or of any fleet above it (the working directory inside one of their run directories; a best-effort check, not an isolation boundary). The key is never an escalation, so no supervisor decision JSON at any level can rule on it. On approve:
  1. the fleet's own daemon stops the way SIGTERM stops it: the loop ends, no `result.json` is written, the run, its Redis and the agents' panes stay (a sub-fleet first sets `launched` in its record, so its parent does not collect it during the restart); the stopping daemon runs no further check and sends no report (the next daemon checks);
  2. the user's `fleetmux answer` process waits up to 60 s for the daemon to let go of `daemon.lock`, then runs the existing `start --allow-development-v2 --resume <run>`, which writes the baseline into the run config and keeps the live worker sessions (Claude / Codex workers whose CLI still runs are reattached; the others get a new generation). The supervisor always gets a new generation (section 17: a supervisor is never reattached), except that a resumed sub-fleet keeps an adopted supervisor whose session still runs;
  3. the next check reports `back to normal`. A resume that fails prints the command to run again. If the deviation is still there after an approved restart (a resume that did not run, a daemon started another way), it is reported again and the answer key waits again.
  One approval restarts the fleet once; every restart switch of that fleet that waits for an answer takes the baseline with it.
- **Reject**: `fleetmux answer ... baseline:<switch> reject` only stops the reports about that same deviation. The next restart of that fleet (any start --resume) still applies the baseline. To keep a value for a while, write an exception with a reason and an until.
- **Release switch**: `fleetmux superpowers baseline check [--before HASH] [--before-report FILE] [--json]` is read-only. It prints the sha256 of the `baseline` block as written, the workflows loaded into each running fleet of this machine (`machines.<self>.fleets` rows whose daemon holds its run lock) and the deviations their daemons report. With `--before` it fails (exit 1) when the hash differs; with `--before-report` (the `--json` output taken before) also on a new deviation or a workflow no longer loaded. `scripts/install_release.sh --activate` runs it with the new release before and after it moves `~/fleetmux-current`; a failure after the move puts the previous target back and fails the install. The logic is `baseline_check.upgrade_report` / `upgrade_problems`, for an upgrade command to call as well.

## 70. dev_task delivery through the local rollout

A passing dev_task gate hands its work to the local rollout instead of moving the deploy branch itself. The rollout
merges, builds and installs; fleetmux only submits and records the request id. The older queue delivery stays as an
option.

- **Switch**: `deliver.via` in the job spec wins; without it, `fleet.yaml#deliver.via` (shipped `rollout`; the
  private file may set its own `deliver` block). `validate_deliver` resolves the value and writes it into the stored
  spec, so a recheck runs the same delivery; a stored spec without `via` was written by an older release and stays
  a queue delivery. The relay renders its specs with `via: queue` (it judges "delivered"
  from the queue file) and keeps its behaviour under any default.
- **Spec, via rollout**: `repo`, `feature_branch`, `deploy_branch`, `test_cmd`, `brief` (absolute path of the task
  brief) and `rollout: {verify, target?, title?, commit_message?, restart?}` are required; `queue` is not. `verify` is the
  acceptance script path inside the repository; `target` names the rollout target when more than one fits;
  `restart` is `none` or a list of service labels, passed as `--restart` (the rollout asks for it when changed files
  belong to no service's import closure; without it the rollout computes the set); `title`
  and `commit_message` override the ones taken from the branch's commit subjects: the request title is the oldest
  subject (plus `(+N more)` for several commits); the squash commit message starts with that subject as written and,
  for several commits, lists every subject in its body. A message is held to the rollout core's shape check (a string
  without NUL, at most 20000 characters, a non-empty first line) at entry for `commit_message` and before the submit
  for a generated one, which then names `deliver.rollout.commit_message`; content rules of the registry's own
  commit message check come back as a refused submit. `test_cmd` is copied into the
  origin, so it is held to the origin's limits at entry (at most 50 arguments of at most 400 characters, no control
  characters). An optional `refix` object
  (`root`, `attempt`, `previous`, `evidence`) is the rollback chain a refix job carries into its next delivery.
- **Order**: (1) the feature worktree is clean and on the feature branch; (2) when the deploy branch head is not
  already in the feature branch, the feature branch is rebased onto it; a failed rebase is aborted and the gate
  fails with the branch left where it was; (3) `test_cmd` runs on the rebased head, and the gate fails when the head
  moved while the tests ran, so the tested sha is the submitted sha; (4) the sha is submitted. The deploy branch is
  never moved by fleetmux.
- **Registry**: `$ROLLOUT_TARGETS` when set, else the first existing file of `fleet.yaml#deliver.registries`
  (shipped: `~/.fleetmux/rollout-targets.yaml`, then `~/rollout-site/registry.yaml`). The target is the `kind: git`
  entry whose `root` is a worktree of the same repository as `deliver.repo` (same git common directory); with several,
  the one whose `live_branch` is the deploy branch, or the one `deliver.rollout.target` names.
- **Client**: the registry's `site.client: {cwd, argv}`. fleetmux runs `argv + ["submit", "--repo", <repo>,
  "--target", <target>, "--request-id", <id>, "--sha", <sha>, "--verify", <verify>, "--title", <title>,
  "--commit-message-file", <file>, "--origin-file", <file>]` (plus `"--restart", <label>` per `restart` entry and
  `"--session", <job>` for a job) in `cwd`, with `ROLLOUT_TARGETS=<registry>` and
  `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1`, for at most `fleet.yaml#deliver.submit_timeout_s` seconds. Both files are written to
  `<workspace>/.deliver/` (`origin-<id>.json`, `commit-<id>.txt`).
- **Origin** (the request field `origin`, checked again by the rollout): `v` (1), `job` (the job id, or null for a
  root fleet's own task), `task`, `brief`, `branch`, `repo`, `host` and `host_source` (the inventory's `source: self`
  machine, else this host's short name), `root_config` (the root fleet's config.json), `test_cmd`, `verify`, `refix`
  (the spec's chain, else a new one rooted at this request), and when known `name` (the job name), `roster`, `roles`.
  `roster` and `roles` are the run's own config values: for a job's sub-fleet `roster` is the run's copy of the job
  roster (`<run>/roster.yaml`, with any rows its supervisor added or removed), because the run keeps no path to the
  file the job spec named; `roles` is the directory the job spec named.
  `restart` is written only when the spec has `deliver.rollout.restart`, as written there (`"none"` or the list of
  service labels); the rollout copies it into a refix task's `deliver.rollout.restart`, so the next delivery
  passes the same `--restart` values.
- **Request id**: `<job>-<sha12>` for a job's sub-fleet. A root fleet's own dev_task has no job:
  `<task>-<sha256(run dir)[:10]>-<sha12>`, the task id cut to `[A-Za-z0-9._:-]` and to at most 56 characters, so the id
  is at most 80 characters. The same task and sha give the same id; two runs with a task of the same name do not
  collide.
- **Done**: the submit exits 0 and its last JSON line says `enqueued` or `exists` for the same request id. The gate
  record keeps `rollout: {target, request_id, status, registry}`; `done_when_problem` and the daemon's recheck
  (`lifecycle_recheck.confirmed`) accept the record only for that sha with a request id. The closing notice names the
  target and the request id and says the change is not merged yet.
- **Blocked**: no registry, no `site.client`, no matching `kind: git` target (or more than one), a submit that cannot
  start, runs past its time, exits non-zero, or answers another status or another request id: the gate fails with
  that reason (the stderr tail for a refused submit) and the job is blocked. No queue file is written as a fallback.
  A submit past its time may have been taken: the reason names the request id and the client's `--status` command,
  and says not to recheck when the rollout holds it (a rerun after the deploy branch moved submits another sha under
  another request id). A rerun lists the request ids this workspace submitted before for the same task (their origin
  files in `.deliver/`) in the record (`rollout.earlier_request_ids`) and in any blocked reason.
- **Review**: the supervisor prompt's merge-read rule covers this hand-off (the Fable supervisor reads the diff once
  before merging or handing the delivery to the local rollout). A supervisor not running on Fable borrows one read: a
  roster row with the `claude-fable` profile reads the whole diff once and writes the list of errors to a file, a
  non-empty list goes back to the author for one fix, and the row is deleted. Where that cannot be done (a run without a
  roster file, a supervisor CLI x claude worker pair that is not verified, Fable at a usage wall) there is no read and
  the delivery goes on as usual. No program gate waits for it.

## 71. `fleetmux upgrade`: the manual entry of the release switch, the upgrade scenario and the rollout hookup

`fleetmux upgrade` moves a running fleet tree (the root, every sub-fleet at any depth, the applets) from the release
it runs to another one with one command, and checks the result before it returns. It is not a second mechanism: the
root daemon's release drift (section 56, "Release drift") and this command hand the same plan to the same switch
(`fleetmux/release_drift.py`, `switch`). `fleetmux scenario upgrade` proves the switch on a real tree in an isolated
world, and the rollout of a new release runs that scenario before it switches the machine.

### 71.1 Invocation

```
<to>/bin/fleetmux upgrade --to <release> [--config <root config> | --all-running]
                          [--verify-timeout <s>] [--stop-timeout <s>]
<to>/bin/fleetmux upgrade status --to <release> (--config <root config> | --all-running)
<to>/bin/fleetmux upgrade running [--config <root config>]
```

- `--to` is a release directory, a sha (or a unique sha prefix) under the releases root, or `version:<v>` (the
  installer's `<releases root>/versions/<v>` link, `releases.resolve`). The directory must hold `.venv/bin/python*`,
  `fleetmux/fleet.py` and `bin/fleetmux`. The releases root is `$FLEETMUX_RELEASES`, else `~/fleetmux-releases`; a
  directory right under it is a release named by its directory, whatever the root is called (`releases.release_of`,
  `releases.from_python`).
- `--config` is the root fleet's `config.json` (default: this machine's root fleet). `--all-running` moves every root
  tree whose root daemon runs on this machine, one after the other; a failed tree does not stop the others, and the
  exit code is the most severe of the trees.
- The command is run from the new release (`<to>/bin/fleetmux`), so the old release does not need to know it.
- It first moves itself into a session of its own: the parent waits and exits with the child's code, and the child
  keeps writing to the inherited stdout.

### 71.2 Steps and their order

The steps are `release_drift.STEPS`, in this fixed order; each prints `upgrade: <step>: ok|FAIL <detail>`:

1. `preflight`: the root config, the release `--to` names, a process for every daemon that holds its lock, and the
   switch locks (see 71.5). A refusal here changes nothing.
2. `backup`: one read of `fx:jobs` and of the state the checks compare with, written to
   `<fleet home>/upgrade/<UTC time>/before.json` and `fx-jobs.json`, outside the run directory. A Redis that cannot be
   read stops the switch before anything is touched.
3. `stop_root`: SIGTERM to the root daemon, then wait for its lock.
4. `stop_children`: SIGTERM to the sub-fleet daemons level by level; a level is stopped only after every parent above
   it has let go of its lock.
5. `stop_applets`: the applet processes. SIGTERM ends a daemon, not its applets, and the next daemon would adopt a
   live copy on the old interpreter.
6. `restore_files`: `<run>/jobs.json` and the job-runs index, written from the snapshot of step 2, never read again
   after the stop.
7. `redis_down`: `SHUTDOWN SAVE` of every Redis of the tree, until none answers.
8. `terminal_down`: the run's `fleetmux server` and window. The fleet's own tmux keeps running.
9. `start_new`: every config of the tree (root and every sub-fleet) gets `python` = the new release's interpreter,
   then the same run continues: `FLEETMUX_REL=<to> <fleet home>/start.sh --resume <run>`, or, without start.sh,
   `<to>/.venv/bin/python <to>/fleetmux/fleet.py start --resume <run>` with the fleet's home and state. The start
   environment drops every `PYTHONPATH` entry inside the old or the new release, puts `<to>/bin` first on `PATH` and
   sets `FLEETMUX_BIN`, so the new release imports its own code. Named sub-fleets without autostart are asked up
   again.
10. `verify`: the checks of 71.3, polled until `--verify-timeout` (default 300 s).

Why the root stops first: while a root daemon lives, it collects every sub-fleet daemon that exits as a sub-fleet
that died, and closes its job. Stopping the children first therefore loses the jobs they run. The root is stopped
first, and each lower level only after the level above it has gone.

Every daemon gets SIGTERM only, which ends its loop without cancelling anything. `fleetmux stop` and the stop event
retire every slot and record the jobs as cancelled, so the switch never uses them. This is also how a tree that runs
a release without `fleetmux upgrade` is moved: the command comes from the new release, and the old daemons only need
to end their loop on SIGTERM, which every release does.

Actors, hook lines and history panes are started with the config's `python`, so switching the configs is enough for
everything started after the switch. A CLI session that was already attached keeps its hook lines until its next
generation.

### 71.3 Checks: not worse than before the switch

| Check | Holds when |
|---|---|
| `daemons` | The root daemon and every sub-fleet daemon that ran before hold their locks on the new release, and so does the `fleetmux server` if one ran before. A sub-fleet that ended during the switch is skipped. |
| `interpreter` | Every daemon process, every config's `python` and every applet process is on the new release's interpreter. |
| `jobs` | Every job that was open before is back as running or queued, or has ended with a result of status `ok`. |
| `supervisor` | The root supervisor is not held. |
| `subfleets` | Every named sub-fleet that ran before runs again. |
| `applets` | Every applet that ran before runs again. |
| `window` | The `fleetmux gui` window that was on screen before is on screen again (macOS window list). |

A check fails only when it held before the switch and does not hold after it. What did not hold before is reported
as `n/a`, never as a failure: a supervisor that was already held, or no window before the switch.

The interpreter of a process is its argv[0], with one exception on macOS: a venv made from a framework Python
(Homebrew, python.org) re-executes the framework's `.../Python.app/Contents/MacOS/Python`, so `ps` shows that path.
For such a process the check takes the venv's python from `__PYVENV_LAUNCHER__` in the process's start environment
(`ps -wwE`, `releases.launcher`, `release_drift.process_python`). Any other argv[0] is taken as it is; on Linux a venv
python keeps its own path as argv[0] and no environment is read.

### 71.4 Output, failure and recovery

The last line of stdout is one JSON object: `result`, `step`, `reason`, `from`, `to`, `backup`, `recover`,
`rollback_step`, and, when the switch ran, `rolled_back` and `checks`
(`{"<check>": {"status": "ok"|"fail"|"n/a", "detail": ...}}`).

| Exit | Step | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | `""` | The tree runs `--to` and every check is `ok` or `n/a`; also when the tree already ran `--to`. |
| 1 | `verify` | A check failed after the new release started. |
| 2 | `preflight`, `backup` | Refused before anything was stopped. |
| 3 | a stop step | A stop step failed. |
| 4 | `start_new` | The new release did not start. |

A failure after the root stopped goes back the same way to the release that ran before: the same stop steps, the
configs switched back, the old release started and checked. `rolled_back` is `true` when the tree is back (or
nothing had been stopped) and `false` when the way back failed too. The way back is judged by the same checks
against the same before-state, so a way back whose old release started but whose own check fails also gives
`false`; `reason` then names that check after "going back to release <from> failed too". `rollback_step` tells the
two `false` cases apart: `"verify"` when the release before is up again and only a check of the way back failed,
another step name (`start_new`, a stop step) when the way back stopped there and the tree may be down or mixed.
It is `""` when the way back worked and `null` when there was no way back (a pass, a refusal, or a failure before
the root stopped). `upgrade status` judges by `result` and `step` only. `recover` lists shell commands for the person
who reads the report and is never run by the command: run the same command again, go back with
`upgrade --to <from>`, start `from` directly when the way back left the tree down, resubmit a lost job from the
backup. The same JSON is kept as `<fleet home>/upgrade/last.json`.

A failed switch, manual or automatic, counts towards `max_failures` of its `--to` release (section 56 "Release
drift"), except a refusal at `preflight` or `backup` that stopped nothing (exit 2, `rolled_back` true): the run went
on as it was and the release was never tried. The report of such a refusal says it was not counted.

### 71.5 One switch at a time, and a switch process that died

- The manual command holds `<fleet home>/upgrade/.lock`, and every switch of a run (manual or automatic) holds
  `<run>/release-drift.lock` (flock). When either is held, the command exits 2 and changes nothing.
- A switch process can die between the stop and the start: the root is down, `<run>/release-drift-plan.json` exists
  and `<run>/release-drift-outcome.json` does not. The same command then continues from `start_new` with the plan's
  own snapshot. A different `--to` is refused, and the reason names the `--to` of the switch that died.

### 71.6 `upgrade status`, `upgrade running` and `--all-running`

- `upgrade status --to <t>` is the health command after a switch. Per root tree it judges the newer of
  `<fleet home>/upgrade/last.json` (the last manual upgrade) and `<run>/release-drift-outcome.json` (the last switch
  of the run, written by the manual command and by the daemon's own release drift) that name `<t>`. A manual upgrade
  refused at `preflight` changed nothing; it counts only when no switch to `<t>` is on record. Result: `pass` (exit 0)
  when that switch passed and the root and every running sub-fleet run `<t>`; `fail` (exit 1) when it failed, the
  root daemon is down or a daemon runs another release; `defer` (exit 2, with `next_check_at`) while a switch is in
  progress or no finished switch names `<t>` yet. With `--all-running` it covers the trees running now and the ones
  the last `--all-running` moved.
- `upgrade running` lists, read-only, the release directories the fleet daemons of this machine run now (each root
  fleet and its running sub-fleets, each directory once). With no daemon it lists the installed release
  (`~/fleetmux-current`) with `"running": false`; with neither, an empty list. A daemon whose release is unknown (no
  `<run>/release` record and no release in its `python` path, for example a daemon started from a checkout) or whose
  release directory is gone fails with exit 1 and step `running`.
- `--all-running` and `upgrade running` find the root fleets in `ps`: every `fleet.py daemon <config>` line whose
  config has no `parent_config`, whatever its fleet home. A test world or a scenario tree that runs on the machine at
  the same moment is listed too.

### 71.7 The upgrade scenario: `fleetmux scenario upgrade`

`fleetmux scenario upgrade --from <release> --to <release> [--repo <checkout>] [--timeout <s>] [--keep]` is a
subcommand of the Rust program (`rust/crates/app/src/scenario/`). It runs a real switch end to end: the real binary,
real Python daemons, the real terminal server, a private tmux and a private Redis; only the model is replaced by a
scripted agent (`fleetmux/scenario_actor.py` plugged into the demo actor).

- **Releases.** `--from` and `--to` are release directories (used read-only) or git references resolved in `--repo`.
  A reference is installed with that commit's `scripts/install_release.sh --bin-dir --headless` inside the world
  (never `--activate`), under `<world>/fleetmux-releases/<sha>`, with the binary built from that commit's own
  `rust/` tree (`cargo build --release --locked -p fleetmux`; build directory `$FLEETMUX_SCENARIO_CARGO_TARGET`, else
  a per-user cache in the system temp directory). A release directory that is given is never written; `~` and
  links in it (`versions/<v>`) are resolved first.
- **The same release.** When `--from` and `--to` resolve to one release directory (a reinstall of the running
  release, or a retry after a switch left a tree on the new one), there is nothing to switch: the run passes right
  after `prepare` with the reason `from and to are the same release: nothing to switch`, starts no tree and goes
  straight to `cleanup`.
- **Isolated world.** A new directory `/tmp/fmxu-XXXXXX` (mode 0700) holds `HOME`, `SHELL=/bin/sh`, `TMUX_TMPDIR`,
  `FLEETMUX_HOME`, `FLEETMUX_STATE`, `FLEETMUX_RELEASES`, the Redis root and the runs; tmux runs as
  `tmux -f /dev/null`. The caller's `FLEETMUX_*`, `TMUX*` and `CLAUDE*` variables are removed.
- **The tree.** A root service fleet on `--from` with two workers (`worker-1` for the jobs, `ops-1` as the
  sub-fleet's supervisor), a named autostart sub-fleet `ops`, an applet, and two jobs: one running (its scripted
  worker commits one step per turn) and one queued.
- **The switch.** The `--to` release's own `fleetmux upgrade --to <to> --config <root config>`, as on a real machine.
- **Checks from outside**, after the switch: the daemons and their interpreters are on `--to`; every job open
  before is back with the same id (running stays running, queued is queued or has started) and none published a
  result other than `ok`; the root supervisor is not held (unless it was before); the sub-fleet and the applet run
  again; the running job commits new steps on `--to` without repeating a step it had committed. Then the scenario
  releases the running job, which must end with a published result `ok` (a job that ends blocked did not go on),
  and the queued job must start and commit. Sub-fleet supervisors reported held after the switch began are recorded
  (`supervisor_holds`), never a failure. The steps run in the fixed order of `steps.rs` and stop at the first
  failure. A failed `upgrade` lets the checks after it run, as evidence,
  only when its result has no `rolled_back` (the upgrade's own check failed and the new release still runs); after
  a way back to `--from`, successful or not, nothing on `--to` is left to check. Either way the run fails. The
  report keeps the tree's `fleetmux status` after each check.
- **The window** is `n/a` in the scenario: the tree runs with `--no-view` in an isolated world, so there is no
  desktop window to check. `fleetmux upgrade` checks the window on a real machine (71.3).
- **Clean-up.** The `cleanup` step runs on every path: a normal end, a failed step, a panic, `--timeout` and
  SIGINT / SIGTERM / SIGHUP. It stops only what the run itself started: the world's Redis, tmux and terminal
  servers, the process groups the run spawned (guarded against pid reuse by the leader's start time) and any process
  of the user whose command line names the world directory. Nothing is found by program name. The world is removed
  unless `--keep` is given.
- **Output.** One line per step, `scenario: <step>: ok|FAIL|n/a <detail>`, then one JSON line
  `{"result", "step", "reason", "from", "to", "duration_s", "report"}`. Exit 0 pass, 1 a step or check failed
  (timeout included), 2 a usage error or an incomplete environment (a missing program is never a skip), 130
  interrupted. `--timeout` defaults to 3600 seconds.

Two uses: during development `--from main --to HEAD` (the delivery gate runs it once), and during a rollout `--from`
each release the target machine runs (71.8).

### 71.8 The rollout of a release

A rollout target that installs fleetmux releases runs, for each approved release (details: the rollout repository's
`docs/contracts.md`, release install targets):

1. `install`: the new release directory is installed; `~/fleetmux-current` is not moved.
2. `running`: the new release's `fleetmux upgrade running` lists the releases the machine runs.
3. `check`: the new release's `fleetmux scenario upgrade --from <each listed release> --to <new release>`, once per
   listed release. Any failure stops the request before the switch, and the scenario's failing step and reason are
   carried into the request's failure. With an empty list (nothing runs and nothing is installed) no scenario runs.
4. `activate`: `~/fleetmux-current` moves to the new release, only after every scenario passed.
5. `restart`: `fleetmux upgrade --to version:<v> --all-running` from the new release, inside the target's usual
   time window.
6. `health`: `fleetmux upgrade status --to version:<v> --all-running` until `pass` or `fail`.

A failed `running` or `check` can be retried; the retry reads the running releases again and runs the scenarios
again before it switches.

### 71.9 A skipped test is a failed test

- The fleetmux and rollout test suites load a skip policy (`tests/skip_policy.py` in each repository) that holds for
  every run, a whole suite or a single file. Every skip is collected; a skip without an entry in
  `tests/skip_allowlist.yaml` (`file`, a substring of the `reason`, and `why`) fails the run.
- A missing program, package or build output (redis-server, tmux, node, uv, the real fleetmux binary ...) is never an
  allowed reason: the dependency is installed instead. There is no switch that lets such a run pass.
- The last line of every run is `skip-policy: total=<n> allowlisted=<n> not_allowed=<n> new_vs_main=<n>`; the last
  number counts the skip sites that main's baseline (`tests/skip_baseline.yaml`) does not have.
- For this, the GitHub tests workflow (`.github/workflows/tests.yml`, both jobs) installs what the suite would
  otherwise skip without: the Pi package at the version `deps.yaml` pins (`agent_installs.pi`, installed globally
  with npm after `setup-node`, for the Pi and TS contract tests), uv (`astral-sh/setup-uv`, for `tests/test_install.py`),
  Python 3.12 next to the default Python (`setup-python` with both versions, the default one listed last, for
  `tests/test_script_stdlib_shadow.py`) and, on Ubuntu, zsh (for `tests/test_tmux_attach_quoting.py`). pytest runs
  with `-rs`, so the log names every skip and its reason.

### 71.10 Known limits

- A CLI session that was already attached before the switch keeps its hook lines until its next generation.
- `--all-running` and `upgrade running` list the root fleets that `ps` shows, including a test world or a scenario
  tree that runs at the same moment. A daemon of such a world that runs from a checkout makes `upgrade running` fail
  (the rollout then does not switch until a retry).
- The scenario checks no desktop window (71.7).
- A sub-fleet whose supervisor has a turn in flight at the stop may find that supervisor held after the switch.
  The root supervisor releases it with the program's heal request, and the job then goes on. The `supervisor`
  check looks at the root supervisor only.

rollback), `tests/test_upgrade_verify.py`, `tests/test_upgrade_interpreter.py`, `tests/test_upgrade_macos_venv.py`,
`tests/test_upgrade_stop_order.py`, `tests/test_upgrade_old_release.py`, `tests/test_upgrade_restore_file.py`,
